                    dial costs one hash pair and four lane folds per staged quadword, inside the
                    copy the staging sink was already doing.
      [GSPLATE] the stream does not plate its rect: clip=<W>x<H> b0=<x0,y0,x1,y1>
               -- one-shot, armed when a layer's plate was refused over a
               rect the first vertex group does not span
      [BENCH] canvas census pass <n>: rows=<n> changed=<n> span=<lo>..<hi>
               px<=<n> of <n> fp=<hex> cost=<n> us
               -- row 9d's premise row, and the row that decides whether a
               present is work or redundancy.  `changed=` counts ROWS whose FNV
               hash differs from the same row in the PREVIOUS pass, so `px<=` is
               an UPPER bound on the damage: a row changed in one pixel and a row
               changed wholly hash and count alike.  fp= is the xor of the row
               hashes, so two passes with one fingerprint are one picture no
               matter what changed= says about which rows.  cost= prices the scan
               itself -- the honest thing a census owes, because 9b's caller died
               when deciding cost 1.53x the column it was meant to reduce, and
               this row prints its own price so that reading stays open here.
      [ASYNC] input plane Timer0 (INTC 9): period=<us> compare=<ticks> ticks=<n> of
               the <n> this <us> us window owes -> [the INTC registry REJECTED the
               handler: ] LIVE | STALLED | DEAD
      [ASYNC] isr wcet=<ticks> ticks = <us>.<cs> us of the <budget> us budget,
               overruns=<n>  workers: input <us> us, ui <us> us against a <us>
                             us
               deadline -- [PSTAT] budget
      [PSTAT] plane: reports=<n> on the input plane, <n> in a pass, dropped=<n>
               queue max=<n> of <n>, collected on <n> periods
      [PSTAT] plane: held max=<us> us mean=<us> us over <n> delivered -- isr <us>.<cs>
               us of the <budget> us budget, overruns=<n>
      [PSTAT] budget input: runs=<n> max=<us> us against the <budget> us budget,
               misses=<n>
      [PSTAT] budget ui: passes=<n> max=<us> us against the <budget> us budget,
               misses=<n> | deadline=<us> us, late=<n>
               -- the two budget rows are the ONLY place a worker plane's WCET is
               read, and they print in the same shape from a session's exit and from
               the bench's closing receipt, so a headless gate and a machine with a
               person on it quote one row.
      [PSTAT] stroke pair: samples=<n> slow=<n> counts in <n> ms (<n>.<cs>/ms), fast=<n>
               in <n> ms (<n>.<cs>/ms) -> <n>.<cs>x | no ratio yet
               -- the two-speed slide pair that decides 13.4's macOS row, kept by
               ps2_ptr_stroke.h and printed wherever this port prints pointer
               statistics, the bench's closing receipt included (13.1).
               `no ratio yet` is one sample, not a measurement of zero; the verdict rows
               under it name the experiment to do instead, and `samples=` is the number
               of slides that were long enough to count.
      [PSTAT]   the same slide carried the same counts at both speeds | the
               fast slide carried more counts | carried FEWER counts | both at
               nearly one speed | no pair yet | the mouse handler has seen NO
               report since boot -- the six verdicts, in the order a reader
               meets them: distance on the wire, an accelerator above this
               port, something that divides by speed, a run that was never the
               experiment, a session that has not done it yet, and a wire that
               delivered nothing.  The last two refuse in OPPOSITE directions,
               and the first live session needed the last one: a row that says
               `slide further` to a bus which sent zero reports is advice
               aimed at the wrong party.  So the splitter counts every report
               it is fed, moving or not, and keeps the longest stroke it saw;
               an unfinished session prints both numbers, and a silent one
               says so instead.
      [BENCH] <tag> <w>x<h> <n>px/rep <n> us/rep max <n> us  <ns>.<cs> ns/px
               sum=0x<hex> chk=ok | MISMATCH | fill
               -- chk= is this instrument's own integrity check, not a property of the
               pixel: every row that moves bytes folds its destination AND its source
               band, and a copy that prints MISMATCH means a tag and a branch disagree
               (10.1 tells that story, and it is why the row is printed at all).  A
               fill prints `fill` because it has no source to fold.
      [BENCH] RAM-to-RAM, the loop or the DRAM -- same bytes at two instruction costs
               -- the phase's NAME stays a question because that is what a reader
               greps for; its ANSWER is 10.1.  It prints before the region table for
               the reason any deciding row does: a row that chooses a lane has to be
               in the log even when the run is cut off downstream.
      [BENCH]   -- size sweep, even phase, load+store and store-only
      [BENCH] ram: rect <w>x<h> does not fit the canvas, skipped
               -- a copy needs a source band as large as its destination and the two
               bands may not overlap, so 300 rows x 800 px = 240,000 px is as far as
               a copy can be taken on this canvas at all.  The refusal prints rather
               than the row going missing quietly, which is how a sweep looks flat
               because it stopped early.
      The tags, so a row's SHAPE is readable from its name alone:
        copy / fill / blit   which leaf is priced: btron_row_blit(),
                             fill_rec(), or the scan-then-copy walk
                             src/window/wnd.c takes over a window's client
                             area
        even / odd           the destination's PIXEL column.  On this canvas
                             an even column is 8-aligned in bytes and an odd
                             one is 4-mod-8, so one character carries the
                             entire alignment defect
        +peel / +16 / +32    the loop body: peel to the shared phase, then
                             take 8, 16 or 32 bytes per iteration.  +peel is
                             the rung BELOW what shipped, kept printed so that
                             a landing's price stays auditable against the
                             rows that bought it
        +16b / +32b          the same two widths measured AGAIN after the
                             other one has run.  The gap being read is ~5% and
                             the drift between adjacent rows is ~1%, so a
                             width that wins only in the first slot is a
                             position effect, not a result
        fillunr / fillunr8   fill_rec()'s leaf with its row pointer
                             incremented instead of recomputed, four stores
                             deep then eight
        blit scan / wide     the opacity decision one pixel at a time, then
                             two pixels per 64-bit load, with the copy still a
                             second read of the same bytes.  This is
                             btron_row_copy_twopass(), the row every port but
                             this one runs.  The copy beneath both is the
                             shipped header, so the pair's difference is the
                             scan and nothing else.
        blit f2 / f4 / f8    the FUSED pass: one load per pixel, spent on the
                             decision AND on the store, at 2, 4 and 8 pixels
                             per decision.  `f8b` is the same group decided by
                             one arithmetic bit test instead of compares;
                             `f4w` / `f8w` / `f16w` are the same shapes in
                             32-bit words, which is the form that promises no
                             alignment; `ship` is btron_row_copy_skip_zero(),
                             the function the walk calls, priced beside its
                             own lookalike.
        h / o prefixes       the same rect under the other two shapes of the
                             problem: `o` starts the row at an odd pixel
                             column, and `h` puts the transparent sentinel in
                             one pixel of eight in the source band.  A fused
                             rung that wins the clean rows and loses the `h`
                             rows is not taken.
        <w>x<h>, px/rep      the rect and the pixels one repetition moves,
                             with the mean us/rep and the max beside it: a max
                             well above the mean says the first repetition was
                             cold, not that the loop is slow.  The sweep's
                             largest rects pay fewest reps, because a 480,000
                             px row at 256 reps is a run, not a measurement.

9.2  Who owns which cycle column

    Columns              Owner            What it decides
    ------------------------------------------------------------------
    poll hook evt        this port        the pass' own bookkeeping: HID polls, the
    net note                            bench frame hook, event dispatch, the
                                        two timer nets, [PSTAT] accounting
    render swap upload   this port        the three stages of one present:
                                        composite, byte order (structurally 0,
                                        see 7a), GIF DMA
    px nb full           this port        the area, the band count, and
                                        whether band 0 is the canvas
    why                  this port        WHICH RULE made it the canvas: 1 a
                                        non-move event, 2 the convergence
                                        timer, 4/8/16/32 global menu / tracker
                                        / window manager / IME, 64 band-set
                                        overflow, 128 damage that already
                                        spanned it
    dmg                  shared           the band set as the app and the pointer left
                         compositor       it, before any rule above replaced it
    comp ...             shared            that composite's own split, from
                         compositor        g_render_stats: decorations, the apps'
                                        paint callbacks, the client-area blit, panel,
                                        bars

  The cycle row is NOT behind BTRON_PS2_BENCH: an unarmed profiler costs one
  branch per boundary, and gating it would make the row unavailable to whoever
  has the real machine.  Arming is `left`, which the app phase sets, so a
  desktop session pays for it and does not print it.

10. What a pixel costs
----------------------

  LAW 10 (price per unit of work, not per stage).  Every raster cost claim
  shall be attributable to ONE billable unit -- a pixel, a span step, a
  triangle setup, a vertex, a call -- and the unit's price shall be
  reproducible from rows in one log.

  WHY  A stage table says where time went; it cannot say WHAT was billed, and
       a plan built on stages spends effort on the wrong term.  The prices
       below came from fitting rows of the same image against each other,
       which is the only method that separates a per-pixel cost from a
       per-triangle one.  Both mesh rows in the fit come from ONE binary: a
       pair of rows from two different images prices whatever changed between
       them, not the mesh.

  MEASURE  reproduce with `SMOOTH_APP=1 SMOOTH_FRAMES=3 SMOOTH_IDLE=20
           scripts/ps2_smooth.sh` and read the [XMBT] raster rows of
           build/ps2-smooth.log; the pixels themselves are comparable frame by
           frame with `make test-xmb-render-tgl`, which writes PPMs to
           .build/tgl/.

  The stage rows these prices were fitted from (2026-10-11, PS2_GL=tinygl,
  800x480 canvas, XB_RIBBON_ROWS/COLS 32, PS2_FP_COUNT=0, steady frame
  draw=173750 us):

    stage      tris    verts     px        spans   px/sp fill us  geom us
    ---------------------------------------------------------------------
    f_bg          5        4    382,731      2,495   153   29,311     132
    f_ribbon   1,828   1,984     102,320     16,289     6   49,778  21,763
    f_menu      646   1,436       46,441      8,964     5   38,769  11,905
    f_clear       --     --      384,000        --    --        --   3,398
    f_surface     --     --           --        --    --        --  18,479

  The last two are stage timings rather than raster windows: neither opens a
  fill dispatch, so their microseconds belong, by this book's own column
  definition, entirely outside the fill.

  The same stages under the backend that SHIPS, PS2_GL=eegs, read off one
  steady frame (2026-10-14, the app phase's frame 2 of a `BENCH_APP=1` bench
  built on PS2_FP=hw PS2_MSX=0; [XMBT] frame 2 (eegs) draw=46864 us swap=0):

    stage      tris    verts     px        spans   px/sp fill us  geom us
    ---------------------------------------------------------------------
    f_bg          5        4    382,731     2,495   153       0      159
    f_ribbon   1,839   1,984     101,858    16,391     6       0   25,142
    f_menu      646   1,436      46,694      8,964     5       0   12,318
    f_clear       --     --      384,000        --    --      --    3,408
    f_surface     --     --           --        --    --      --    5,746

  Three readings come out of this table, and none of them transfers to the one
  above it.

  One.  `fill us = 0` is STRUCTURAL, not an instrument that failed to time.
  The GS arm of gl_draw_triangle_general (src/gl/tinygl/clip.c) returns before
  the fill's clock bracket is opened, so that column has nothing to
  accumulate.  The px and spans columns still fill because tgl_count_fill()
  runs AHEAD of that branch and bills the triangle's analytic area -- the
  counters describe what the rasteriser was asked to draw, and that question
  is the same whichever engine answers it.  So every per-pixel price in the
  list below is a TINYGL price: under the shipped backend the EE writes no
  pixels at all, and the billable unit this frame offers is the VERTEX.

  Two.  The vertex price survived the swap untouched.  159 + 25,142 + 12,318 =
  37,619 us over 4 + 1,984 + 1,436 = 3,424 mesh vertices is 11.0 us/vertex,
  and the row fitted from tinygl on 2026-10-11 reads 11.0 = 3,240 cycles.  The
  pair of sub-prices repeats as well -- the ribbon's 12.7 against the menu's
  8.6, where that fit said 11.0 against 8.3.  A price identical across two
  backends is a property of the transform and the clipping, and there is no
  fill work left on the EE to charge it to.

  Three.  What the shipped backend charges for the SAME triangles, from the
  present's own bracket: [GSTS] groups=51 qw=29997 verts=7470 prims=2490
  gs=3879 us, clip=768x500 b0=0,0,767,499 plate=covered.  prims= is these
  three stages' tris summed to the unit (5 + 1,839 + 646 = 2,490), which is
  what makes the pair comparable without a guess: handing 2,490 triangles to
  the GS costs the EE 1.56 us apiece against the 11.0 us it spent to make each
  one's three vertices -- the front end is 21x the sender.  gs= is the EE's
  POSTING time and not its recording time -- the correction matters, because the
  two were once written as one term here: the bracket gs= measures opens after
  the composite in ps2_paint_region() (src/cores/core_ps2.c) and contains
  eegs_present_send() and the cover re-uploads, while the packet's per-vertex
  work -- gs_rec_vertex() and everything it calls -- runs inside xb_paint() and
  is therefore already inside paint= and inside the 11.0 us.  So this 1.56 us is
  the price of pushing 29,997 quadwords -- some 469 KB -- at the GIF, and not the
  price of building them.  The ring's own row says that push did not wait
  (posted=52 started=52 queued=0 inflight=0
  drop=0), so this 21x is not a queue effect: it is the whole distance between
  what the machine does per vertex and what it has to do per vertex to get a
  picture.  That distance is LANE 12's, and it is 9d's context.

  The same frame's pass partitioned, from its own cycle row ([XMBT] cycle 1
  period=106544 pass=90534 render=85764 swap=0 upload=4757 px=480000 nb=1
  full=1 why=c comp ... total=76754):

    the app's paint (comp paint=)          46,874  of which geometry 37,619
    the composite's canvas work            27,989  bg 7,484 + frame 7,851
                                                     + blit 12,654
    panel + bars                            1,878
    the present's bookkeeping               9,010  render= less comp total=
    the GIF transfer of the canvas          4,757  upload=
    ------------------------------------------------------------------------
                                          90,508  against pass= 90,534

  The 26 us residue is the columns' own rounding, one part in 3,400, so the
  ledger is the pass and not a sample of it.  Read against the canvas census
  row -- three consecutive passes, rows=600 changed=0, one fingerprint -- this
  partition is what reopens lane 9 on its canvas half: 27,989 us rebuilding a
  canvas, 4,757 us re-sending it, and the 3,408 f_clear spends blanking a
  surface 11.5's refusal then declines to copy -- 36,154 us, 40% of the pass,
  for bytes a census says never moved.  That is row 9d, and the reading of it
  belongs to LANE 9's own cell.

  The price list, in cycles per unit on this machine (295 of them per
  microsecond):

    What is billed                             Price   Which rows say so
    ---------------------------------------------------------------------
    an opaque smooth-shaded pixel                22     f_bg: 382,731 px in 29.3 ms
    a blended pixel, mode RECOGNISED             77     the four-row fit on f_ribbon
    a blended pixel, mode NOT recognised        110     the same fit on the binary
                                                        before TGL_BLEND_VARS knew
                                                        GL_DST_COLOR/GL_ONE.  The
                                                        difference, 33 cycles, needs
                                                        no fit: it is the 11,550 us
                                                        the two 32x32 rows differ by
                                                        over 102,320 px.  This is the
                                                        per-pixel switch dispatch
    a blended TEXTURED pixel                    190     f_menu: 38.8 ms - 646 setups
                                                        - 8,964 spans, over 46,441 px
    a triangle's gradient setup          10.9 us=3,206  the fit; 1,828 of them are
                                                        19.9 ms of f_ribbon's fill --
                                                        the third-largest line in the
                                                        frame, not a rounding error
    a scanline step                            <= 60    f_bg's 2,495 spans leave under
                                                        2% of its row unexplained
    a vertex (transform, viewport, clip)  11.0=3,240    f_ribbon's geom over 1,984
    a vertex needing no clipping         8.3=2,440      f_menu's geom over 1,436
    a vertex, under the GS backend       11.0=3,240     9's eegs companion: 37,619 us over
                                                        3,424 verts.  The SAME price from
                                                        a backend that bills no pixel at
                                                        all, which is what makes it the
                                                        transform's and not the fill's,
                                                        and what makes LANE 12 the frame
    a triangle's stream POSTED (not built) 1.56=460   gs=3,879 us over prims=2,490: the
                                                        sender's price alone; the packet
                                                        that reaches it is built inside
                                                        the 11.0 us above, per vertex
    a cleared pixel                             2.6      f_clear: 128,000 px/ms
    a pixel copied RAM to RAM                   4.6      REFUTED AS AN INFERENCE, and now
                                                        MEASURED twice.  The row used to
                                                        read 14.8 on the argument that a
                                                        price flat from 768 px to
                                                        613,440 px is the DRAM's, not the
                                                        loop's.  10.1 built the control
                                                        that argument needed: the same
                                                        bytes at four instruction costs
                                                        come back 96.4 / 21.2 / 16.9 /
                                                        16.1 ns/px, so a flat price
                                                        cannot tell a bandwidth from a
                                                        tail.  The 32-byte form shipped,
                                                        and the re-run of the landed
                                                        header prices the floor at
                                                        15.65 / 15.68 / 15.69 for 12,800,
                                                        80,000 and 240,000 px -- 4.6
                                                        cycles, size-flat because the
                                                        loop is at its floor, not
                                                        because the bus is the limit.
    a pixel filled, store only                  2.6      the control's other half, at the
                                                        same sizes: 8.76 / 8.80 / 8.79
                                                        flat from 12,800 px to the whole
                                                        480,000 px canvas, and 9.49 on
                                                        the 400x16 strip where the row
                                                        pointer is recompute-bound.  It
                                                        agrees with f_clear's 128,000
                                                        px/ms to 0.02 cycles -- two
                                                        independent instruments pricing
                                                        one store.
    a pixel the compositor blits             11.8        39.86 ns/px, measured rather
                                                        than fitted, and 10.1 separates
                                                        its two terms with rows: `blit
                                                        scan` 39.86 against `copy even`
                                                        15.96 leaves 23.9 ns/px -- 7.1
                                                        cycles -- for the opacity test
                                                        wnd.c runs before it copies, and
                                                        `blit wide` at 36.57 says 3.3 of
                                                        THAT is loop as well.  The old
                                                        row billed the pair as one
                                                        "copied pixel" at 14.8 cycles,
                                                        which is what made the ratio in
                                                        1.5 read 29x.  Production's own
                                                        column moved from 30,755 us to
                                                        24,426 us over the same 613,440
                                                        px, with no plane claimed.
    a swap                                       0       it was 10.5 ms until the byte
                                                        began travelling with the
                                                        pixel (7b)

  Two terms are NOT per-pixel, and both were assumed to be software floating
  point.  They are not entirely: the calls are not the whole of the 3,206 and
  3,240 figures.  Counted per stage on the same source, a steady frame asked
  for 578,963 of them, and at the census prices (28 / 47 / 23 / 536) that is
  58.8 ms against a 173.8 ms frame.  What is left of both windows is integer:
  the edge walk, the fixed-point span interpolation, the blend, and the
  load/store traffic of the vertex record.  The ledger:

    stage              ms     float calls    ms float   ms integer
    --------------------------------------------------------------
    f_surface        18.5        159,528        15.9          2.6
    f_clear           3.4             134         0.0          3.4
    f_bg             29.4          1,302         0.1         29.3
    f_ribbon         71.5        269,596        27.5         44.0
    f_particles       0.1             294         0.0          0.1
    f_menu           50.7        148,109        15.3         35.4
    draw            173.8        578,963        58.8        115.0
    swap              0               --           --           --

  f_surface is 86% floating point -- arithmetic that happens to be in float
  rather than rastering -- and it is the only stage where making the
  arithmetic cheaper is close to making the stage cheaper.  Of the three
  raster stages, f_ribbon is 38% float and f_menu 30%; f_bg is 0.3%, i.e. a
  pure pixel cost.

  CONSEQUENCES, all four normative for where work goes:

  1. THE FLOAT LAYER'S CEILING WAS 1.51x, AND THE GS RETIRED IT.  Deleting
     every one of the 578,963 calls -- an infinite-speed FPU -- took the draw
     from 173.8 ms to 115.0 ms, and the frame a person needs is 16.7 ms.  With
     the fill on the GS the app frame is 46.3 ms and its float layer is
     460,861 single-precision calls and ZERO doubles, so the layer that was
     34% of a CPU-raster frame is essentially all of this one.  The ceiling is
     therefore no longer a share to be argued but a subtraction: keep the
     instruction, drop the call.  Rows 7 and 8 (16.4) are that subtraction,
     and the 28-cycle price of 6.5 -- 15 cycles of which are the jal and its
     prologue -- is what makes it worth doing.
  2. THE STATIC BACKGROUND IS THE LARGEST CPU-SIDE PRIZE, AND ITS PRICE IS A
     MEMORY NUMBER, NOT A PIXEL ONE.  The copied-pixel row prices a cached
     blit of the baked gradient at 4.6 cycles and f_bg paints it at 22, so the
     cache saves 17.4 cycles a pixel and costs 1.5 MB -- 7.4 was what the old
     row said it saved, and it was wrong because it billed the compositor's
     opacity scan as part of the copy (10.1).  The refutation therefore OPENS
     this lane rather than closing it: 382,731 px at 22 cycles is 28.5 ms and
     at 4.6 is 6.0 ms, so a copy of the baked gradient is 4.8x cheaper than
     re-rastering it and the representation was never the thing that made
     caching uninteresting.  What makes it uninteresting is the IMAGE:
     1,536,000 B for the XMB's own 768x500 client against the ~1.5 MiB the
     image sits under ps2.ld's ASSERT, so this is a budget question with a
     number on both sides, not a pixel question.  Until someone pays that,
     only the AREA is left: everything that moves in a steady XMB frame is
     inside one full-width horizontal band, measured as 28.7% of the canvas.
     Three facts about the mechanism: tinygl has no glScissor and glViewport
     is the only rect that clips a triangle; glClear is whole-plane and the
     only fill cheap enough to replace it is an opaque quad at 2.6 cycles; and
     the surface DOES persist across frames, so not painting outside the band
     leaves the right pixels there rather than garbage.
  3. THE SPAN LOOP IS NOT A LEVER and the mesh dial is not one either.  f_bg's
     row proves a scanline step is noise, so "short spans are what a fine mesh
     pays for" is false.  And the dial has been priced and lost: 32x32 ->
     16x16 is worth 47.0 ms and costs 10.81% of the sheet's pixels with a
     worst channel delta of 145/255 -- three orders of magnitude more than the
     affine trade below -- so it does not ship, and
     verify/tests/test_xmb_render.c refuses n < 32*32 as a law.
  4. A THIRD OF THE FILL IS PER-TRIANGLE AND LIBRARY-INTERNAL: ~27 ms of a
     173.8 ms frame in ztriangle.h's setup block rather than its span loop,
     two thirds of it integer.  This is bit-risky ground -- its answers come
     from (GLint)(fdy2*d1 - fdy1*d2) after fz has been DIVIDED through, so an
     integer rewrite would round in a different place -- and is the line to
     attack only with the twelve-PPM byte-identity gate in hand.

  Four cuts landed on this layer, each with its pixel price stated rather than
  implied:

  - Affine instead of perspective for a flat z (78.3 -> 41.7 ms of f_menu
    fill): 0.02% of the frame's pixels change, each a one-texel column on one
    vertical edge of a glyph; worst channel delta 102/255.  The px/span column
    is the row that says which other layers benefit the same way.
  - The (GLint) rewrite: float -> int has NO EE instruction (no trunc.w.s), so
    it ran the bit engine through a format descriptor and 32-bit halves.
    ps2_sf_fixsfsi() now reads the exponent band and emits 32 straight-line
    integer instructions with no call: 47 cycles instead of 536.  A conversion
    whose result cannot differ cannot change a pixel, so this saving has no
    visual price to watch.
  - One double that had no business being there: gl_clipcode()'s guard band
    was written `w1 * (1.0 + CLIP_EPSILON)` -- a double literal, so the
    GLfloat was promoted, the multiply ran on the bit engine, and the result
    was truncated back: three double calls and one float multiply per vertex,
    for a band whose whole purpose is to be off by one part in 100,000.
    Writing it `(GLfloat)(1.0 + CLIP_EPSILON)` took 10,713 double calls per
    frame to zero.  On a target where GLfloat IS double -- the hosted and Pi
    400 builds -- the cast is a no-op and the expression is bit-for-bit what
    it was.
  - The second blend recognition (TGL_BLEND_BRIGHTEN for GL_DST_COLOR/GL_ONE):
    f_ribbon's fill 61,328 -> 49,778 us and the steady frame 185,559 ->
    173,750 us, while f_bg, f_clear, f_surface and the ribbon's own geom
    column did not move at all -- 34 cycles per blended pixel, which is
    exactly the difference between the two prices above, and the second
    measured proof that in this rasteriser the DISPATCH, not the arithmetic,
    is what a blended fragment pays for.  Nothing about the picture changed,
    and that was shown three ways: a sweep over every sum the expression can
    produce (33,488,895 values, 0 mismatches), every (channel, multiplier)
    pair (1,051,392, 0 mismatches), and the twelve PPMs byte-identical with
    the recognition on and off -- shown to DISCRIMINATE by adding 1/255 of red
    inside the fast arm, which moves exactly the three frames the ribbon
    appears in and none of the other nine.

10.1  RAM to RAM: the control that refuted the DRAM
----------------------------------------------------

  LAW 10.1 (a pixel price is a claim about a loop until a control says
  otherwise).  No cost in this chapter may be attributed to the memory system
  unless a control has moved the SAME bytes at a DIFFERENT instruction count
  and come back at the same price.  A price that is flat across sizes is not
  that control, because a per-iteration tail is flat across sizes too.

  WHY  Row 9's open half is a choice between two lanes -- shave the EE's own
       pixel loops, or hand the pixels to the GS -- and the book priced it as
       though the first lane were already shut.  The evidence was the
       compositor's own `blit=` column: 50.2 ns/px at 768 px and 50.2 ns/px at
       613,440 px, three orders of magnitude of size at one price, read as the
       DRAM's signature rather than the loop's.  The reading was not
       falsifiable, and that is the whole objection to it -- nothing in this
       tree had ever moved one byte range at two instruction counts, so no row
       could have contradicted it.  `btron_row_blit()` made the difference by
       accident: it took its 8-byte loop only when BOTH pointers were
       8-aligned, and a damage rect starts at an arbitrary pixel column, so an
       odd `left` left every row of that rectangle 4-mod-8 and the whole of it
       went through the byte tail.  The aarch64 half of that header has always
       peeled to the shared phase, and its own comment says a present became
       one bus transaction per byte without the peel; the generic half, which
       is the half this port compiles, had not been asked the question.

  MEASURE  `scripts/ps2_bench.sh` with no app dial: ps2_bench_ram_ops() is the
           first pixel phase of the bench, so its rows are in
           build/ps2-bench.log even when everything downstream of it is cut
           off.  Verbatim from the run that carries both landings, 2026-10-13,
           PS2_GL=eegs PS2_FP=hw:

    [BENCH] copy even      400x  16   6400px/rep   102 us/rep   15.96 ns/px  chk=ok
    [BENCH] copy odd       399x  16   6384px/rep   103 us/rep   16.17 ns/px  chk=ok
    [BENCH] copy odd+peel  399x  16   6384px/rep   135 us/rep   21.23 ns/px  chk=ok
    [BENCH] copy even+16   400x  16  ...           107 us/rep   16.74 ns/px  chk=ok
    [BENCH] copy even+32   400x  16  ...           101 us/rep   15.79 ns/px  chk=ok
    [BENCH] copy odd+16 / +32 / +16b / +32b        16.93 / 16.14 / 16.91 / 16.12
    [BENCH] fill even      400x  16  ...            60 us/rep    9.49 ns/px  chk=fill
    [BENCH] fill odd       399x  16  ...            61 us/rep    9.65 ns/px  chk=fill
    [BENCH] fillunr even / odd                     10.40 / 10.43
    [BENCH] fillunr8 even / odd                     8.99 /  9.17
    [BENCH] blit scan      400x  16   6400px/rep   255 us/rep   39.86 ns/px  chk=ok
    [BENCH] blit wide      400x  16   6400px/rep   234 us/rep   36.57 ns/px  chk=ok
    [BENCH] blit odd scan / odd wide               40.11 / 36.87
    [BENCH]   -- size sweep, even phase, load+store and store-only
    [BENCH] copy 768px      48x  16     768px/rep    15 us/rep   19.74 ns/px
    [BENCH] copy 12800px   800x  16   12800px/rep   200 us/rep   15.65 ns/px
    [BENCH] copy 12800+32  800x  16   12800px/rep   200 us/rep   15.65 ns/px
    [BENCH] blit 12800     800x  16   12800px/rep   507 us/rep   39.68 ns/px
    [BENCH] blit 12800w    800x  16   12800px/rep   464 us/rep   36.28 ns/px
    [BENCH] copy 80000px   800x 100   80000px/rep  1254 us/rep   15.68 ns/px
    [BENCH] copy 240000px  800x 300  240000px/rep  3767 us/rep   15.69 ns/px
    [BENCH] fill 768px      48x  16     768px/rep    14 us/rep   18.41 ns/px
    [BENCH] fill 12800px   800x  16   12800px/rep   112 us/rep    8.76 ns/px
    [BENCH] fill 80000px   800x 100   80000px/rep   704 us/rep    8.80 ns/px
    [BENCH] fill 480000px  800x 600  480000px/rep  4221 us/rep   8.79 ns/px
    [BENCH] fillunr 480k   800x 600  480000px/rep  4960 us/rep  10.33 ns/px
    [BENCH] fillunr8 480k  800x 600  480000px/rep  4350 us/rep   9.06 ns/px

  The ladder is the refutation.  Its first rungs, measured the same day and
  before anything shipped, moved one band at four instruction costs and got
  96.4 ns/px through the byte tail, 21.2 peeled to the shared phase at 8
  bytes, 16.9 at 16 and 16.1 at 32 -- a 6x spread on identical bytes in
  identical memory.  A bandwidth cannot do that; a loop can, and did.  Its
  aligned twin cost 27.8 at the body width the header used to carry, and it is
  the same loop as the peeled one at 1.31x the price, because clang inlines
  the header's body into the compositor's row loop with two `addu`s per
  iteration instead of the two `addiu`s it compiles when the body stands in a
  function of its own: 11 instructions per 8 bytes against 9.  Loop SHAPE, not
  only loop alignment, is what the pixel is charged for, so a width is
  something to time rather than to reason about.  The 96.4 rung is not in the
  table above any more, because the peel shipped: getting that row back means
  un-landing the fix, and it is quoted here as the rung whose price decided
  the header.

  Read as instruction counts -- 20 for the tail, 4 at 8 bytes, 3 at 16, 2.4 at
  32 -- the first three rungs land at the 4.7 ns an instruction costs here
  (8.4) plus a fixed 2.4 ns a pixel, and the fourth sits 2.5 ns/px below what
  that model allows.  So this machine has FLOORS, and from today they are the
  assumption rather than the finding: a copy stops being charged below ~15.7
  ns/px and a fill below ~8.8.  The gap between the two floors, once both
  leaves are fixed, is one load per pixel at 6.9 ns -- 1.4 instructions'
  worth, not the 2x a serializing bus would make it.

  What the flat row had really been pricing is the compositor's SECOND pass
  over the same bytes.  `blit=` walks a row to decide whether it may be copied
  at all -- src/window/wnd.c:703-719 reads every pixel and bails at the first
  transparent one, because a transparent pixel must leave whatever is below it
  on the canvas -- and the copy then reads that band again.  Two rows separate
  the terms without arithmetic: `blit scan` 39.86 against `copy even` 15.96
  leaves 23.9 ns/px, 7.1 cycles, for the decision, and `blit wide` at 36.57,
  which makes the same decision two pixels per 64-bit load, says 3.3 ns/px of
  even THAT is loop.  Production's own column moved with the landings: the
  `[BENCH] compositor:` whole-canvas row read 30,755 us over 613,440 px on the
  build that measured this phase, and the same row of the same walk reads
  24,426 us over the same 613,440 px on the build that carries both leaves --
  50.2 ns/px to 39.8, with no plane claimed and no primitive added.  The two
  cross-checks are the shipped compositor's own rows from the same run --
  `bg=7490(480000 px)` is 15.60 ns/px, the copy floor read off a real
  whole-canvas background restore, and `blit=24426(613440 px)` is 39.82, `blit
  scan`'s 39.86 read off a real walk.

  GATE  The row carries its own integrity field, and it exists because of a
        bug in this instrument rather than in the pixel loops: the dispatcher
        in ps2_bench_ram() read `mode >= 2` where it meant `mode == 2`, so the
        16- and 32-byte copy rungs fell into the fill branch and printed
        fill_rec()'s price under a copy tag -- the same 0xf9f6d730 the fill
        rows print, three rows in a row, in every one of the first four runs.
        Nothing in the log said so; the fold disagreed and nobody was reading
        it.  Every copy row now folds its source band the same way it folds
        its destination and prints whether the two agree, so chk= is MISMATCH
        rather than a silent lie, and reintroducing `>=` is the control that
        proves this table can go red.  A width claim needs repetition rather
        than one row, because the step being read is 5% while the drift
        between adjacent rows is ~1%: the two widths run interleaved at 256
        reps with the pair repeated (+16b, +32b) after the other width has
        run, so a width that wins only in the first slot shows up as one.  32
        bytes went into include/btron/fast_blit.h on that evidence and on
        `copy 12800px` falling from 16.53 to 15.58 in the landing run and
        reading 15.65 again in the re-run above -- the same row, 0.4% apart,
        which is the drift this paragraph warns about and not a second result
        -- and it is the size `bg=` actually bills.  Eight stores went into
        dp_core.c's fill_rec() on the eight-deep rung at 8.99 / 9.17 against
        the four-deep at 10.40 / 10.43, which fill_rec() then matched at
        8.76-8.80 across the sweep.  The floors themselves are the assertion
        to re-read after any change to either leaf: a copy row that prices
        below 15.7 is a win, and one that prices above it is a regression, and
        neither is a mystery once these rows are in the log.

  CONSEQUENCE FOR LANE 9, stated as the price to beat rather than as an
  opinion.  The EE's own floors are now 15.7 ns/px for a copy and 8.8 for a
  fill, so a GS SPRITE poster for the whole 480,000 px background has to beat
  7.5 ms -- and on the primitive alone it plausibly does, since the same
  480,000 px already crosses DMAC channel 2 into VRAM in ~821 us (1.5's upload
  row, the landed pair) -- and a paged 9b has to beat the walk's 24.4 ms.  The
  refutation also buys an EE-side win that needs no plane at all: 3.3
  ns/px of the compositor's scan, which is 2.0 ms of every full-canvas walk,
  and the row that prices it (`blit wide`) is already in the table.  What
  that row did NOT take was the scan's second READ of the same bytes: the
  walk still decided and then copied, so 39.86 became 36.57 while the 15.96
  under the copy was still paid twice.  The shape that pays it once is a
  FUSED pass, and LANE 9's `Shave` cell carries it LANDED 2026-10-14 -- the
  rungs from 2 pixels per decision to 16, the port dial that leaves every
  other target on the old row, and the two content regimes that decide which
  of them is real.  Its price against this table's 39.84 is 20.95 ns/px, and
  on the shipped walk's own column `blit=12665(613440 px)` against the
  24,481 us the same pass billed two lines above.  Lane 9's
  Budget and Gate cells in 16 read these numbers, not the 50.2 the old row
  quoted.

  The price to beat turned out to be incomplete, and the missing term is what
  closed 9b.  Beating 7.5 ms with a primitive is not enough: a poster also has
  to beat the cost of DECIDING which pixels it may cover, and that term is not
  in the paragraph above because nothing had measured it.  Read 2026-10-14 it
  is 11,498 us for one compare per pixel -- against the 7,499 us the whole
  column costs -- so the decision is worth more than the work it would skip,
  at any content.  LANE 9's Price cell carries the rows; 10.1 is the floor the
  primitive had to beat, and the census is the wall the caller hit.

  One thing no measurement in this tree can settle, and it now covers this
  section's rows as much as the fitted ones above it: every number here is
  PCSX2's.  An R5900 with real RDRAM may put its floor somewhere else, and
  then the ladder's SHAPE still refutes a bandwidth argument while only its
  numbers move.  The identical image over SIO on a real console is the
  outstanding check.

11. The frame, attributed stage by stage
----------------------------------------

  LAW 11 (an app's frame is not the render its own log times).  The unit of
  presentation on this port is the PERIOD, and it is attributed by the cycle
  row, not by the app's draw column.  xb_paint() reports [XMBT] frame ...
  draw=<us> and stops at egl_swap_buffers(), while the pixels reach the GS
  only after the compositor has blitted the window's surface onto the canvas
  and the port has uploaded that rectangle.  The two halves are joined by
  [XMBT] cycle, which ps2_gui_pass() fills and ps2_task_dly() prints once the
  hold has been spent -- because the hold is part of the period the body sees,
  and nothing else times it.

  MEASURE  the shipped configuration, PS2_GL=eegs, counted build so the float
           bill is visible in the same run (2026-10-12, cycle 1 of the app
           phase).  This is the build row 9a landed INTO, so frame= below is
           the PRE-REFUSAL price and bg=, blit= are the PRE-10.1 prices.  The
           landed image reads those two columns at 15.60 and 39.82 ns/px,
           which are the copy floor and the floor plus the scan, so a lane
           that argues from the microseconds below is arguing from a retired
           rate; 11.5 quotes the refusing build's own three cycles, and R3
           forbids averaging the two.

    period=141338 pass=125329 tail=8 hold=16001/16 |
      poll=1 hook=7 evt=0 net=1 paint=125318 note=0 |
      render=123355 swap=0 upload=1962 px=480000 nb=1 full=1 why=c
        dmg=12,20,788,556/1 |
      comp bg=9917 frame=20342 paint=49238 blit=30741 panel=1832 bars=568
        total=112648 bfn=0 wins=4/4
    [GSTS] groups=51 qw=30165 verts=7512 prims=2504 gs=1142 us |
             drop=0 unsplit=0 texsw=22 texmiss=0 | cover=4 coverdrop=0

  Read as a ledger, the same frame in the un-counted image -- the one whose
  numbers a person would feel -- is:

    ms        stage                                    who pays
    -----------------------------------------------------------------
    46.3      the app's draw on the GS pipeline         the app
    30.7      the client-area blit, 613,440 px          the compositor
    20.3      four window decorations                   the compositor
     9.9      the background restore, 480,000 px        the compositor
     1.8      the panel and its gold bar                the compositor
     0.6      the eight test bars                       the compositor
     1.0-2.0   the GIF upload of the canvas (gs= is     this port
              the EE's bracket inside it, not the GS)
     0.007     polls, dispatch, timer nets, [PSTAT]     this port
    16.0       the hold the body asked for              dly_tsk()

  Against ASYNC_UI_BUDGET_US = 4000, the composite and present together are
  123,355 us of a 141,338 us period.  That is violation 1 of 1.4, and it is
  why lane 9 exists.

  GATE   three things hold this attribution, all of them headless:

  - THE STAGES SUM.  The six comp columns sum to 112,641 against the
    composite's own total=112,648 -- within microseconds of self-consistency,
    which is what makes the row usable as an attribution rather than a
    collection of numbers.
  - STALENESS BELONGS TO ONE WINDOW, NEVER TO A COMPOSITE.  WND::img_valid is
    the app's declaration about ITS OWN client GDEV, and the window's dev
    persists between composites, so blitting a valid window's surface and
    re-running its callback write the same pixels.  The walk used to read the
    composite's MODE instead of that flag, so one app's inval_wnd() billed
    every other window's frame to that app.  The walk now gates on
    wnd->img_valid per window (src/window/wnd.c:514), which closed a 55 ms
    row:

                before      after      measured by
      comp paint=   238,860   184,260     cycle 1, same bench, same frame
      render=       333,682   279,041     the same row
      full_render_us 112,670   57,764     scripts/ps2_smooth.sh

    The flag is honest: img_valid goes FALSE when rsz_wnd() re-opens the
    client GDEV, when an app calls inval_wnd(), and at creation (opn_wnd()
    callocs the struct), and nothing but the composite's own blit erases a
    client GDEV.  Every time-driven app in the tree ticks itself through
    inval_wnd() rather than relying on the composite.  Pi 400 takes this
    shared change, and the selective-composite block of
    verify/tests/test_desktop_clip.c counts each window's callback separately
    and requires that invalidating one of two overlapping windows renders
    exactly one -- with the gate removed it reports "A rendered 1, B rendered
    1" and FAILS, while the pixel comparison still passes.  The tally is the
    assertion; the pixel diff is the safety net.
  - EVERY STAGE OF THE WHOLE-CANVAS PATH IS NAMED.  A zero in a timing column
    once meant "this path does not time itself": the whole-canvas composite
    filled none of the comp columns, so its cost appeared only as residue.  It
    now goes through render_desktop_background_rect() -- the one builder that
    owns the cache and bfn -- and records bg/panel/bars/total the way the
    damage mode does (src/desktop/desktop.c:363-404).  Positive control, both
    halves: removing the total's btron_render_stat_max() makes the coverage
    check fail at `0 >= 21`, and routing the path back to the untimed builder
    makes the background check fail at bg=0 -- and the PIXEL comparison passes
    under both, so again the timings are the assertion and the picture is the
    safety net.  bfn=0 on a full=1 row is the premise corrected: the
    whole-canvas path never did the procedural rebuild, so there was no 25 ms
    of LZW decodes to cache away.

11.4  A stall guard smaller than a real frame fires on every frame

  LAW 11b (an animation clock is stepped by elapsed time, never by frame
  count).  A clamp that replaces "impossible" with "nominal" must be larger
  than the worst REAL frame, and an effect clock must advance with time.

  WHY  Two defects stacked made "the 3D surface is not moving".  First,
       xb_paint had `if (dt < 0 || dt > 250) dt = 16`, and this machine's
       achieved cadence is 337-398 ms between paints -- so the guard replaced
       the true elapsed time with a nominal 16 ms EVERY FRAME, and everything
       that consumes dt ran at 1/21 of real time; a 167 ms menu tween took ten
       frames, i.e. 3.5 s.  The clock itself was never the problem: get_tim()
       is ps2_task_ms(), which accumulates real CP0 Count microseconds and
       reported 354/398/337 ms correctly while the app threw the number away.
       The bound is now 4,000 ms, which is a stall and not a slow frame.
       Second, the effect clock advanced 0.01 per DRAWN FRAME (the original
       gfx_display.c:874 does exactly that), so the wave's speed WAS the frame
       rate: at 2.7 frames a second the same 0.01 is 0.13 px/s, a surface that
       reads as frozen.  It is now `s_effect_time += dt / 1600.0f`, which is
       the same sequence of values at the designed cadence and real time at
       any other.

  MEASURE  the row that separates the three claims "not moving" could mean:

    [XMBT] wave n=2 dt=144 ms t1024=131 mid1024=334640 dm=495  dsum=-294
    [XMBT] wave n=6 dt=127 ms t1024=479 mid1024=335231 dm=-341 dsum=-875

  Before, the same row read dt=16 t1024=+10/frame dm=49..53 dsum=+-40.  After,
  the clock steps 217-246 in 1/1024ths per frame instead of 10, dm runs
  0.3-1.35 px with its sign flipping as the vertex crosses its extremum, and
  dsum -1262..-5091 against the +-40 the frozen sheet measured.

  GATE  the increment is bit-identical where the cadence is as designed:
        16.0f/1600.0f is the same float as 0.01f, and
        verify/tests/test_xmb_render.c drives exactly that (g_clock += 16 per
        paint), which is why 10 of its 12 PPMs are byte-identical across the
        change.  The two that differ are the two taken after the test's own
        deliberate 600 ms jump, where the new behaviour is the point.  Raising
        a clamp means trusting the unit of the clock underneath it, so the
        three cores that link src/apps/xmb.c were READ rather than assumed:
        core_arm64.c ticks at 1 kHz and says so, core_posix.c computes ms from
        gettimeofday, core_ps2.c derives ms from Count.

  What this does NOT buy is smoothness: an animation whose wall-clock duration
  is 167 ms cannot be shown in more than one frame by a machine that paints
  every 340 ms.  The menu now snaps instead of crawling, and the only way to
  get frames back for it is the pixel work above and the GS below.

11.5  A plate the stream buries is not a cost the EE pays

  LAW 11c (a refusal must be proven by set equality, never by a saving).
  frame= paints every window's flat body plate, and on a GS layer window the
  present uploads the band FIRST and sends the recorded stream after it -- so
  the stream's pixels land on top of that plate and the plate buys nothing.
  Refusing it is sound only while the refused set equals the rect the stream
  covers: a set equality, checked from both sides, and not a number that
  merely got smaller.

  MEASURE  the landing run, three cycles paired by cycle index; un-counted
           bench build, PS2_GL=eegs, read 2026-10-13:

    [XMBT] cycle 0 ... comp bg=9937 frame=13833 paint=49429 blit=30796
             panel=1834 bars=576 total=106404 bfn=0 wins=4/4
             body=725568px skip=384000px
    [GSTS] groups=51 qw=29937 verts=7455 prims=2485 gs=1145 us |
             drop=0 unsplit=0 texsw=22 texmiss=0 | cover=4 coverdrop=0 |
             clip=768x500 b0=0,0,767,499 plate=covered

  frame= fell 6,547 / 6,508 / 6,529 us, from 20,314-20,380 to 13,785-13,833,
  and total= went 112,711 / 110,146 / 109,627 -> 106,404 / 103,713 / 103,251.
  body= 1,109,568 px became 725,568 against skip=384,000 refused, and the
  arithmetic closes without a loose term: 725,568 is exactly the three desktop
  bodies' 715,360 plus the XMB ring's 10,208 (394,208 - 384,000).  No other
  column moved -- the largest move among them is 0.5%, in paint= -- which is
  what proves frame= to be the whole-body flat fill and nothing else.  The
  control row of the same build walks three desktop windows and reads wins=3/3
  with skip=0: the refusal is a property of the data, not of the build,
  because only egl_create_window_surface() sets gl_layer and that is compiled
  in only under -DBTRON_GL_PREFER_EEGS.  That is also why the Pi 400 cannot be
  reached by it.

  One figure refuses to smooth, and it is recorded rather than fixed.  This
  build has two points -- 715,360 px at 13,069 us and 1,109,568 px at 20,380
  us -- so its marginal rate is 18.55 ns/px, and the refused px came back at
  16.95-17.05 ns: 8% UNDER the fit.  That is the honest direction for a
  refusal, because fill_body_part() is still called once per strip, so the
  border, inset and clamp arithmetic survives and only the inner write is
  skipped.  A poster would take those calls too, which is why the row is a
  refusal's floor and not its ceiling.

  GATE   the equality itself, on the host: verify/tests/test_desktop_clip.c
         paints four canvases -- the desktop, the recorded stream, the plate,
         and the painted result -- and requires the plate's hole, the window's
         client rect and the stream's clip to be ONE rectangle,
         204,152,496,316 = 47,888 px, asserted from both directions.  It
         requires the counters to match the geometry rather than to be
         believed: recorded 47,888 skipped against 40,192 drawn, and 0 skipped
         / 88,080 drawn on the plain window and the recorded non-layer window.
         A 32x32 box inside a layer reports the whole rect while writing only
         its own pixels, and a resize grip drawn inside the hole is STILL
         drawn -- the skip is the plate, not every fill in the rect.  42/42
         checks with 1 SKIP (the stubbed panel bar), read 2026-10-13.

  In the image the same premise stays live and is printed: [GSTS]
  plate=covered, with b0= reaching all four edges of clip=; [GSPLATE] silent;
  drop=0 and coverdrop=0; vram_left=1081344 unchanged, because a refusal
  claims no plane; and body= + skip= equal to the unrefused total.

  INFERRED, and labelled so: plate=covered is NECESSARY, NOT SUFFICIENT.  b0=
  is the span of the FIRST vertex group only, rounded inward -- a vertex at
  767.4 reaches 767, never 768 -- so it proves the first group buries every
  edge, not that a later group leaves no interior fragment uncovered.  That is
  why the refusal stops at the client rect and no wider, and why the union of
  all groups was rejected as an instrument: a union can be covered by two
  groups that each cover half.

11.6  A producer that posts is a producer that keeps painting

  LAW 11d (a bound on the bus is a bound on buffers, never on slots).
  PS2_GIF_RING_CAP says how many transfers the ring may OWN at once -- the
  queued descriptors plus the one the channel is reading -- and not how many
  entries s_gif_ring[] has room for behind the in-flight one.  The reason is
  the buffer-lifetime law and not the array: a DMA reads its packet while the
  EE is doing something else, so every outstanding descriptor is a producer's
  memory this port has promised not to touch, and the one on the bus is
  exactly as much a promise as the seven behind it.  A queue-only cap would
  let the ring hold CAP+1 buffers while printing `cap=512` as though that
  number were the promise.

  LANE 10 LANDED 2026-10-14 as src/drivers/ps2/ps2_gif_ring.{c,h}, and its
  shape is three calls, none of which waits at the wrong moment:

    post()   append, and start if -- and only if -- channel 2 is free.
             Never spins.  At capacity it refuses and counts the refusal.
    drain()  hand the channel as many queued descriptors as it will take,
             and return with one still in flight.  Never spins.
    reap()   the completion check, at the NEXT yield: drain, and if a
             transfer is still running wait for that one and count the wait.

  The yield is eegs_present_begin() -- src/gl/eegs_present.c:88 -- once per
  paint pass, and it is the only place the port asks "has the GIF finished
  reading what I committed last pass".  That one row is the lane's whole
  trade: the question moves from 52 moments a frame to one, and the answer it
  waits for is still an empty ring, because the stage IS the buffer those
  transfers read.  A begin() that merely drained would return with a group in
  flight and the recorder would overwrite bytes channel 2 is reading --
  correct on a fast consumer, corrupted at exactly the moment the GIF fell
  behind, which is the class of defect no word gate can see.

  Two producers post, and both are the paint pass's own memory: the staging
  buffer, which has one writer and is untouched between send() and the next
  begin(); and env_pkt (ps2_gs.c:738), a static that is rewritten only at the
  next apply, which is after that begin().  Everything else stays
  synchronous on purpose -- ps2_gs_gif_send() now reaps, starts, waits on
  ps2_gif_hw_busy() and counts its own wait into the sync= column -- because
  setup_pkt, img_tag and the canvas have a writer in every console character
  and no reap scheduled between one and the next.  Order across the two kinds
  survives because the ring is a FIFO and because the synchronous path reaps
  before it starts, so it cannot overtake a group it never waited for.

  The capacity is derived, not chosen: one transfer per recorded group,
  EEGS_MAX_GROUPS = 256 of them, plus the environment packet, so a legal
  frame cannot reach 512 and the gif row's `drop=` means the CONSUMER fell
  behind rather than the producer asked for too much.  eegs_present.c ties
  the two numbers at compile time through the repo's
  `typedef char name[(cond) ? 1 : -1]` idiom -- the image is -std=c99, so
  _Static_assert is not available to it.  LAW 16b's "capacity is a latency
  knob, never a correctness limit" holds one layer ABOVE this one, and the
  ring is deliberately not given the same licence: a refused descriptor drops
  the tail of a frame, which is a picture with pieces missing, so the first
  refusal ends the frame and the lost groups are counted into the same drop=
  column the stage's own overflow uses.  An overflow is therefore visible in
  both rows, and they answer different questions -- the recorder row's drop=
  says words did not cross, the gif row's drop= says the ring is what
  refused them.

  MEASURE  the instrument is [GSTS] gif:, three windows read off one armed
           bench run of 2026-10-14 (BENCH_APP=1, PS2_GL=eegs PS2_FP=hw,
           un-counted).  Each row is a profile taken since the last print,
           so the FIRST of the three is since BOOT: its sync= counts the
           atlas, the font sheet and every console line that ever blocked,
           and reading 39,214 us as a frame cost would be the first wrong
           thing a reader could do with this row.

    [GSTS] gif: posted=52 started=52 queued=0 inflight=0 cap=512 | drop=0
      hold=190 us | reap=18182 blocked=18514 us max=190 us | sync=30241
      blocked=39214 us max=55 us
    [GSTS] gif: posted=52 started=52 queued=0 inflight=0 cap=512 | drop=0
      hold=189 us | reap=627 blocked=815 us max=189 us | sync=1489
      blocked=1697 us max=55 us
    [GSTS] gif: posted=52 started=4 queued=48 inflight=1 cap=512 | drop=0
      hold=28 us | reap=7 blocked=7 us max=1 us | sync=31 blocked=820 us
      max=55 us

  The third window is the ring doing the thing it was opened for: the pass
  posted 52 transfers, the channel had taken 4, and 48 descriptors plus the
  one in flight were still owned when the producer RETURNED.  `queued=48
  inflight=1` is what a §3.2 producer that was not stopped by its consumer
  looks like, and it is the only row in this port that can show it.  The
  first two read `queued=0 inflight=0` because their begin() reaped the ring
  empty -- the same fact one moment later, from the other side of the yield.
  hold= is the longest a descriptor sat queued before its transfer started:
  189-190 us in the two windows that were reaped, 28 in the one that was
  still draining when the row printed, and equal to wait_max in the first
  pair, which is what a FIFO whose head is the long wait looks like.  The
  residue of VIOLATION 2 is the sync= column -- 31 sends costing 820 us in
  the steady window, none over 55 -- and it is the number that will tell the
  next reader whether the canvas band deserves a second, wider ring.  It does
  not get one in this increment, and that is a lane boundary, not an
  oversight.

  What this run does NOT show, said so that it is not read as shown.  drop=
  is 0 in every gif window and in every recorder row of the run, so the
  REFUSAL path never executed on the guest: the bound was only ever measured
  as being far above a frame's 52.  It is exercised on the host, where the
  gate overrides PS2_GIF_RING_CAP to 8 and posts nine.  SKIP, not PASS.  And
  STR says the quadwords left the EE, not that the GS drew them -- the ring
  inherits the completion boundary the synchronous sender always had, and
  nothing here measures GS pipeline time (M7).

  The non-regression pair, one bench composition read either side of the
  landing:

    [BENCH] full      41,822 -> 41,887 us   (+0.16%), and the whole of the
                      +65 is accounted: 54 us in upload= (821/822 ->
                      875/875) and 10 in render=, i.e. the instrument's own
                      bookkeeping on the synchronous path it now times
    [PSTAT] budget ui passes=12 max=112,082 -> 111,810 us, misses=10 -> 10,
                      late=9 -> 9

  so the presentation plane was not made later by being made non-blocking,
  and 1.4's VIOLATION 1 and the budget rows are untouched by this lane.  No
  [XMBT] cycle-row pair exists for the pair -- the pre-ring full log was
  overwritten -- so the cycle columns are SKIP on this lane too, and 11.5's
  three cycles stay the reference for frame= and total=.

  GATE   three suites, and each row names the edit that turns it red.
    - Host, `make test-ps2-gif-ring`: 59 checks, 0 failures.  It drives a
      simulated channel whose busy() answers a countdown and whose wait
      costs clock, because the first cut of that sim freed the channel only
      when the test said so and the gate sat in reap()'s wait loop until it
      was killed.  A hanging gate is not a gate, so the sim now carries a
      poll budget and the run ends on `no transfer needed the poll budget`:
      a future wedge reads as a red row instead of a stuck terminal.
    - Host, `make test-eegs-present`: 70 checks, 0 failures.  The rows that
      matter here are `begin() left nothing outstanding`, `the held frame is
      the same three transfers, not six` and `the following frame is its own
      three transfers` -- a ring that leaked a descriptor fails those, and
      the picture it would corrupt is a window drawing behind somebody
      else's primitives.
    - Image, `make test-ps2`: 92 / 92, 2026-10-14, PS2_GL=eegs PS2_FP=hw.
      The ring's rows are seven, and they are structural rather than
      numerical -- channel 2's four registers are written at the seam and
      nowhere else; the draw environment leaves through ps2_gif_post() and
      touches no DMAC register itself; the synchronous sender reaps before it
      starts and counts its own blocked time; eegs_present_begin() calls
      ps2_gif_reap(); and the ring's bookkeeping links as seven functions
      that touch no GS and no DMAC register, which is the same purity that
      lets the host gate run it on a machine with no PS2 in it.

  The controls that made those rows mean something are reproducible:
  `scripts/ps2_gif_ring_controls.sh` applies each of the seven defects in
  turn, rebuilds the named gate from scratch, and restores the file, and it
  ends by requiring both gates green again on the restored tree -- so a
  GREEN from that script is a statement about the instrument, not about the
  luck of the working copy.  Run 2026-10-14: six RED rows on the ring gate
  with 18, 1, 10, 3, 3 and 6 failing checks, one RED on the present gate
  with 1, then 59 and 70 green.  The failure counts, and the perl `d` trap
  that made one control read green for no reason at all, are in the
  quarantined record dated 2026-10-14.

=====================================================================
PART FOUR -- THE INTERCONNECT: INPUT AS A REAL PLANE
=====================================================================

  ASYNC.txt 5 forbids "USB progress only in the GUI loop".  On this machine
  that prohibition became a 1 kHz plane with a measured 1.68 us handler, and
  the pointer became a chain of five layers whose units differ.  This Part
  states both, because every pointer symptom in this port's history came from
  measuring one layer's output and correcting it at another.

12. The 1 kHz plane and the HID handover
----------------------------------------

  LAW 12 (the plane collects; the pass delivers).  The interrupt handler reads
  the bus and NOTHING ELSE.  Everything with a side effect on the rest of the
  image -- the event-queue syscalls, the prints, the report processors --
  stays in the pass.

  WHY  A report read at present time inherits the GUI loop's latency, which is
       ui=16667 us plus whatever a frame costs.  And a handler that renders,
       allocates or blocks makes its own measured WCET meaningless, which is
       the only number that justifies running at 1 kHz at all.

  MEASURE  what crosses the boundary.  src/drivers/ps2/ps2_usb.c divides its
           own work in two: intr_take() is the bus half -- read the
           descriptor's condition code, eight uncached byte reads, advance the
           ring index, ed_unhalt(), intr_top_up() -- and is the only part that
           runs on the plane.  The handler therefore touches no heap, renders
           nothing, and calls no BIOS service that could block.

  LAW 12b (one queue, one writer per index).  hoff_push() publishes into a
  256-slot ring; the plane owns s_hoff_in and the pass owns s_hoff_out, and no
  other party writes either, so there is NO LOCK and no test-and-set in the
  handler.  A full queue increments dropped rather than overwriting an unread
  report.  hoff_drain() stamps each slot with the Count reading taken at the
  handler entry that collected it, so the hold the pass reports is the hold
  from the BUS, not from the moment the slot was written.

  LAW 12c (the handover happens once, and only on evidence).
  ps2_usb_input_plane(1) is called in the [ASYNC] boot block when the plane's
  own verdict is LIVE (got + 1 >= owed over the boot window), BEFORE the
  divisor and wcet rows print, so the cost of the collection is inside the
  measurement of the handler that performs it.  It sets s_plane_collects
  *before* installing the hook: the flag is the protocol, and installing a
  producer the consumer does not know about would collect into a queue nothing
  drains.  On DEAD or STALLED the pass keeps polling the rings itself -- a
  queue nobody fills is a dead keyboard, so the fallback is not optional.  The
  check for a reclaimed IOP-RAM shadow block also stays on the pass side,
  because reading it is a bus access and the handler has a budget.

  GATE   proven on this machine: the handler's cost with the collection inside
         it is `isr wcet=498 ticks = 1.68 us`, against `wcet=104 ticks = 0.35
         us` with an empty hook -- so a period of polling four interrupt rings
         costs about 1.3 us, 3.4% of the 50 us budget, with overruns=0.
         ASYNC.txt 10's 2 ms pointer-update gap is the gate on the other side:
         plane_held_max_us is the longest hold the pass has measured and is
         judged against 2000.

  What a headless run can and cannot show.  scripts/ps2_smooth.sh moves no
  host cursor, so a run legitimately prints reports=0.  The instrument that
  proves the collector runs with no hand on the mouse is `collected on 2223
  periods`: plane_periods counts handler entries that passed the host guard
  and reached the collector, and a period needs only the clock while a report
  needs motion.  plane_handover_miss is judged at 0 for a LIVE plane and
  SKIPped for any other verdict; plane_reports_in_pass must not grow once the
  plane owns the rings.  Non-zero plane_reports_collected and a non-zero
  plane_held_max_us require `make run-ps2` and a hand on the mouse:

    [PSTAT] plane: reports=0 on the input plane, 0 in a pass, dropped=0
                queue max=0 of 256, collected on 2223 periods
    [PSTAT] plane: held max=0 us mean=0 us over 0 delivered -- isr 1.68 us of the
                50 us budget, overruns=0

  One fact about the handover is easy to lose, because it is not a timing
  problem: the keyboard half of it is EDGE-ONLY.  A held key crosses this
  boundary as a press and, much later, a release -- nothing in between -- so
  the repeat a person expects is not missing from the bus, it was never on it.
  Who produces it, and on whose numbers, is chapter 19's LAW 19f.

13. Which layer owns the pointer
--------------------------------

  LAW 13 (one constant per layer).  A count means something different at each
  stage of this chain, and each stage has its own setting.  Correcting a
  layer's output at a different layer is how this pointer was tuned in circles
  for a session; the table in 13.4 is the thing the instruments read against.

13.1  The instruments: four commands, four different questions

  - ptrcal    -- IS THE MAPPING CORRECT?  It feeds synthetic reports through
    the driver's own report entry, so a count travels the same road as a count
    off the bus (decoded, scaled, bordered, posted as an event) with the hand
    out of the loop.  It turns the cap off for the run and restores it
    afterwards, because the cap is a policy about how fast a PERSON may be
    moved while ptrcal measures how a count becomes a pixel; left in place it
    would report the limiter as a broken mapping.  Its header prints the live
    source= and curve= because a [CAL] log copied out of a session is incomplete
    without the policy that made it: the field is kept so that a gain row which
    is NON-linear under this port's own shaping reads as a fault and not as a
    feature -- which is what it used to mean on the hw profile, and what it no
    longer means on either one since LAW 13d.
  - ptrstat   -- WHAT IS ACTUALLY ON THE WIRE?  It counts EVERY report, not
    the one-in-sixty-four the [RAW] rows print, and sorts them by magnitude.
    It closes the window since its last call, so the first call of a session
    reports whatever accumulated during boot: type `ptrstat boot` to discard
    that window, then perform a slide, then label the window that contains it.
  - stroke pair -- WHICH SPEED OWNED THE SAME SLIDE?  The ratio below is the
    one test that decides whether a guest gain can ship at all, and taking it
    used to mean TYPING between the two halves of a single experiment.  So the
    reports are filed into strokes as they arrive: 100 ms of stillness ends
    one, a slide of 800 counts or more becomes a sample, and the session keeps
    its slowest sample and its fastest one.  Every place this port prints
    pointer statistics -- `ptrstat`, Ctrl+\, the session's own exit row, and
    the headless run's closing receipt -- ends with that pair, its two speeds,
    and the ratio between them.  The speeds are printed beside it because two
    slides at nearly one speed are NOT the experiment, and a row that said
    1.00x about them would be a false plateau in the other direction.  The
    refusals go further than a shrug: an unfinished session prints how many
    reports it did see and how long its longest stroke was, so the reader can
    check the numbers against their own hand, and a session whose pointer
    device never answered prints that instead -- the two faults are opposite,
    and 13.5 tells which one the first
    live session hit.

  - ptgain    -- WHAT IS A COUNT WORTH TO THE HAND THAT SENT IT?  The only
    instrument here whose denominator is a human movement of known length
    rather than a constant, and therefore the only one that can CHECK
    PS2_EMU_POINTER_SCALE instead of assuming it.
  - ptrsrc    -- WHICH OF THE TWO PROFILES IS ANSWERING, AND WHY?  See 13.2.
  - sens / maxstep -- this port's own POLICY, applied only once the first two
    agree the path is sound.  Since LAW 13e `sens` writes the same variable the
    Settings panel and the launcher dial, so it is the prompt's hand on one dial
    rather than the only hand on it.

  ptrcal drives the same report handler ptrstat counts, so it must not run
  inside a measurement window.  Every ptrstat call resets, so forgetting costs
  one keystroke.

  The pair resets with nothing, and that is the point of it.  The window is
  the wrong shape for this test twice over: a hand that cannot place the
  cursor is a hand being asked to type at a prompt, and the keystroke between
  the slow slide and the fast one DISCARDS the slow slide -- the very sample
  the answer rests on.  The splitter's own arithmetic is gated on the host
  instead, 59 checks and a matched pair of controls with OPPOSITE answers
  (counts proportional to distance must read 1.00x; counts proportional to
  speed must read acceleration), by `make test-ps2-pointer`.  Three further
  controls there are refusals of a different kind: deleting the 16-bit frame
  mask, accepting any nudge as a sample, and letting a pair of near-identical
  speeds answer.  What a headless run prints is `no pair yet` with its two
  readings attached -- 404 reports seen, longest stroke 8480 counts -- which
  is the correct answer for a synthetic stream that has one speed per phase:
  the pair is settled by a hand and by nothing else, and the row that ships a
  gain is the row a live session prints.

  The decisive protocol is five windows, of which the two that decide the
  layer question the splitter now keeps on its own -- see the `stroke pair`
  row at the foot of every [PSTAT] print, and 13.1's note on why a window
  cannot hold a two-part experiment:

    Window           Hand                        Decides
    ---------------------------------------------------------------
    ptrstat boot     nothing                       clears the warm-up
    ptrstat slow     one deliberate slide          distance at LOW speed
    ptrstat fast     THE SAME PHYSICAL SLIDE       ...and at HIGH speed
    ptrstat h        sideways only                 does the other axis stay 0
    ptrstat w        Mac cursor, left edge to      gain against a host distance
                    right edge                     that needs no measuring

  Read the `vs previous window` row:

    x = 1.0x          the wire carries DISTANCE.  A single guest gain is then
                      correct and sufficient, and sens is where the feel gets
                      set.
    x >> 1.0x         the numbers are already cursor pixels, multiplied by
                      speed by something OUTSIDE this port.  No constant can
                      be right for both rows.  The answer is NOT a cap:
                      dp_ptr_limit() spends a bounded distance per INPUT
                      period and abandons debt past DP_PTR_LIMIT_DEBT steps,
                      which makes where the cursor lands a function of hand
                      SPEED rather than hand DISTANCE -- the same
                      non-proportionality, moved.  What this port does instead
                      is take the ratio as evidence that the wrong source
                      profile is live; the gain belongs to the layer above
                      (macOS's own cursor curve, the only multiplier here that
                      acts on distance).
    still large       a real mouse that is not moving answers IN tokens with
                      NAKs, not with 00 00 00 reports, so a big still count
                      says a synthetic source is producing reports whether or
                      not anything moved.  Cross-check the e0/e1 column of the
                      [SCH] row.
    x max << 127      a genuine device saturates at |127| on a fast slide; a
                      source whose maximum never approaches it across a slide
                      as fast as a hand can manage is coalescing motion into
                      something other than device counts.
    reports -> N/s    the bus's effective polling rate, set by the device's
                      bInterval, NOT by this controller's 1 ms frame.  USB 1.1
                      gives low-speed interrupt pipes a 10 ms floor and
                      full-speed HID 1-8 ms, so 100-125/s is what a REAL PS2
                      mouse also reads at and is not by itself evidence of an
                      emulator.
    net << total      counts are leaking into the axis nobody asked for -- a leak no
      (the h window)   per-axis synthetic test can see, because the synthetic
                      sweep never moves both axes the way a hand does.
    border / onwall   what the screen edge did to the same window, in guest
                      pixels and per paint pass.  This is the row that answers
                      "why is the cursor sticky at the edges" and no count row
                      can, because the answer is about POSITION rather than
                      the wire.  A relative pointer's position is the integral
                      of its counts, so pixels the border discards are gone
                      for good while pushing further into the wall is free and
                      produces nothing: AN EDGE IS A ONE-WAY SINK, and leaving
                      it costs exactly as much host travel as entering it gave
                      away.  Read discarded as the share of the hand's
                      distance the wall ate: near zero means the cursor merely
                      RESTS at the edge and the stickiness is elsewhere; a
                      third or more means the gain is spending the whole 800
                      px canvas faster than the hand can cross the desktop it
                      sits on -- a saturation limit, lowered with sens, not
                      raised.
