13.2  Two source profiles, chosen from the device's own identity

  A count off this bus is worth a different number of pixels depending on what
  produced it, and no single guest constant can be right for both cases.  The
  port therefore ships two stubs, picked from the enumerated device's own USB
  identity at boot rather than from where the code happened to be built:

    Profile  Selected when                       Gain        Curve       Cap
    ------------------------------------------------------------------------
    emu      the pointer answers as PCSX2's      96/256 =    none        off
             HID Mouse, device 0627:0001         0.375 guest
             (desc_id == 0x00010627)             px per count
    hw       anything else answers -- a mouse    verbatim    none        off
             somebody sells                      256         (LAW 13d; it
                                                             was dp_ptr_
                                                             riscos(), see
                                                             below)

  LAW 13d (no guest-side multiplier scales by speed).  Both profiles are
  proportional now: one report count is worth the same number of guest pixels at
  a crawl and at a flick, and the only dial in front of the cursor is `sens`.
  What this port used to carry was the Pi 400's curve on the hw profile --
  dp_ptr_riscos(), whose multiplier is 256 + step*128 + (mag>4 ? 128 : 0)/256.
  Measured against the dial's own range, that is not an accelerator one can
  decline: `g_mouse_step_mult` accepts 1..4 from Settings > Input, which is
  384/256 to 768/256 -- a FLOOR of 1.5x -- and any value outside that range is
  rewritten to 4, so the shipped default of 2 handed a real mouse 2.0x on small
  moves and 2.5x past four counts, with no setting at which the pointer was
  simply proportional.  A PS2 hand could not ask for 1:1.
  The curve is therefore gone from this target's shaping, and three things are
  NOT thereby changed.  (i) The Pi 400's feel is intact: dp_ptr_riscos() stays in
  src/graphics/dp_accel.c, where core_arm64.c calls it and Settings > Input dials
  its step, because the shared header is not the place to settle one machine's
  feel and the working reference must not pay for this lane.  What changed there
  is that its body now sits behind `#ifndef BTRON_PS2_TARGET`, so this image
  carries neither a call to it nor its text -- an uncalled curve is still a
  speed-dependent multiplier living inside an image whose law forbids one, and
  only a disassembly read tells dead code apart from a branch a merge restored.
  The Pi 400 does not define that macro, so its compiled bytes are unchanged.
  (ii) The live PCSX2 profile was already proportional, so a session on
  the emulator's hidmouse feels exactly as fast as it did; the multiplier in
  front of those counts is macOS's own cursor curve, above this port and named
  by 13.4.  (iii) `sens`, `maxstep` and the subpixel carry are the same code,
  so the measurement instruments 13.1 describes still measure what they say.
  Two things keep this law.  The predicate above makes it a LINK error: a PS2
  translation unit that calls dp_ptr_riscos() fails to build rather than quietly
  re-accelerating the pointer on the one machine that has no screen to complain
  with -- the declaration stays visible to every target so that failure names the
  function instead of degrading into an implicit-declaration message about the
  header.  And scripts/test_ps2.sh's pointer-policy rows read the linked image,
  not the source: they require no jump to dp_ptr_riscos() inside the mouse
  handler or anywhere in the image, require that function's text to be absent
  from the image, keep the two dp_ptr_scale() calls that still do the gain (a
  window that failed to extract would read zero of everything and certify a
  pointer that had been deleted), and require the hw profile's own gain row to
  announce a proportional shape with no curve -- a needle this paragraph does not
  spell out, because this file is the /SYS payload and a needle spelled here
  would be in the image whatever the code did.

  Detection runs once at boot after the host engine has enumerated, and again
  after `usb probe`; the boot log says which one it picked, in both units:

    [PS2] Pointer source emu (detected): 96/256 px per count = 0.37 px per host
        cursor px, no curve, cap off
    [PS2] Pointer source hw (detected): 256/256 px per count, proportional (no
        curve), cap off

  `ptrsrc hw` / `ptrsrc emu` override that for a session, which is the only
  way to run the emulator's stub against a hardware-shaped count stream or the
  reverse without changing what is plugged in; `ptrsrc auto` hands the
  decision back to detection.  Since LAW 13d the two profiles differ in one
  thing -- their gain -- and the same proportional shaping stands behind both.
  Switching profiles clears the carried subpixel remainder and any distance the
  previous one had not spent, so a change mid-session cannot leave the old
  source's half-pixel tail to be paid out by the new one.

  LAW 13b (one gain constant).  There is ONE gain in this stack: the live
  variable LAW 13e names, whose default on the emulator profile is
  PS2_EMU_MULT_FP, reached from the console as `sens`.  PointerXScale and
  PointerYScale look like a second copy of it -- both are in the repo's ini,
  set to 8 -- but neither name exists anywhere in PCSX2 v2.8.2's source, a USB
  Type::Pointer binding has no scale factor at all (InputManager.cpp:1043-57),
  and its callback is handed the raw delta (:1398-1403).  Editing them changes
  nothing.  The same is true of PointerXSpeed, PointerYSpeed, PointerInertia
  and the two dead-zone keys: they are read (:1645-1653) and applied
  (:1372-81) to the SCALED value on the ProcessEvent path -- pad and lightgun
  bindings -- while the hidmouse callback gets the unscaled delta.  They are
  INERT here, not neutral, and the only live pointer setting in the ini is
  `[USB1] hidmouse_Pointer = Pointer-0`.

  Where 96 came from is a hand, not arithmetic: at 32/256 a stroke could not
  cross the 800 px canvas, at 256/256 the cursor sat pinned against a wall,
  and 96/256 is what has shipped between those two reports.  Those
  observations were taken against COUNTS, so they survive the scale correction
  unchanged; what does not survive is reading them as "3 px per host px",
  which was eight times too flattering -- the shipped pointer has been
  tracking the hand at a third of its travel all along.

    sens   px per count    guest px per host cursor px
    ---------------------------------------------------
      25      64/256                     0.25
      38      97/256        0.38 -- the shipped default
      51     130/256                     0.51
      64     163/256                     0.64
      77     197/256                     0.77
      89     227/256                     0.89
     100     256/256        1.00 -- the too-fast bound, counts verbatim

  Roughly ONE sens per hundredth of a guest px per host px.  The prompt value
  does not survive a reboot, so what a dial settles belongs in PS2_EMU_MULT_FP
  afterwards -- and the same is now true of the two hands LAW 13e puts on the
  same dial, because nothing in this stack writes a pointer setting to /SYS.

  LAW 13e (one gain, one home, several hands).  This stack has one multiplier
  and it lives in one variable: `g_ptr_gain_fp`, in 256ths of a pixel per count,
  defined in src/settings/input.c next to the dial that draws it and read by this
  port's pointer on every pass.  Three hands turn it and none of them keeps a
  copy of it: `sens` at the Stage-1 prompt, the Settings > Input panel's gain
  block, the launcher's row in the pointer band.  Each writes that one int and
  the core notices between passes -- it re-reads the global, clears the subpixel
  carries and the unspent distance for the reason 13.2 already gives for a
  profile switch, and prints the new gain with the same words the prompt uses, so
  a hand that changed it from a window and a hand that changed it from the
  console read the same row back.  0 means "the live source profile's own
  default", which is what keeps PS2_EMU_MULT_FP and PS2_MOUSE_MULT_FP defaults
  rather than a second copy of somebody's decision.

  What leaves this target with the law is the pair of controls that sat where the
  gain block sits now.  They wrote `g_mouse_step_mult` and
  `g_mouse_accel_profile`, and since LAW 13d nothing on this machine reads
  either.  A dial that the pointer ignores is worse than no dial: it teaches the
  hand that this machine cannot be trusted to answer a control, which is the
  exact suspicion this chapter exists to settle.  The two names are therefore
  defined only for a target that compiles the curve -- the same predicate
  LANE 15 put dp_ptr_riscos() behind, and for the same reason -- so a PS2 row
  that sets one of them fails at the LINK rather than surviving as scenery.  The
  declarations in the applet and the launcher are cut with it, so the failure
  names the variable instead of leaving an orphan extern behind.  The Pi 400
  keeps its radios, its globals and its curve unchanged: every word of this
  paragraph is inside `BTRON_PS2_TARGET`, and the arm64 image is compared
  byte-for-byte with the build that predates this lane.

  The dial's stops are one array, `btron_ptr_gain_fp`, declared beside
  dp_ptr_scale() in <btron/dp_accel.h>: index 0 is the auto choice and 64, 96,
  128, 160, 192, 256 are the six gains a hand can ask for.  Panel radios, the
  launcher's enum row and the nearest-step index all read those numbers, so what
  "a quarter of a pixel per count" is called exists in one file instead of three
  that can drift apart.  That 96 and 256 are also the two profile defaults is
  the dial pointing at what ships, not a second home for those constants.

  This is a dial, not a measurement.  The number that makes the cursor equal the
  Mac's own is still unsettled -- 13.2's open task, and `ptgain` is the
  instrument for it -- and a hand that moves this control is choosing a stop, not
  taking a reading.

13.3  The cursor's frame rate is the paint pass's frame rate

  LAW 13c.  A relative pointer's screen position is only ever updated when the
  screen is repainted, so no gain, scale or accelerator changes the cursor's
  cadence; only the cost of a present does.

  WHY / MEASURE  the table that reversed this document's own conclusion.  The
                 old argument was that a narrow region "has to be uploaded a
                 row at a time" and therefore that bands must be FULL-WIDTH to
                 be cheap.  The per-row GIF setup is real -- 16 rows cost 10
                 us of upload, 32 rows 20 us, about 0.6 us a row -- and it is
                 nothing next to the 528 us the same 16x16 region costs to
                 render:

    region presented            px      us/pass   render    swap   upload
    ----------------------------------------------------------------------
    whole canvas 800x600      480,000     265,782  215,049  49,094   1,637
    one 16-row band, full w    12,800       5,370    4,015   1,309      45
    64-row band, full width     51,200      20,378   14,964   5,237     175
    cursor sprite 16x16             256        528      486      29      10
    80x16                          1,280      1,033      883     133      14
    16x80                          1,280      1,204    1,006     146      50

  Render and swap scale with PIXELS, not rows.  So the present is clipped in
  BOTH dimensions and a cursor pass presents the union of the old and new
  sprite boxes at 536-1050 us instead of 5,370.  Against the emulator's ~60
  reports a second, that is the difference between 27 painted positions per
  second (one screen update per four hand movements) and 137-267 -- which is
  why the supply is now the limiter rather than the repaint.

  The ceiling that bites is CADENCE, not width.  New motion is published once
  per emulated EE frame (Counters.cpp:500 VSyncStart -> PollSources), so at 60
  fps the host makes at most ~60 distinct reports a second however often OHCI
  polls at its 1 ms frame; button-unchanged motion coalesces into one slot of
  a 16-slot queue (hid.cpp:404-18), and a full queue drops (:383-89).  The
  |127| byte ceiling is NOT one of them: `dx = int_clamp(xdx, -127, 127); xdx
  -= dx;` carries a report's overflow into the next one, so no distance is
  lost at speed -- which is why ptrstat's sat= counts a saturated report, not
  a missing stroke.  And the emulator keeps a delta in a 16.16 accumulator,
  exchanges it whole at each poll (InputManager.cpp:1434, :1367) and truncates
  the float on the way into the device (usb-hid.cpp:731), so SUB-count
  fractions are dropped at that boundary.  At one count per host px, sat=
  cannot bite until 127 px of cursor travel inside one event, and a brisk drag
  reaches that only as a flick.  One constant, one boundary.

13.4  Which layer owns what, with the label each claim carries

  Read each block as one row of the pointer's topology: what the layer does to
  a movement, what can be set about it, and what the claim is worth.

  macOS
       behaviour  pointer acceleration and inertia, applied to the host cursor
                  before any byte exists
       setting    defaults write -g com.apple.mouse.scaling -1
       status     INFERRED (no raw mode in PCSX2 to bypass), and NO LIVE
                  SESSION HAS PRINTED A stroke pair VERDICT YET -- the
                  instrument that decides this row is compiled and gated on
                  the host, but the row it fills needs a hand.  The first live
                  session printed neither answer, only windows in which NO
                  MOUSE REPORT LANDED, which measures the hand and the window
                  rather than this layer

  PCSX2 hidmouse
       behaviour  one HID event per IN token; byte 0 = 3 buttons + pad, then
                  X, Y, wheel as three 8-bit signed fields; reports the MAC
                  CURSOR'S OWN DELTAS, not device counts
       setting    nothing -- the two ini Scale keys are read by no code
       status     PROVEN from v2.8.2 source: raw delta to the callback, no
                  scale on a Pointer binding, int_clamp carries the remainder,
                  motion once per EE frame -> ~60 reports/s

  OHCI frame
       behaviour  HcFmInterval = 0x27782EDF -> 11999 bit times at 12 Mbit/s =
                  1 ms, and the periodic list is walked once per frame, so a
                  device polls at 1/2/4/8/16/32 ms
       setting    bInterval at enumerate time
       status     PROVEN: the same word real PS2 firmware writes

  Real PS2 hardware
       behaviour  one OHCI shared with the IOP at IOP physical 0x1F801600,
                  Full Speed + Low Speed only, IOP interrupt 22, DMA
                  structures confined to the IOP's 2 MB; the host stack is an
                  IOP module and the EE is a client of it over SIF
       setting    nothing in this port
       status     spec-derived; our probe reading that base proves only that
                  PCSX2 decodes it, and "the EE cannot poll it on retail
                  hardware" is the open risk

  This port
       behaviour  source profile -> dp_ptr_scale()'s 8.8 gain (the one variable
                  LAW 13e names, which `sens`, the Settings panel and the
                  launcher all write) -> dp_ptr_limit() px per INPUT period
                  (maxstep, off; the period is the plane table's, scaled by the
                  periods a pass actually owes) -> the screen borders.  Nothing
                  in that chain reads the counts' SPEED, which is what LAW 13d
                  says and what 13.1's `curve=` field is now there to catch:
                  `none` on both profiles.
       setting    ptrsrc, sens, maxstep, and the panel's or the launcher's gain
                  dial, which is the same variable and not a fourth one
       status     PROVEN by ptrcal for the chain; for LAW 13d the proof on
                  offer is the set of rows in scripts/test_ps2.sh that read the
                  LINKED IMAGE -- the handler's shaping window, the absent
                  accelerator's text, this target's symbol table -- and for LAW
                  13e the same kind of pair: the dead dial's strings and symbols
                  gone, the gain dial's label present, and the panel's still-live
                  rows present so an absent row cannot be a missing file.  A
                  fresh `ptrcal` run over the hw profile -- the [CAL] gain rows
                  flattening to one constant -- is SKIP, not PASS, because no
                  booted session has printed it since the curve came out.

  Two rows cut opposite ways.  The frame interval is a MATCH with real
  hardware -- the value our boot log measures is the value Sony's own usbd
  writes -- so the bus row will not change on a retail console and there is no
  point tuning for it.  The device-rate row is the opposite: 100-125 reports/s
  is normal there too, so the emulator cannot be blamed for it, and the only
  real-hardware divergence that matters for pointer FEEL is the macOS row
  above the wire.  That row is the one the stroke pair exists to settle: it is
  the only claim in this table whose evidence is a hand doing the same slide
  twice, so it is stated as INFERRED until a live session prints its verdict,
  and turning the Mac's own curve off is a TEST of this table rather than a
  fix for a cursor.

13.5  Settled facts, so they are not re-opened

  - The first live session that asked for the stroke pair settled nothing
    about the layer it was meant to test, and the reason is worth keeping so
    no one re-runs it blind: 55 s on the desktop, some two dozen Ctrl+\
    prints, and every pointer window between them read reports=0 while the
    input plane stayed alive on its own row (reports= climbing, dropped=0,
    overruns=0, the ISR inside its 50 us budget).  A second session the same
    afternoon settles the other half -- `[HID] first report landed, frame
    0x0315 kbd=0 mouse=11`, eleven mouse reports before anyone touched a key
    -- so the wire is NOT dead, and the zeros were a hand at a keystroke
    rather than a port at fault.  The row now tells those two apart by name,
    and neither session fills 13.4's macOS row, which needs the same slide
    twice at two speeds.
  - A live row nobody copied out is a row that never ran: PCSX2 writes one log
    path, `pcsx2/PCSX2/logs/emulog.txt`, and every launch overwrites it -- the
    12:18 session above survives only as the rows quoted here.  The headless
    bench is the exception only because scripts/ps2_bench.sh runs against a
    mktemp datapath, so it never touches that file.
  - The emulator's |127| clamp is not biting (2026-09-24 run, 460 reports: max
    count on any axis 27, median 2, byte ceiling 127).  Lowering PointerXScale
    to buy headroom buys nothing; do not re-open this without a sat= column
    that is non-zero.
  - Nothing is dropped between the bus and the handler: [DEV] p= retired
    reports and the [RAW] sequence numbers agree (460 vs #448 at 1-in-64
    sampling) and [RING] n=1 max=1 says the ring never queued up.  The 20-50
    reports/s is what PCSX2 PRODUCES, not what this port loses.
  - The counts are small enough that the guest's own step is 3 counts: at
    96/256 a 1-count report pays out 0 px and holds the remainder in the
    carry, so with a median of 2 the pointer moves in whole pixels only every
    couple of reports.  Distance is not lost -- the carry keeps it -- but that
    is why slow motion reads as reluctance.
  - PS2_EMU_POINTER_SCALE is 1, settled from the emulator's source rather than
    inferred: across 460 reports the largest count was 27, and eight counts
    per host pixel would make that a cursor crawling under 10 px/s, which no
    hand does.  What ptgain still measures is what the source cannot say: what
    one count is worth on this Mac, whose own acceleration curve sits in front
    of every delta PCSX2 reads off QCursor::pos().  The shipped gain stayed at
    96/256 rather than moving to the 768/256 this row once prescribed, because
    the hand's two bounds were taken against counts -- keeping them
    means keeping 96.
  - ptgain's protocol: `ptgain 0`; park the MAC cursor hard against the left
    edge of the Mac's display and slide it in one unbroken stroke to the right
    edge -- the travel is exactly the display's logical width, which System
    Settings states, and it is the one host distance that needs no measuring;
    `ptgain <width>` reads the measured counts-per-host-px next to the
    constant it checks; `ptgain <width> <percent>` sets the gain so the cursor
    moves percent/100 guest px per host px.  It warns when the y totals say
    the sweep was not straight, and caps the gain at 1024/256 because
    dp_ptr_scale() truncates a report at DP_PTR_MAX_PIXELS, so above that the
    top of a fast stroke would be eaten inside the shaper instead of here
    where the row says so.  Its `loop:` column is the pass rate -- the
    pointer's frame rate -- and a low number there is steppiness that no gain
    constant will smooth.
  - [PSTAT] lat: stamps the report that opens an empty queue and reads after
    the flush, so it is queueing PLUS the paint -- the latency the eye
    actually gets, not the one loop: only implies.  A large average with a
    small maximum is a uniformly slow repaint; a large maximum on a small
    average is one stall somewhere else in the loop.
  - Read the RATIO between the paint columns, not the milliseconds: PCSX2 does
    not model EE cycle counts, so absolute timings are the emulator's, while
    the proportion between render, swap and upload is instruction counts --
    and that is what picks the fix.

=====================================================================
PART FIVE -- THE PRESENTATION PLANE: THE EE/GS BACKEND
=====================================================================

  Part Three priced the pixel and Part Four proved that the EE pays that price
  inside its own deadline.  This Part states the consequence: the per-fragment
  work moves to the one programmable rasteriser the console has, and the laws
  that make that arrangement legal on this machine are stated before the code
  that follows them.

14. Why the Graphics Synthesizer, and only it
---------------------------------------------

  LAW 14 (the presentation plane belongs to the engine that owns the pixels).
  Per-fragment work shall run on the Graphics Synthesizer.  The Emotion Engine
  shall keep per-vertex work, state, and the bus.

  WHY  It is not an optimization preference, it is arithmetic from chapter 10.
       The frame the user needs is 16,700 us.  One of the XMB's own draw
       functions, f_bg, costs 29.4 ms of touching pixels -- 1.75x the whole
       budget by itself -- and the entire float layer's measured ceiling,
       every call in it replaced by the fastest software path this port has,
       is 1.51x.  No amount of work on the EE closes a 31x overrun through a
       1.51x door.  The GS is the only hardware rasteriser on this machine, so
       the answer to a per-fragment cost is to stop paying it on the EE.

  MEASURE  chapter 10's price list and chapter 11's attribution, read on the
           same frame: the fill is where the milliseconds are, and under
           PS2_GL=eegs the XMB's draw= goes 176.5 ms -> 74.047 ms with the
           identical sources.

  GATE  the present host suite, 64 checks, and the image's rows 50-56; 5c's
        own result is the user's eyes, because it is the first increment that
        can change what a window looks like (see 22 for why that is a gate and
        not a confession).

  Four facts fix the shape before any code is written, and all four are read
  from this tree rather than from a manual, which is what makes them binding:

  FACT 1  The surface an app renders into is the compositor's blit SOURCE.
          egl_create_window_surface() sets surf->pixels = wnd->dev->pixels
          (src/gl/egl_surface.c:40) and redraw_all_windows_clip() blits
          src = wnd->dev->pixels into g_screen_dev->pixels row by row
          (src/window/wnd.c:539).  The GS cannot write EE memory -- its
          primitives land in its own local VRAM -- so a GS backend cannot keep
          that arrangement.  The GL window's contents stop being an array the
          CPU copies and become something the GS itself composes.  This is the
          one architectural consequence of the change; every law in 15 follows
          from it.

  FACT 2  What the apps actually ask for is a 2D UI, which the GS does
          natively.  xmb.c uses 18 of gl_ops_t's 38 entry points: 2D vertices,
          affine texture coordinates (glTexCoord2f, never a q), per-vertex
          RGBA, GL_QUADS, glBlendFunc, one bound texture per quad, and no
          depth at all.  Gouraud shading is PRIM.IIP, affine texture
          interpolation is TEX0/TEX1 with MMIN/MMAG, and blending against the
          destination already in VRAM is ALPHA with BLEND_COLOR_DEST under
          PRIM.ABE -- all hardware.  glgears.c is the counter-example and
          stays on TinyGL: it needs lighting and display lists, for which the
          GS has no hardware.

  FACT 3  The GS's coordinate window is 12.4 fixed-point around a 2048 offset.
          Vertices are integer-quantised at 1/16 px, which is invisible for a
          768x500 UI, and it is the reason a first increment needs no VU
          program: an EE-side float-to-packet conversion per vertex is enough
          when the vertex count is thousands and the pixel count is 384,000.
          Lane 12 is what a VU would buy once the vertex count matters.  The
          window's extent, in whole pixels relative to the page origin, is
          -2048 .. +2047 -- `gs_vertex_coord16()` adds the offset to `px * 16`
          and the sum goes through a 16-bit unsigned field, so it is asymmetric
          by one and a coordinate past either end folds onto the other side of
          the canvas rather than being clipped.  That is the fact LANE 12f's
          batch gate is made of, and `verify/tests/test_eegs_raster.c` measures
          both ends of it.

  FACT 4  The port already owns the DMA path it needs.  ps2_gs.c builds
          GIFTAGs, primes BITBLTBUF/TRXPOS/TRXREG/TRXDIR and streams DMAC
          channel 2 today.  The primitive path is the same pipe with
          FLG=PACKED and a different REGS list, so nothing here touches the
          driver's blit path and the canvas upload stays as it is.

  LAW 14b (the reference is headers, never source).  This backend is written
  against third_party/ps2sdk/ as a description of hardware only --
  ee/draw/include/*.h, ee/libgs/include/libgs.h, common/include/gif_tags.h,
  common/include/gs_gp.h.  No .c from that tree is linked or copied, in this
  or any later increment.

  WHY  The port's cleanroom law and its single-source law are the same law: a
       borrowed library would be a second model of the GS's field tables, and
       it would drift exactly where drift is invisible on a screen -- one
       field's bit position, one rounding of a clip.

  Selection: a user setting AND a build flag, both naming the same three
  answers.

    OpenGL PS2 : [x] TinyGL   [ ] EE/GS

  following the existing radio-pair idiom (g_wm_style / pmc_set_style(), drawn
  in src/settings/appearance.c:114-115 and hit-tested at :160-171).  The value
  lives with the GL layer, not in the settings file, and in its own TU
  (src/gl/gl_backend_choice.c) because it has three callers on two sides of a
  link boundary -- gl_backend_select()/gl_backend_selected() are the applet's,
  gl_backend_default() is the build's answer, gl_backend_software_default() is
  the rule that used to be the EGL_DEFAULT_BACKEND macro in egl_surface.c (now
  deleted).  There is one of each, which is what stops the three drifting
  apart.

  LAW 14c (a choice nobody made reads as the build's answer).  So the default
  image is byte-for-byte the behaviour this section described before the row
  existed.  LAW 14d (the row is PS2-scoped, and provably so): the row is #if
  defined(BTRON_PS2_TARGET)-scoped, because src/settings/*.c is linked by
  every target including the Pi 400, which links no GL source at all and must
  keep identical behaviour -- verified: the row's three strings are in
  btron-ps2.elf and in zero bytes of btron-posix.  LAW 14e (a choice is
  remembered, not applied): egl_create_window_surface() asks
  gl_backend_selected() at every surface creation (egl_surface.c:38), so it
  takes effect at the NEXT GL window and not under the one on screen;
  gl_resize, egl_make_current and egl_destroy_surface keep branching on
  gl_active_backend() -- what is installed, never what was requested.

  The build dial is the Makefile's PS2_GL=, which had two values and now has
  three: tinygl (the shipped image), virgl (the other software rasteriser,
  kept for the timing comparisons [XMBT] already makes), and eegs, which makes
  the image BOOT on the backend the row offers.  That is the point of it: an
  A/B against the GS pipeline is `make ps2 PS2_GL=eegs`, not a walk to the
  Display applet -- and the applet still outranks the build on all three.
  Splitting the rule in two is what makes the third value expressible at all,
  because a placeholder that answers a GS request with "the default" would, on
  a build dialled to eegs, be a call to itself.

  LAW 14f (an unknown dial value is an error, not a silent tinygl).  WHY: for
  the reason every measurement law in this book gives -- a typo builds an
  image that works, prints timings, and describes a pipeline nobody asked for.
  GATE: the value itself is stamped into .build/ps2-gl.stamp and compared by
  content, with the objects that read it deleted when it disagrees (make
  cannot see a CHANGE of flag; the three objects stay newer than their sources
  and get linked into an image built for the other value).  And the gate must
  be handed the same value as the build -- `make test-ps2 PS2_GL=eegs` --
  because test-ps2 depends on the image and would otherwise rebuild it back to
  tinygl and grade a different artifact.  That is not a hypothetical: it is
  how the first attempt at row 52's control passed while believing it was
  testing the eegs image.  verify/tests/test_gl_backend_choice.c is the other
  half of the same law, one compile per dial value, because an image row can
  only see the value the shipped image was built with, and a flag that
  selected NOTHING looks exactly like a flag that selected tinygl.

  Three rows read the selection law off the image (scripts/test_ps2.sh 50-52):
  gl_init calls tinygl_backend_init twice, once per arm; the same function
  contains no jal to gl_backend_software_default and none to
  gl_backend_default, which are the placeholder's shape and the recursion's;
  and the constant gl_backend_default returns is at most 3, since 3 is what
  PS2_GL=eegs means and the two rows before it are what make the NAME mean
  something rather than the number.  Each was shown to go red: restoring the
  pre-5c placeholder arm fails the first with "1 times"; (GL_BACKEND)7 as the
  default fails the bound with the two above holding.

15. The standing laws of the present path
-----------------------------------------

  5c is the increment that makes this book's Part Five true of the shipped
  image rather than of a plan.  Six laws, each earned by writing its
  consequence down before the code, and each stated here with the consequence
  attached -- because three of the six exist to prevent a failure that has no
  symptom on a console.

  LAW 15a (activation installs the linked pipeline and names itself).
  gl_init()'s GL_BACKEND_EEGS case takes g_gl = &g_tinygl_ops and sets the
  active backend to GL_BACKEND_EEGS: the GS stage hangs on TinyGL's own
  draw_triangle_* hooks, so the CPU rasteriser still builds vertices,
  clipping, textures and state, and only the span loops are replaced.
  CONSEQUENCE: every test that asked gl_active_backend() == GL_BACKEND_TINYGL
  was really asking "is the linked CPU front end live?", and once EEGS is
  active six of them are wrong -- egl_make_current() and egl_destroy_surface()
  would take the virgl arm of a build with no virgl context,
  gl_resize()/gl_shutdown() the same, and gl_fill_stats() would answer a
  caller's question with zeroes.  They now ask one predicate,
  gl_tinygl_pipeline(), true for TINYGL AND EEGS.  A `|| EEGS` written six
  times is six places to forget the seventh.

  LAW 15b (the order on the bus is canvas, environment, primitives -- so the
  stream is staged).  The recorder hands each full group to its sink while the
  application is still drawing, and the composite that drives the drawing runs
  BEFORE ps2_gs_upload().  A sink that went straight to DMAC channel 2 would
  put every triangle into VRAM and then overwrite it with the canvas's stale
  copy of the same rectangle -- stale, because LAW 15c has just stopped the
  blit.  So src/gl/eegs_present.c owns a per-frame staging buffer; the sink
  appends whole groups to it; the environment packet and the staged groups go
  out after the uploads.  The DMA stays one send per group, because a group is
  already a whole GIF packet with its own EOP and NLOOP -- staging changes
  WHEN a group is sent, never HOW.

  GATE  row 55, the bus order as three call-site addresses inside one
        function, whose control is the regression itself: moving the band's
        ps2_gs_upload() below the present block fails 55 ALONE, with 53 and 54
        holding.  That is the evidence row 55 measures an order and not a
        presence.

  CAPACITY, measured rather than modelled: the estimate was about 13,700
  quadwords for an XMB frame; the frame turned out to be 29,787, and the
  counter added for exactly this said so on the first boot -- `drop=42`, the
  stage holding the first nine groups in recorded order and refusing the other
  forty-two, which is "what would have reached the screen was the beginning of
  a frame." EEGS_STAGE_QW_CAP is 65,536 now (1 MiB of .bss, and .bss costs no
  image bytes against chapter 4's budget).  The suite's overflow row counts
  its triangles from the cap rather than from a constant, so raising the cap
  again cannot quietly turn that row into a pass that tests nothing.

  LAW 15c (one layer, and it is a property of the window).  EGL_SURFACE is the
  only object that knows both the WND and which backend answered gl_init(), so
  egl_create_window_surface() marks the window gl_layer when the GS is live.
  redraw_all_windows_clip() then does two different things for that window: it
  still calls its paint -- that is what records the stream -- and it no longer
  blits the client pixmap into the canvas.  The second half has a consequence
  the first does not: the canvas stops holding a picture of the layer AT ALL,
  so any band that presents over the layer's rect must have the layer
  re-recorded underneath it.  Hence the rule in two halves --
  wnd_damage_needs_paint() answers TRUE when a layer window intersects the
  damage (otherwise a banded pass picks the blit_only composite and the layer
  is never re-recorded), and the walk re-runs the layer window's paint even
  when its img_valid is TRUE.  GATE: row 54, both of the layer's facts come
  from the window manager rather than a copy of them (wnd_gl_layer_take,
  wnd_gl_cover_take).

  LAW 15d (what is drawn over the layer is re-uploaded after it, one rect at a
  time, and only by a producer that paints its whole rect opaquely).  The
  panel and its gold bar, the bottom test bars, an open deskbar or tracker
  dropdown and the IME candidate window are composited into the canvas and
  uploaded with the band, which the primitive stream now follows -- so
  wherever the layer's rect overlaps them, the GS buries them.  The compositor
  therefore registers each canvas region it writes after the window walk
  (wnd_gl_cover_add()) and the present re-uploads every registered rect
  intersected with the layer, after the stream.  Two consequences fell out of
  writing that down.  The accumulator cannot be a UNION: the panel is rows
  0-28 and the bars are the last 40, so their union is the whole canvas, and
  re-uploading a union would replace the entire layer with the canvas's stale
  copy of it.  It is a bounded list -- eight entries, with coverdrop= in
  [GSTS] because a dropped rect is a buried overlay with no evidence attached
  -- and the cost is one small BITBLT per producer per frame (the panel is
  5,600 quadwords against a whole-canvas upload's 120,000).  That is also why
  the present does not upload the complement of the layer: those pixels are
  already on the bus.  And a producer may register ONLY what it paints
  opaquely over its whole rect, because the canvas holds no picture of the
  layer to restore.  The pointer sprite does not qualify: it writes its own
  60-odd white and black pixels and leaves the rest of its 16x16 box as the
  canvas had it, so uploading that box over the layer would replace the
  layer's pixels with the desktop background from before it.  Hence the sprite
  is not a cover producer, and the pointer is invisible while it is inside a
  GS window -- the one visible thing 5c does not close, and not a thing the
  cover mechanism can close.  The cure is a primitive, not another canvas
  upload: ALPHA at source-over is (Cs - Cd) * As + Cd, which returns Cd
  untouched wherever the source alpha is 0 and paints Cs wherever it is 255,
  so a 16x16 CT32 plane holding the cursor bitmap and one textured quad at the
  pointer position draw the pointer THROUGH the layer, clipped to it by the
  same SCISSOR -- and outside it the canvas keeps drawing the pointer exactly
  as today.  That is increment 6.

  LAW 15e (the environment's four negotiation words have one answer each, and
  only ALPHA needed a fact).  FRAME names the page the PCRTC already reads --
  page 0, FBW 13, CT32, FMSK 0 -- because a primitive and a blit that disagree
  about where a pixel lives have no way to say so.  ZBUF sits at the last 8 KB
  page of VRAM, above every plane the cursor has handed out, with ZMSK 1 while
  TEST.ZTEN is 0: nothing reads it and nothing writes it, so its only duty is
  not to be someone else's address, and choosing it without the allocator
  leaves increments 3 and 5b's hand literals where they are.  TEST is 0 -- no
  alpha test, no depth test, every fragment passes.  ALPHA is
  gs_pack_alpha(BLEND_COLOR_SOURCE, BLEND_COLOR_DEST, BLEND_ALPHA_SOURCE,
  BLEND_COLOR_DEST, 0), the selects the GS's own equation color = (c1 - c2) *
  a >> 7 + c3 names, which is (Cs - Cd) * As + Cd -- what TinyGL's
  TGL_BLEND_ALPHA_ADD and the XMB's GL_SRC_ALPHA / GL_ONE_MINUS_SRC_ALPHA ask,
  spelled in the hardware's terms.  The field codes are WHY ps2_gs_pack.h
  declined to name them at increment 2: position 3 is an ALPHA select where 0
  means As, while the other three are COLOUR selects where 0 means Cs, so the
  same numeral does not mean the same thing in the four places.  They are
  named in the header now, because a gate pins them -- and the control says
  what for: writing the destination select as Cs instead of Cd costs 2,
  [FAIL] ALPHA is (Cs - Cd) * As + Cd  got=0x...04 want=0x...44.
  CLAMP/TEX0/TEX1 go into the environment packet as zero, because it names no
  plane: eegs_texture() emits the trio as an AD group before any vertex that
  samples, and need_plane() will not let TME be set without one.

  LAW 15f (one GL target, process-wide).  g_gl is a single global
  (gl_dispatch.c:21) and TinyGL's ZBuffer is one static pair, so the image has
  one GL target, "last surface created wins", and no per-window backend choice
  exists to fall back on.  This is a limit of the port, not of the GS; it is
  why a second GL window opened while a layer exists draws into the FIRST
  window's rect, and why glgears stays on the CPU (fact 2).

  What 5c does not close, stated so nobody has to rediscover it: the pointer
  and any overlay that is not an opaque rectangle (increment 6); glClear(),
  which is TinyGL's own framebuffer fill and NOT a primitive, so the layer's
  background is the canvas's pixels showing through wherever a triangle does
  not land -- invisible for the XMB, whose mesh covers all 768x500 of its
  client area (box=0,0..767,499 is that census), and a real gap for an app
  that clears and draws less; LAW 15f's one drawing environment and one frame
  buffer; and D2_QWC, a 12-bit field, while the canvas upload has chunked at
  8,000 quadwords since before this plan existed -- a fact about every
  Host-to-Local transfer on real hardware, found here because 5c had to state
  the bound, and left alone because changing it moves every upload number in
  this book.

16. The increments, and the lanes that follow
---------------------------------------------

  LAW 16 (each increment leaves the image bootable, and each carries its own
  decisive gate).  The evidence for every row -- control runs, failure counts,
  the measurements later withdrawn and why -- is quarantined in
  doc/md/PS2-GS-LOG.md, linked per row.  A number that was retracted is not
  repeated here; it is read there.

    #  increment                                        engine          status
  ---  -----------------------------------------------  --------------  -------
   1   the backend as a user setting AND a build flag,  EE              landed
       both naming the same three answers
   2   the EE-side packet builder: nineteen encoders,   GS              landed
       no bus write, no caller
   3   the drawing environment as one AD-pair stream,   GS              landed
       plus the VRAM cursor
   4   the recorder: packed PRIM, RGBAQ, ST, XYZ2       GS, DMAC ch2    landed
       groups; split rather than drop
   5   presentation on TinyGL's own seam, in three      GS              landed
       steps: 5a the raster hooks, 5b the texture pages, 5c the present
   6   the pointer and the window decorations as GS     GS              open
       sprites over the GS-rendered layer
   7   the geometry kernel on COP1                      EE / COP1       landed
       (src/gl/tinygl/vertex_stage.c)
   8   the wave's lattice on COP1                       EE / COP1       landed
       (src/apps/xmb_wave.c), the same shape one TU
       later

  The gate that says each landed, in one column each: 1 --
  test_gl_backend_choice.c plus the image's dial rows; 2 -- 35 words against
  literals hand-derived from the field table (55 today), the SCISSOR bit-47
  control failing exactly one; 3 -- 59 checks, three controls one per law
  (order, the 12.4 scaling, the 8 KB page unit); 4 -- 139 checks; 5 -- 78
  raster + 103 texture + 64 present checks, the image's rows 44-56, `gs
  planes:` and [GSTS] on the console; 6 -- the user's eyes, because nothing
  headless can certify a z-order; 7 and 8 -- draw= and the counted frame's S=
  column, one instrument and its own attribution.

  LAW 16b (capacity is a latency knob, never a correctness limit).  A full
  recorder buffer is sent and a new group started rather than a vertex dropped
  -- legal because a packed group's NLOOP counts vertices and a GIF transfer
  may carry any number of groups.  The one exception is an op this recorder
  does not know, where the vertex is refused and unsplit= counts it.  So no
  application can overflow the recorder, and what the counters report is DMA
  starts per frame -- which is the number that costs milliseconds.  A stream
  that DROPS a tail is the failure mode designed out, because a partial frame
  with no evidence is exactly the bug that cannot be seen on a screen already
  600 ms behind; and 5c met that failure one layer downstream, in the staging
  buffer rather than the recorder, which is why the drop counter is in both.

  The measured shape of the law, on the shipped frame:

    verts      7,434   = 2,478 primitives x 3 corners, because the seam row 5
                        chose is handed one triangle at a time AFTER clipping
                        (clip.c:271-284) and records its three every time --
                        f_ribbon's 1,828 triangles are 5,484 recorded vertices
                        from its 1,984 mesh ones
    qw         29,853  = 7,434 x 4 + 51 group tags, +66 from the vertices a
                        continued strip re-emits
    groups        51   -- plus the one environment packet, so a frame costs 52
                        DMA starts, not the 4 the shipped 4,096-QW capacity
                        predicts.  The group's SIZE bound is not what closes a
                        group in practice; the STATE change is.
    gate         64 QW -- test-ps2-gs-rec overrides the capacity in its own
                        #define, where a group is 12 vertices in 49 and a frame
                        is 286 starts.  That is the knob, and the reason the gate
                        turns it rather than the code.

  The fourth quadword per vertex is the price of a claim this book does not
  make.  PRIM's texture, blend and fog flags are decremented by the hardware
  as each primitive completes, under PRMODE.MODE/OFFSET, so a strip that sets
  them once loses them from its second triangle on -- how multipass texturing
  is done, and the opposite of what a UI wants.  This recorder re-sends PRIM
  per vertex and claims nothing about PRMODE's layout.  The cheaper answer
  programs PRMODECONT and PRMODE (offsets 0x1A/0x1B, already named in
  ps2_gs_pack.h) into the environment stream once, with the decrement select
  at "no decrement", and drops PRIM from the REGS list: a third of the bus
  traffic, for a field table this book does not yet hold with enough
  confidence to gate.  Recorded as the follow-up it is -- when MODE[3:0],
  OFFSET[6:4], A_MODE[10:8] and CONTEXT[11] are confirmed and pinned by a hand
  literal like every other field here, GS_REC_REGS_N goes 4 -> 3 and the
  per-vertex price goes with it.  INFERRED, and labelled so by R2.

  Two laws make every row above readable, and they bind every increment:

  LAW 16c (a byte scan must be operand-general).  0d010046 is ONE pairing of
  trunc.w.s $f4,$f4; a C cast compiles to trunc.w.s $f6,$f6 = 0x4600318d.  The
  row that shipped as a literal grep was green on an image containing nine of
  the instructions it existed to forbid.  Match the opcode's fixed fields,
  never a whole instruction, and check the pattern against a real compiled
  instance before trusting it.

  LAW 16d (a positive control must be able to turn the row that names it red,
  and the harness must survive proving it).  A gate that cannot go red is not
  an instrument.  Every failure count in the record above is one edit, and the
  row it convicts is named beside it.

  Lanes.  Five agents are named here as structurally open, four of them still
  are, and lane 10 closed on 2026-10-14.  Each is one of LAW 1's missing
  agents rather than a slow subroutine.  They are numbered the way increments
  are, so a console line and a lane can be cited in the same sentence.

  LANE 9  GS compositor token posting -- the presentation plane's producer.
    Close   VIOLATION 1's biggest single share: the 613,440 px blit and the
            480,000 px background restore, both of which are rectangle copies
            the GS does natively -- SPRITE for a fill, Local-to-Local BITBLT
            for a copy.  Nothing in this port blits inside VRAM today.  The
            lane's premise changed on 2026-10-13, so it is restated here
            rather than left back in 10.1.  The copied pixel used to be priced
            at 50.2 ns on the argument that a price flat across three orders
            of size must be the DRAM's; that argument is retired, and what the
            EE charges is 15.7 ns/px for a copy and 8.8 for a fill -- its
            FLOORS, not its bandwidth.  So the GS is being offered a 7.5 ms
            whole-canvas background (480,000 px at the copy floor, which is
            the `bg=` column read off this build) and a 9.6 ms client-area
            copy plus a 14.7 ms opacity scan in front of it (613,440 px at
            15.7 and at 23.9), against the ~821 us the same canvas already
            costs to CROSS.  The lane is still worth taking, and it is no
            longer the only lane: 3.3 ns/px of that scan is an EE-side shave
            that claims no plane, no page and no primitive.
    Budget  The lane has a CAPACITY under it, and the capacity is a row, read
            2026-10-13:

            [BENCH] gs planes: registered=2 pending=0 vram_left=1081344

            Above the canvas -- GS_VRAM_CANVAS_BYTES 1,996,800 B, rounded up
            to 1,998,848 -- the two planes the XMB already own take
            1,114,112 B: 512x512 for the 384x384 atlas, which the GS
            addresses only at powers of two, and 128x128 for the 128x96 font.
            What is left is 132 pages of the allocator's own 8 KB unit
            (GS_PAGE_FRAME, ps2_gs_vram.h:25), and the subtraction is exact in
            that unit: 512 pages in the 4 MiB, 244 for the canvas, 136 for
            those two planes -- 512-244-136 = 132 = 1,081,344 B / 8,192.  One
            of them the Z page wants back.  A second full canvas does not fit,
            and neither does the XMB's own 768x500 client at 1,536,000 B.  So
            the lane is two, and the row that landed left the floor exactly
            where it was read, because it claimed no plane.

            9a LANDED on 2026-10-13 as section 11.5, and it is NOT the row
            this budget planned.  What shipped is a REFUSAL, not a poster: over
            exactly one layer window's client rect the EE's flat body plate
            is not drawn, because the recorded stream is sent on top of it at
            present.  It cost no plane and no primitive, and it took
            6,547 / 6,508 / 6,529 us off the three cycles' frame= --
            112,711 / 110,146 / 109,627 us of total= becoming 106,404 /
            103,713 / 103,251.

            The measurement also settles the rest of the decomposition, and its
            answers cut the other way: frame= IS the whole-body flat fill, and
            it is the only column that moved.  bg= (480,000 px) can
            NEVER be refused -- no stream is ever recorded over the
            background, so nothing lands on top of it, and a poster is the
            only price-cut available there.  panel= and bars= carry text and
            the cursor drawn into the same canvas, so a flat fill is only a
            part of those stages.  blit= (613,440 px) is 9b, and 9b is taken
            only once a page shape is CHOSEN AND PRICED, because the wrong
            guess there is a gs_tex_register() that returns nothing and a
            window drawing with somebody else's texture.  So the lane's open
            half is precisely the fills that have no stream above them --
            which is what this Close cell has said all along.
    Price   The lane's premise is now MEASURED rather than argued, and the
            measurement is of the background, not of the primitive.  A SPRITE
            poster carries one flat colour and no picture -- the background's own
            1,920,000 B do not fit in the 1,081,344 `vram_left=` reports free --
            so the question is how much of `bg=` is one colour, and into how many
            rects the art breaks it up.  The answer comes out of the painted plate
            and not from a second copy of desktop.c's layout constants, because two
            encodings of one truth is the defect this port's own laws name: a GDEV
            copied from the session's screen but pointed at a scratch array makes
            bg_cache_holds() answer yes, so render_desktop_background() hands back
            the cached plate -- the pure background, before any window, deskbar or
            cursor pixel is in it.  ps2_bench_poster_price(), read 2026-10-14 in
            build/ps2-bench.log under the bench image's own dials -- PS2_FP=hw and
            PS2_GL=tinygl, because scripts/ps2_bench.sh calls a bare
            `make ps2-bench` and the dial stamps reset with it:

      [BENCH] poster price: flat=0xff808000 (witness row 0 of 600 rows) flat=448273 px of 480000
      [BENCH] poster price: posterisable=433001 px  ee keeps=46999 px  trapped=15272 px (inside an art row's box)
      [BENCH] poster price: rows flat=143 art=457  rects=119  bands=13  art bbox=46999 px
      [BENCH] poster price: at the 15.7 ns/px copy floor the poster takes 6798 us off one whole-canvas bg= pass
      [BENCH] poster price: control, same scan told to believe 0xff808001 -- posterisable=0 px (a broken match would print the same big number)

            433,001 px of 480,000 -- 90.2% -- is one colour, 0xff808000, the
            desktop's teal, and the control says the scan really is matching
            pixels: ask the identical loop to believe a colour one bit off and
            the posterisable set collapses to 0.  At this port's copy floor the
            433,001 px are worth 6,798 us, and the same build's same run bills
            the whole-canvas pass at `bg=7483(480000 px)` -- 15.59 ns/px -- so
            the poster is offered 90.8% of that column.  The figure is an UPPER
            bound and is labelled here as one: the plate has no window, no
            deskbar and no pointer on it, and a real present's set is this minus
            every rect a canvas-carried foreground occupies.  What the bound
            still settles is the SHAPE, and it settles it against the simpler
            candidate decisively.  The 143 fully flat rows are 114,400 px, worth
            1,796 us; the general shape adds the 318,601 px of side runs that sit
            outside an art row's bounding box, so it collects 3.8x what the
            13-band shape -- whole flat rows only -- would.  The emitter has to
            take per-row spans, and `trapped=15272` is the flat area that
            bounding the row by its own art gives back.

            `rects=119` is also why this is ONE packet and not 119: 119
            channel-2 starts would spend the saving on queueing.  Batched, 119
            packed SPRITE groups are 477 quadwords, 7,632 B, which is 0.4% of the
            1,920,000 B a whole-canvas upload already crosses -- `w * h / 4`
            quadwords at ps2_gs.c:267, not the 1,996,800 B the padded 832-px VRAM
            page occupies.  One redundancy in the row set is stated rather than
            left to be found: `ee keeps=` and `art bbox=` are the SAME number by
            construction, because a row's posterisable px are `w - (x1-x0+1)`, so
            the two columns are one fact printed twice.  They are kept as the pair
            because the equality is the scan's own arithmetic check -- the
            instrument's comment now says so, so a reader is not owed a second
            measurement by it.

            9b LANDED PARTLY on 2026-10-14: the EMITTER, not the caller.
            src/drivers/ps2/ps2_gs_poster.{c,h} builds that one batch -- a packed
            SPRITE group per rect, PRIM/RGBAQ/XYZ2/XYZ2, NLOOP back-patched when
            the count is final in the recorder's own idiom -- and owns no register
            write, no DMA and no knowledge of windows.  It is host-gated and NOT
            in PS2_SRCS, because nothing in the image calls it and ps2.ld ASSERTs
            `_end <= 0x01FF0000`: an unreferenced TU would buy image bytes for a
            saving that has not been made yet.  verify/tests/test_ps2_gs_poster.c
            carries 56 checks (`make test-ps2-gs-poster`) on the three things no
            screen can show and the code cannot derive: the colour word (a poster
            read in the desktop's byte order paints teal as brown at an identical
            packet length), the corner word (diffed against gs_vertex_coord16()
            over every coordinate the 16-bit field holds, so the integer shift is
            shown to be the recorder's encoding and not a rival one), and NLOOP
            (which must shrink with the rects a refusal removes, or the DMA's QWC
            is shorter than the tag and the GIF reads the overflow as a packet of
            its own).  Two mutations were run to show the gate can go red: the
            page offset to 0x4000 -- 10 failures, the differential among them --
            and R/B swapped in the colour read -- 4 failures, the 0x12345678
            witness among them.  Both restored, 56/0.  What has NOT landed is the
            caller, and the six rows below are why it does not land rather than a
            note that it is still owed.

            The bound is of a PLATE, and the lane's real question is what a
            present's canvas holds.  ps2_bench_poster_price() now scans BOTH --
            the cached plate and the live canvas -- and times the cheapest
            decision that could possibly be made about one: one compare per
            pixel, no rect bookkeeping, no packet built.  Read 2026-10-14 in the
            same log and the same run, a second after the plate rows and at 4.8
            s of emulator time, on the just-launched workbench with its windows
            up:

      [BENCH] poster census: the cheapest possible decision, one compare per pixel, costs 11498 us = 23 ns/px and it counts 76318 flat px per pass
      [BENCH] poster census (live canvas): posterisable=57923 px  ee keeps=422077 px  trapped=18395 px  flat=76318 px of 480000
      [BENCH] poster census (live canvas): rows flat=24 art=576  rects=113  bands=4  art bbox=422077 px  pointer at (460,280)
      [BENCH] poster census: the plate offers 433001 px and the canvas 57923 px -- 375078 px is foreground the canvas carries and the cached plate does not, and that is the exclusion set counted rather than enumerated
      [BENCH] poster census: control, same scan told to believe 0xff808001 -- posterisable=0 px
      [BENCH] poster census: deciding costs 53935 us for the 480000 px of one canvas = 112.36 ns/px -- the scan a caller pays to learn which runs are flat -- and the poster takes 6798 us off bg=, so the net is MINUS 47137 us

            Three things follow, and the first closes the half on its own.
            DECIDING COSTS MORE THAN THE CANVAS IS WORTH: 11,498 us to compare
            every pixel exactly once, against a `bg=` this same build bills at
            7,499 us for those same 480,000 px.  Break-even therefore wants
            11,498 / 15.7 = 732,000 posterisable px out of a canvas that holds
            480,000, so NO per-pass census can pay whatever the desktop is
            showing -- and 23 ns/px is a LOWER bound on a caller rather than an
            estimate of one, because a caller also builds the packet and splits
            the canvas upload around its own holes.  The instrument's real scan,
            the one that returns spans a caller could post, costs 53,935 us
            (112.36 ns/px) and prints its own verdict: MINUS 47,137 us against
            the plate's upper bound.

            Second, the premise this cell used to hand the caller is answered,
            and it is cheaper than expected: matching the flat colour IS the
            exclusion.  The pointer's 16x16 box, a window's body and every glyph
            hold a word that is not 0xff808000, so they leave the posterisable
            set by themselves and no rect list is needed to stay correct -- and
            `375078 px is foreground the canvas carries and the cached plate
            does not` is that exclusion counted rather than enumerated.  The
            same row prices what is actually on offer at present: 57,923 px, not
            the plate's 433,001 -- 909 us at the copy floor, 12.1% of that `bg=`
            and 2.2% of the `comp=41008` the same compositor row totals.  The
            other half of the finding is the failure mode the shortcut would
            have had: a caller that skipped the census and posted the plate's
            cached 119 rects would write teal wherever the list says flat, and
            the corruption set that would bound is the same 375,078 px, read
            from the other side.

            Third, the byte-order question this lane left open is closed by
            measurement and not by the law.  Asked whether the canvas is the
            same picture with R and B exchanged, the scan counts the swapped
            word and gets zero:

      [BENCH] poster census: canvas[0]=0xffc8d0d4 plate[0]=0xff808000  differs=375095 px of 480000
      [BENCH] poster census: the canvas holds the R/B-swapped plate word 0xff008080 in 0 px -- its centre is 0xcc000000 where the plate's centre is 0xff808000
      [BENCH] poster census: pixels whose RGB differs from the plate's: 374925 of 480000 -- so of the 375095 words that differ by all 32 bits, 170 differ ONLY in the alpha byte
      [BENCH] poster census: the canvas's OWN colour 0xffc8d0d4 gives posterisable=14705 px flat=84922 px witness row 0 rects=18 -- if this is the big number and differs is one too, the plate and the canvas are the same picture in two orders

            So 2.1's order holds on this path as it holds everywhere -- those
            170 alpha-only words are why the all-32-bit count and the RGB count
            are two numbers -- and what differs is the SCENE.  The workbench's
            own modal word is 0xffc8d0d4 and it is flat over only 84,922 px in
            18 rects: a live canvas is a busy picture, not a teal field with
            something drawn on it.  The census's own control, `posterisable=0`
            at 0xff808001, says the live scan matches pixels the way the plate
            scan does, so none of the four numbers above is a broken comparator.

            VERDICT, written as a closure and not as a to-do: LANE 9b's CALLER
            IS NOT TAKEN.  The emitter stands exactly as it landed -- host-gated,
            out of `PS2_SRCS`, no image byte spent -- because the only caller
            these rows can price is a census that costs 1.53x the column it is
            meant to reduce, and the only bound that clears that cost belongs to
            a plate no present ever shows.  What would reopen the half is not a
            better poster but a decision that costs nothing: a flat-area set
            handed to the caller by the compositor's own rect bookkeeping instead
            of read out of the canvas.  That door was already taken; it is 11.5's
            refusal, landed, and it is why `frame=` fell while `bg=` did not.

            9b's caller died on the COST OF DECIDING, and that is the reason a
            different question was worth asking: not WHICH PIXELS a poster could
            paint, but WHETHER THE ARRAY CHANGED AT ALL.  The second question has
            an answer the compositor does not have to scan for -- damage is
            already booked as bands -- so it was priced before anything was
            written.  ps2_bench_canvas_census() hashes every row of the array
            ps2_gs_set_framebuffer() installed and ps2_gs_upload() therefore
            sends, and diffs the hashes against the previous pass.  Read under
            the SHIPPING dials, PS2_GL=eegs PS2_FP=hw PS2_MSX=0, over three
            consecutive app frames of the workbench with the wave live -- the
            wave's own [XMBT] wave rows step n=4, n=5, n=6 inside the window
            these three passes cover:

      [BENCH] canvas census pass 4: rows=600 changed=0 span=-1..-1 px<=0 of 480000 fp=a3f484d0 cost=14796 us
      [BENCH] canvas census pass 5: rows=600 changed=0 span=-1..-1 px<=0 of 480000 fp=a3f484d0 cost=14867 us
      [BENCH] canvas census pass 6: rows=600 changed=0 span=-1..-1 px<=0 of 480000 fp=a3f484d0 cost=14831 us

            One fingerprint three times, while the same three passes' cycle rows
            each present px=480000 nb=1 full=1: 27,989 us of canvas rebuilt and
            4,757 us of bytes re-sent for an array that did not move.  Read this
            as what it is and not as the poster's bound come back -- the flat
            census offered 57,923 px of 480,000 and died on its decision price;
            this row says nothing about which px could be painted flat and
            everything about whether the upload had to happen.  Why it did not:
            under eegs the app's picture never enters the canvas (the same run's
            [BENCH] xmb surface frame 3 reads 768x500 black=384000 other=0), and
            11.5's refusal already declines to copy that surface, so the array is
            a desktop plate with a GS stream laid over it -- plate=covered, in
            the recorder's own words, and the frame partition above shows the
            wave's own 46,864 us living entirely outside the canvas.

            Three caveats, before the verdict and not after it.  (i) cost= is
            14,796 us, 30.8 ns/px -- almost twice the bg= column it audits, and
            on 9b's own arithmetic a scan this wide is a MEASUREMENT and never a
            shipped decision; the instrument stays behind BTRON_PS2_BENCH for
            exactly that reason.  (ii) Three samples span ~0.2 s of a session, so
            what is excluded is a PER-FRAME change -- the only kind a waving
            bank causes -- and a once-a-second clock row or a menu tween is not
            excluded by these rows.  (iii) `changed=` counts rows and `px<=` is
            an UPPER bound, one pixel and a whole row hashing to the same
            difference; the claim this frame needs is the stronger one, that the
            fingerprint itself repeated.

            So 9d is opened, and what it is: a present stops rebuilding and
            stops transferring the canvas region a layer stream provably
            covers.  The decision is not taken from the pixels -- it is the
            layer window's own client rect, which wnd.c can answer BEFORE the
            composite runs (the same arithmetic the walk uses at its own
            `s_gl_client`, and therefore one encoding of it, not a second), plus
            the premise 11.5 already gates on: that the rect is the one this
            pass's stream scissors.  What it refuses is the band minus that
            rect, as strips, so every stage the compositor already clips to a
            rect -- the plate blit, the window walk, the upload -- is simply
            handed a smaller rect.  Two facts bound the saving and both are
            rows, not guesses.  (i) The cover list: canvas content that must
            STAY visible over the stream -- the panel's clock rows, the bars,
            an open menu -- is re-uploaded after it, so the hole cannot cover
            what a cover rect covers, and this frame's [GSTS] row says cover=4.
            (ii) The hole moves: when the layer's rect shrinks or the layer
            closes, the canvas under it has not been drawn for as long as the
            hole stood, so the refusal is bounded by the INTERSECTION of this
            pass's hole and the last one's, and a pass with no hole is a full
            pass.  Neither fact is priced yet, so 9d's bound is gross: 36,154
            us of a 90,534 us pass, and the row that turns it into a net is the
            cover map printed under this instrument.  Geometry's 37,619 us at
            LANE 12 is the other 42% of the same pass, and the arithmetic of the
            port's own bar -- a 16,667 us deadline against a 106,544 us period,
            of which 16,001 is the hold -- is that 9d and 12 together are the 5x
            (90,534 minus 36,154 minus 37,619 leaves 16,761 us).  Neither lane
            is reportable as the win alone.  That subtraction is the arithmetic
            of the pass as it read that day, and 12f's landing cell re-reads it
            on the pass as it reads now -- geometry is 24,343 us rather than
            37,619, 9d's own terms are 24,823 rather than 36,154, and the
            residue neither of them can refuse is 18,768 us, which is 4.5x and
            not 6.4x: after 12f the bar lives in `frame=` and in the composite's
            unnamed 9,011 us, and naming those two is this lane's next rung, not
            LANE 12's.
    Gate    A refusal's gate is its premise, not its saving: the refused set
            must EQUAL the rect the stream covers, checked from both sides.
            On the host that is 11.5's four-canvas assertion -- the plate's
            hole, the window's client rect and the stream's clip as one
            rectangle, a 32x32 box reporting the whole rect while writing only
            its own px, and a grip drawn inside the hole still drawn.  In the
            image it is [GSTS] plate=covered with b0= reaching all four edges
            of clip=, [GSPLATE] silent, drop=0 and coverdrop=0, body= + skip=
            = the unrefused total, vram_left=1081344 HELD, and frame= falling
            in [XMBT] while every other column stays inside its own
            cycle-to-cycle spread.  A poster's gate is now a number rather
            than a direction: every pixel it takes off the EE's hands is worth
            15.7 ns of `bg=` or 39.9 of `blit=` -- 23.9 ns of that second
            figure being the scan the walk runs before it copies, which a
            poster removes along with the copy (10.1) -- against the ~1.7
            ns/px the same pixel costs to CROSS channel 2 (1.5).  So the test
            is that `bg=` or `blit=` falls by the posterised area at that
            rate, that groups= grows in [GSTS], and that a canvas census
            proves the canvas still paints.  Both columns are read in ns/px
            and not in ms, so a poster that wins on a large rect cannot hide a
            loss on the rate.  That gate has never been run, and the census is
            why: a lane does not get an image test until its pre-gate says the
            saving is collectible.  The rows above stand as the receipt for
            whoever reopens the half from the compositor's rect list instead of
            from the canvas.

            A poster's premise, ANSWERED twice -- once by reading the code and
            once by measuring the canvas.  There is ONE canvas and the pointer
            is drawn INTO it: init_baremetal_desktop() is handed
            s_desktop_backbuffer at core_ps2.c:6486, the GS is given that same
            array (ps2_gs_set_framebuffer(), core_ps2.c:6875), ps2_fb() returns
            exactly it (ps2_gs.c:59-62), and draw_baremetal_mouse_cursor() at
            core_ps2.c:1217 passes screen->pixels into
            draw_baremetal_cursor_raw() at src/desktop/desktop.c:348-350 -- so
            the cursor's white and black 16x16 box lands in the very bytes the
            GIF upload reads.  What the census settles is that no rect list is
            needed to be SAFE with that fact: a caller whose set is "the pixels
            equal to this colour" cannot erase the pointer, a window's body or
            a glyph, because none of them is that colour -- the exclusion IS
            the match.  A list would only be needed by the other caller, the
            one that reuses the plate's cached rects without looking, and the
            same rows price that one out.  The dead twin found while checking
            this stays on the record: ps2_gs.c:662 ps2_gs_draw_cursor() saves
            and repaints a 16x16 box through ps2_fb() in the driver's own shape
            and has NO caller in src/, so anyone re-deriving the pointer path
            must gate on the live one above, not on that function's idea of
            where the cursor is.

            Two more constraints were written down for the caller before the
            census closed it, and they are kept because a caller rebuilt from a
            rect list inherits both.  The order a poster would go into is fixed
            by ps2_paint_region(): the canvas upload at core_ps2.c:1256, then
            the layer's env and the app's recorded stream at 1278, then the
            cover re-uploads at 1299 -- so a batch sits between the first and
            the second, clipped to the band actually uploaded, re-issued by
            every present whose rect intersects it, because the band's rows are
            the only ones the GS has been given since the poster last went.
            And LANE 10's buffer-lifetime law (ps2_gif_ring.h, restated at
            11.6) admits a posting producer only if its buffer is rewritten
            after the reap at eegs_present_begin(), so a poster wants its OWN
            static batch, rewritten per pass, exactly like ps2_gs.c:743's
            env_pkt and for the reason that comment gives (727-733).  It cannot
            borrow the environment either: ps2_gs_draw_env_apply() posts
            without reaping, so a second apply before the next begin() would
            overwrite a packet still on the bus.  One cost this book has NOT
            priced, and it is why a rect-derived caller is not automatically
            the winner either: skipping interior spans means SPLITTING the
            canvas upload, and no row here measures the per-span start of a
            split IMAGE transfer -- 10.1's `blit scan` is the nearest number
            and it is per pixel, not per start.
    Shave   The refutation left one term of LANE 9 standing, and it is the
            term 10.1 prices twice: `blit=` reads a band to DECIDE whether it
            may copy it, then reads the same band again to copy it.  39.86
            ns/px is that pair -- the copy floor's 15.96 plus 23.9 of
            decision -- and `blit wide` at 36.57 shaves 3.3 of the 23.9 by
            taking two pixels per load, which leaves the SECOND READ of the
            same bytes entirely intact.  LANDED 2026-10-14 as row 9c, in
            include/btron/fused_row.h: the walk reads each pixel once now, and
            the read it removed was worth 18.9 of those 23.9.

            The rungs, priced on the EE at a 400x16 rect, every one of them
            `chk=ok`, the last being the shipped function rather than a
            lookalike of it: the 64-bit series 33.33 at 2 px, 26.39 at 4,
            23.04 at 8; the 32-bit word series 25.52 at 4, 22.51 at 8, 21.09
            at 16; and the header body 20.95 -- against `blit scan`'s 39.84
            and `blit wide`'s 36.58.
            The ceiling was arithmetic before it was a number, because a fused
            pass cannot cost less than the copy it replaces, and this row
            lands 4.99 ns/px above the 15.96 floor: that much is the decision
            and none of it is memory.  Two shapes were measured and refused.
            The 64-bit pairs, because `f4` at 26.39 LOST to the phase-free
            `f4w` at 25.52 -- so nothing is paid for the portability, and a
            pair access would have been a promise about the window's left edge
            that this walk cannot make.  And the bit-test decision, because
            `(w-1) & ~w & 0x80000000` per pixel reads 33.28 clean and 40.57
            dirty, the worst row in the sweep -- worse than the two-pass
            shape it replaced.  Arithmetic does not substitute for the
            compare.  The rung that ships is the fastest measured and not
            the widest written, which is 16 pixels to one decision.

            Two contents, both priced, because they disagree about the ladder.
            The all-opaque band is the regime production lives in --
            `blit=24481(613440 px)` is 39.91 ns/px, the `blit scan` shape
            read off a real walk -- and the fused row takes that same pass
            to `blit=12665(613440 px)`, 20.65 ns/px, 1.93x.  The pass keeps
            `bg=7484(480000 px)`, 15.6 ns/px against this book's own 7,490
            row, because a background restore is a plain copy with no
            decision in it and this lane does not touch it.
            A band carrying the transparent sentinel in one pixel of eight
            reorders everything: the two-pass walk gets CHEAPER there, 30.38,
            because it stops scanning at the first sentinel and never pays
            for the copy, while a fused group of 16 fails its decision on
            every group and pays the fixup -- and it still wins, at 22.40,
            where 8 px lands 25.52 and 4 px 29.34.  So the fixup over a
            failed group is per-pixel and works from the values already
            loaded, because on a transparent window that path IS the row and
            not an error path.  Odd column phase: `blit o scan` 40.17
            against `blit o ship` 21.32.  The 800-px sweep: 39.53 against
            20.46.

            The predicate shipped as a port dial rather than a runtime test.
            This walk is shared code -- src/window/wnd.c composes the desktop
            for every port in the tree, and the standing guardrail is that the
            Pi 400's behaviour does not move -- so the header holds BOTH
            shapes, the scan-then-copy row verbatim including the
            btron_row_blit() it calls, and selects on BTRON_PS2_TARGET.
            aarch64 keeps the two-pass row because its copy is a 64-byte NEON
            loop and a per-pixel decision inside it would take the benefit
            away.  The gate is verify/tests/test_ps2_fused_row.c, run twice
            per `make test` so each side of the dial is held to the scalar
            rule: byte-identical canvas over 45 span lengths at both
            relative buffer phases and four sentinel layouts, every single
            sentinel column of a 34-px row, the first and last pixel of a
            row, 5,275 checks, and two controls that must go red -- a group
            stored without being tested, and a row one pixel short.  The
            bench's own fold cannot be that gate: it samples every 64th
            destination pixel while the holes sit at column 3 mod 8, so
            every `h` row printed `chk=ok` with the sentinel columns
            unchecked.  What is NOT taken: the 4.99 ns/px of decision
            between 20.95 and the copy floor, which a wider group or a
            branchless store has to price before anyone calls this residue
            collected, and the Pi 400, whose row this change does not touch.

  LANE 10  a GIF writer ring -- the sender stops blocking.  LANDED 2026-10-14
           as 11.6, in src/drivers/ps2/ps2_gif_ring.{c,h}.
    Close   VIOLATION 2, ps2_gs_gif_send()'s `while (D2_CHCR & (1 << 8)) ;`.
            The law is not "be faster", it is S_FULL: a producer must not be
            stopped by its consumer, so the ring needs a bounded capacity, a
            drop count, and a completion check at the NEXT yield rather than
            a spin at this one.  drop= and coverdrop= already proved this
            port knew the shape of a §3.2 receipt; what had no instrument was
            the sender's own blocked time, and 11.6 is that instrument.  The
            lane closed as a SHAPE and not as a zero -- post/drain/reap with
            the reap at eegs_present_begin(), a capacity derived from the
            group table so no legal frame can reach it, a refusal counted
            twice (the ring's own drop=, and the stage's column that says
            words did not cross), and a [GSTS] row for the waiting.  The
            canvas upload still blocks, because its buffer is the canvas and
            every console character writes it, and the row now prints what
