            account for.  If `rst=` comes back far above that, the residue was
            never the overlay block and this paragraph is where the reading gets
            corrected.  The column costs two clock reads and a store on every
            target that composites an overlay, and it is not behind a dial, the
            way the rest of the cycle row is not.

            What the named block refuses, and the reading settles the path today:
            the open menu is rasterised TWICE.  `global_menu_is_open()` is
            `(g_gmenu.active_menu >= 0) || tracker_is_menu_open()`
            (src/desktop/global_menu.c:241), and `global_menu_render_overlay()`
            opens with `if (g_gmenu.active_menu == GMENU_HDR_BTRON ||
            tracker_is_menu_open()) { tracker_render_menu(dev); return; }`
            (:406) -- so with the launcher's list up and no header dropdown open,
            which is the state the wave is in, `workbench_render()`'s own
            `if (tracker_is_menu_open()) tracker_render_menu(screen);` (:93)
            draws the SAME plate a second time.  The two `why=` bits are not two
            facts either: bit 4 is derived from bit 8 in exactly that state,
            which is why a steady XMB frame reads `why=c` as though a dropdown
            were open when none is, and why the first run of this instrument
            "returned `why=4` on every steady frame".  PROVEN as a path -- the two
            conditions that make the pair of calls are the two bits the row
            carries, and `ps2_gui_pass()`'s `if (force_full || set.overflow ||
            ov) ps2_bandset_full(&set)` (src/cores/core_ps2.c:6368) is what makes
            them a whole-canvas pass.  Every write on that path is an opaque
            store -- `app_menu_draw_3d_bevel_box()` fills and lines, and
            `render_tc_string()` stores glyph pixels straight into the canvas --
            so the second pass cannot change a pixel, which is rung (ii)'s gate:
            a host differential holding the composited canvas byte-for-byte, and
            in the image `ovl=` halving with `rst=` unchanged.

            Two rungs this cell exposes and does not take.  (a) `why=`'s bits 4
            and 8 are not costs but RULES: an open menu makes the pass present
            the whole canvas, so the overlay block rides on a composite a
            hover-only change does not need -- that is 9d's continuation, not
            this cell's.  (b) The cover list's 3.7 ms is the menu's rect
            re-uploaded every frame, and 12h measured the analogous question on
            the stream side and REFUTED the cheap answer there; the cover side is
            unpriced.

            MEASURED, on the shipped dials, one bench run, three partition rows.
            The seventh column printed and the identity closed: `why=d` gives
            render 202,519 total 193,571 ovl=9,060 rst=-112; `why=c` gives
            199,642 / 190,647 / ovl=8,968 / rst=27; `why=1` -- the no-overlay
            frame -- gives 190,145 / 190,095 / ovl=24 / rst=26.  PROVEN: the
            residue IS the overlay block.  It appears whole in `ovl=` on both
            open-menu rows, within 1% of the 9,011 us the pairing inferred, and
            vanishes (24 us, the bracket's own cost) on the row with no menu;
            `rst=` then reads -112..27 us, inside the 88-219 us band the old
            residue left, the negative sign being the cross-pass artifact the
            print's comment names -- `render=` is summed per band while `total=`
            and `ovl=` are per-call maxima, so the identity is exact only at
            nb=1 and its slack is that band.  Rung (ii)'s share was that
            remainder, and its gate has now been run: the landed rows read
            `why=d` ovl=4,550 rst=11 and `why=c` ovl=4,491 rst=65 -- exactly
            half the 8,968-9,060 above, `rst=` inside the band, `why=1`'s
            ovl=24 unchanged.  The verdict and the three parts of the gate are
            the next paragraph; the visual half stays the user's.

            RUNG (ii), the shape being landed.  The two calls collapse to a
            dispatch, at BOTH sites in src/desktop/workbench.c (the whole-canvas
            `workbench_render()` and the Pi 400's `workbench_render_overlay_only()`):

              if (global_menu_is_open()) global_menu_render_overlay(screen);
              else                       tracker_render_menu(screen);

            The else arm is not dead code and it is not a second drawing rule: it
            is a measured no-op.  `global_menu_is_open()` is the disjunction
            `active_menu >= 0 || tracker_is_menu_open()`
            (src/desktop/global_menu.c), so there is NO state with the tracker
            open and the getter FALSE -- the only state that reaches the else arm
            is nothing open at all, and there `tracker_render_menu()` returns at
            its own state guard (src/desktop/tracker.c: `state !=
            TRACKER_STATE_OPEN`) before touching the device.  The harness proves
            exactly that: in the nothing-open state the retired block makes zero
            calls, the dispatch makes one, and the two canvases are still
            byte-for-byte identical.  It stands as the unit restatement of the
            delegation: the overlay renderer owns the plate whenever the global
            getter answers, and only the renderer's own guard answers otherwise.  What makes the merge
            lossless is the decision table, and it is byte-exact by state:
            tracker open (whatever `active_menu` is) drew the plate twice before
            and draws it once now -- the second call was a pure repeat; BTRON
            header with the tracker closed draws it once, unchanged; any other
            dropdown is a single `global_menu_render_overlay()`, unchanged;
            nothing open is zero writes in both shapes, though they spend it
            differently -- the retired block asks two state questions and calls
            nothing, the dispatch makes one guarded call the renderer itself
            refuses (1 against 0 under the rename), and the byte-for-byte canvas
            is what turns that extra call from a risk into a measurement.  The
            gate has three parts and each can fail independently.  (1) A host differential
            in verify/tests/test_desktop_clip.c -- which is why that suite now
            links `workbench.c` and `global_menu.c` and why its local
            `global_menu_render_bar()` stub is gone: the two block shapes run
            against the same menu states and the canvases must compare
            byte-for-byte.  (2) The census of the plate rect: take the canvas
            after the block, then call `tracker_render_menu()` once more and
            count the pixels that change -- zero proves every write on the path
            is opaque, which is the book's claim this rung converts to a
            measurement.  The call COUNT is taken by compiling the harness,
            `global_menu.c` and `workbench.c` with
            `-Dtracker_render_menu=counted_tracker_render_menu` while `tracker.c`
            and the wrapper's own TU
            (verify/tests/test_desktop_clip_count_port.c) compile WITHOUT it:
            every call site then routes through the counting wrapper, the
            definition keeps its real name, and the wrapper calls it.  The
            shipped TU is untouched and the two block shapes, written out in the
            harness under the rename, must count two in the tracker-open state
            before and exactly one after.  A normal build routes nothing through
            the wrapper, and there those rows say SKIP -- 0==0 is not a gate.  The census says the stronger thing on
            the merged form only: one call after the block changes zero pixels,
            so the refused call could never have changed one.  (3) In the image,
            `ovl=` must land near half its 8,968-9,060 values with `rst=` inside
            the same band, and the `why=c`/`why=d` rows must print identically --
            one plate, not two.  What the host cannot decide is the look: whether
            the launcher's list still paints its hover highlight is the user's
            observation, and until it is given this rung is SKIP for the visual
            half, not PASS.

            LANDED, 2026-10-15, on that dispatch, all three parts run.  (1) The
            differential holds: all four menu states print byte-identical
            canvases between the two shapes, in the rename build AND in the
            plain one -- nothing-open, tracker-open, BTRON+tracker, dropdown.
            (2) The census says a further tracker call changes zero pixels in
            the two states that have a plate, and the counts decide only under
            the rename: 2 -> 1 with the tracker open, 0 -> 1 (the no-op arm,
            pixel-identical by part (1)) with nothing open, 0 -> 0 for the bare
            dropdown; the plain build prints SKIP on those rows instead of
            letting 0==0 pass.  The suite builds its renamed TUs in a private
            `.clip.o' object namespace -- the plain `.test.o' objects are shared
            with every other suite, and a renamed `global_menu.test.o' leaks
            `counted_tracker_render_menu' into links that have no wrapper.
            (3) The image: `why=d` ovl=4,550 rst=11, `why=c` ovl=4,491 rst=65,
            `why=1` ovl=24 rst=65 on one bench run at the shipped dials -- half
            the block refused, the identity closing to the microsecond at nb=1.
            `make test` exits 0, `make test-ps2` 95/95, `make arm64-elf` builds,
            uefi and pc98 link.  OPEN remains the look: hover highlight intact
            is the user's observation, and until it is given the visual half of
            this rung is SKIP, not PASS.

  LANE 13  IOP / SIF as the I/O actor.
    Close   plane 1 and plane 2 living entirely on the EE: the SPU2 has no
            driver here, and CDVD and the memory card are polled by the
            same core that composites.  The IOP is a second programmable
            component with its own bus, which makes it exactly the sort of
            thing LAW 1 says must have an agent or be counted as EE work.

  LANE 14  the full-feature disc -- two volumes and the app set, shipped as
            CD9660, with the image binary frozen.  The user's ask, 2026-10-15:
            ship EVERY B-System application POSIX has -- Terminal, `sc`, `tv`,
            Quake, Lil Cu 64, Cassette (the audio deck), Clarity, the chokanji
            converters, the HMI, the drive setup -- plus BOTH volumes,
            btron_sys.vol and btron_anders.vol, onto one bootable PS2 disc.
            The user's constraint, same day, verbatim: "dont need to write
            image binary i think image binary can be like now" -- so the
            artifact this lane ships is the DISC, and the ELF that boots it is
            not grown by it.  That constraint is load-bearing, not cosmetic.
            `ps2.ld` ASSERTs `_end <= 0x01FF0000` and the live image reads
            `_end = 0x01C52DD0`: 3,789,360 B of headroom in a 32 MB RDRAM
            machine.  The POSIX app set is not free -- `$(QUAKE_SRCS)` alone
            carries a 64 MiB hunk arena, `$(DEMO_SRCS)` a 3 MiB Lil node
            table, and both are deliberately OUT of `PS2_GL_SRCS` for exactly
            that reason (the 2026-10-09 input-dead incident).  So a literal
            "link everything into the ELF" is the one move the budget forbids,
            and the budget is what the user's constraint is pointing at.

            The three partitions of the ask, measured against this repo.
            (i) Code that is ALREADY in the image.  Terminal/gterm, `sc`, `tv`,
            the CLU shell, the tracker launcher, the desktop and the XMB are in
            `PS2_SRCS` today (`src/clu/*`, `src/window/*`, `src/desktop/*`).
            Nothing ships for them -- they are reached with the D-pad now.
            (ii) Code POSIX has and PS2 does NOT link: Clarity (4 units), the
            chokanji family (8), the HMI (7), and eight singles -- b_drivesetup,
            image_decode, dp_sdl, font_mgr, blk_file, blk_part, blk_qcow2,
            desktop/main.c.  Twenty-seven units in all, each path checked to
            exist in the tree.  These are small and
            RAM-cheap; linking them is a headroom decision, not a disc one, and
            it violates the frozen-image constraint.  PARKED under the user's
            own word -- ship as-is -- and named here so the list is one grep,
            not a re-derivation.  (iii) Data, which is what a CD9660 disc is
            FOR, and which the disc can carry with zero bytes added to the ELF:
            btron_sys.vol (1,048,576 B) and btron_anders.vol (1,048,576 B) as
            files, and `assets/quake` -- pak0.pak at 18,689,235 B and the eight
            `drone1mN.dem` demos.  A 32 MB disc holds all of it; a 32 MB EE
            cannot hold the pak in one buffer, so pak0 is a STREAM read off the
            CD, not an embed.

            The law this lane lands, rungs 1-2, and what it does NOT claim.
            Rung 1 is packaging: `make ps2` writes a disc whose tree is
            `SYSTEM.CNF`, `BTRON.ELF`, `SYS.VOL`, `ANDERS.VOL`, and a `QUAKE`
            drawer with `PAK0.PAK` and the demos.  That is a Makefile change
            and the volumes/quake assets it stages -- `btron-ps2.elf` is built
            by the SAME dialled command as before, and the rung's falsifying
            gate is that the PACKAGING writes no image bytes: delete the ISO,
            rebuild the ISO ALONE, and the ELF's mtime and hash must not move.
            (The bare "the ELF hash does not move" version of that gate is
            unsatisfiable by design, because a book edit legitimately moves
            the embedded /SYS; it is stated here in the only form the artifact
            can answer.)  Rung 2 is a gate that
            READS the disc back on the host and says each named file is on the
            ISO with its byte count and hash -- `isoinfo`/`hdiutil` mount,
            `ls -l` and `shasum` -- because "the ISO was written" is not
            evidence "the ISO has the volumes" the way a mount that lists them
            is.  What neither rung delivers: an application that OPENS the disc
            at runtime.  That needs the CD/DVD read path -- `cdrom0:\`, SIF and
            libcdvd on the IOP, the very actor LANE 13 opened and left unpaid
            -- and it is the stage after this one, agreed with the user as
            "apps+embeds first, then CD stack."  Until then the disc is a
            complete, shippable full-feature PAYLOAD that the emulator's own
            ELF path boots and that a later LANE-13 driver mounts; the
            in-drawer volumes are byte-identical to the /SYS the ELF already
            embeds, so nothing on screen changes and nothing is lost by the
            wait.  The image stays frozen; the disc gets the whole system.

            What Rung 1 measured, on landing (2026-10-15, dials
            `PS2_GL=eegs PS2_FP=hw PS2_MSX=0`).  The disc is 26,920,960 B and
            thirteen files, and the tree read back off a mounted ISO is the
            tree the rung specifies -- `SYSTEM.CNF`, `BTRON.ELF`, `SYS.VOL`,
            `ANDERS.VOL`, `QUAKE/PAK0.PAK`, `QUAKE/DRONE1M1..8.DEM`.  Every
            one of them is byte-identical to its source: the disc's
            `BTRON.ELF` to the working image, the two volumes to FRESH
            `mkbtronfs` builds of their manifests (never to the working
            `btron_sys.vol`, which five host suites mount read/write), pak0 to
            the asset at 18,689,235 B, and each demo to its file.  All names
            are 8.3-uppercase, so a level-1 reader sees what the guest will
            ask for.  The freeze then has two halves, and both were run.  Half
            one, the packaging: delete the ISO, rebuild the ISO ALONE, and the
            ELF does not relink -- its mtime and its hash both stay exactly
            where the dialled link left them.  No hash is quoted here, because
            the paragraph that would quote it changes the payload and so
            invalidates itself; the run that executed this control records the
            mtime and the two hashes it printed in doc/md/PS2-GS-LOG.md.  Half
            two, the structural claim, from a byte census of the rebuilt image
            against the image before this lane: the ELF is still 5,927,592 B,
            its section table is byte-identical, and EVERY differing byte but
            two lies inside the embedded /SYS block (file 0x489800 through
            0x589800, the last 1 MiB of `.data`) -- that is this book, refreshed
            as the payload requires -- the two lie in `.text`, and ZERO lie in
            `.data` outside the volume block or in the symbol tables.  It is
            stated as a partition and not as counts deliberately: the
            payload-byte count IS the length of this paragraph, so any figure
            written here is stale before it lands, and the run that measured it
            keeps its numbers in doc/md/PS2-GS-LOG.md.  No second volume got
            embedded and no buffer grew, which is the half the user's constraint
            is actually about.  The two text bytes are worth naming because they
            are not packaging either: two leaf functions in the dial-reading
            trio (`gl_backend_choice.c`, `gl_dispatch.c`, `egl_surface.c`)
            went `addiu $v0,$zero,0` to `addiu $v0,$zero,3`, i.e. they now
            return `GL_BACKEND_EEGS` (3) where the baseline image returned
            `GL_BACKEND_TINYGL` (0), and those three objects are the only C
            objects rebuilt by this lane's link (measured by mtime).  INFERRED
            cause, and it is the documented one: a bare make round between the
            dialled build and the link reset `PS2_GL` in the stamp and relinked
            the image with the trio compiled for the other backend -- exactly
            the symptom the stamp rule at Makefile:1232 exists to catch.  The
            rung-(i) numbers are not contaminated by it, because the bench is a
            separate link and its own rows show the GS path live (`batch=64`,
            no `raster f_menu` row); what the finding costs is that the image
            flashed before this lane was not the image its dial described, and
            it agrees now.  Finally the pair of needles Rung 2's gate uses to
            keep /ANDERS out of the image honest.  The negative one is a string
            only the Anders tree carries -- the project URL that heads every one
            of its documents -- and it occurs 0 times in `btron-ps2.elf` and 48
            times in the disc's `ANDERS.VOL`; that 48 is a property of the
            Anders corpus and not of this book, so writing it here cannot make
            it stale.  The positive control -- the proof that the census can see
            embedded payload at all -- is a phrase this book itself spells, in
            its title line among other places, so a count of it is a property of
            the run and not of the lane: it moved by one when this paragraph was
            rewritten, and where it settled is recorded in doc/md/PS2-GS-LOG.md
            rather than here.  The negative needle is named in
            `scripts/test_ps2.sh` and only described here, deliberately: naming
            it in this book would write it into the image, and the image must
            not contain it.  That is not a hypothetical -- the first version of the
            pair used a word from the Anders corpus as the needle AND spelled it
            out in this paragraph, the paragraph entered the payload with the
            book refresh, and the row went red on its own author.  Reading it
            back: the gate discriminates, including against the person writing
            it.

=====================================================================
PART SIX -- THE PORT: DRIVERS, DESKTOP, STORAGE
=====================================================================

  Everything above is about time.  This Part is about the parts: which unit
  owns which piece of hardware, what the compositor is allowed to assume, and
  how a machine with no file system gets its content.  Each section ends with
  the row that says its claim is true of the artifact on disk.

17. Drivers and files
---------------------

  LAW 17 (a driver is cleanroom, register-level, and imports no vendor code).
  No line of any SDK is linked or copied into this port
  (src/cores/core_ps2.c:13 states the rule at the top of the file); ps2sdk
  appears only as a description of hardware, per LAW 14b.  Every driver here
  was written against a register table and a manual, and every one of them is
  named in a gate row rather than trusted by its existence.

  The units, each in one line, with the law it carries:

    boot_ps2.s     EE reset vector, $gp and $sp, unrolled BSS wipe, the BIOS
                   syscall wrappers SetGsCrt and PutIMR, then ps2_kernel_main.
                   Its wait loop is the image's second .set mips3 exception
                   (5).
    ps2.ld         the layout: .text at 0x0100000-class addresses, and the
                   ASSERT that fails the LINK when _end reaches the stack or
                   the 32 MB RDRAM end -- chapter 4's budget as a link error.
    ps2_gs_reg.s   the image's only 64-bit GPR instructions.  ps2_gs_poke64()
                   and ps2_gs_peek64() perform the GS's single-sd / single-ld
                   register latch; assembled with local .set mips3 under the
                   shared -march=mips2, arguments and return halves 32-bit, so
                   it is O32-legal on both sides (5).
    ps2_builtins.c the whole soft-float runtime the image links against:
                   the 32 __*sf3/__*df3/conversion libcalls clang invents at
                   -msoft-float -nostdlib, a bit engine serving both IEEE
                   formats from 32-bit pieces, and the single-precision entry
                   points that pick COP1 at run time.  One of the three
                   -mhard-float units (6a), and the only one whose float
                   instructions are asserted to be ONLY its own.
    ps2_gs.h       privileged GS registers (0x12000000: PMODE, SMODE2,
                   DISPFB1, DISPLAY1, CSR) and GIF DMA registers (0x1000A000),
                   reached only through GS_REG_POKE / GS_REG_PEEK.
    ps2_gs.c       display initialization, vsync, the Host-to-Local GIF DMA
                   blitter over the desktop's own canvas, the texture-plane
                   upload, and the GIF sender that still blocks -- by the
                   buffer-lifetime law, and now with its wait counted (11.6).
                   Also the only place the image answers the ring's three
                   hardware questions.
    ps2_gif_ring.{c,h}
                   the bounded, non-blocking writer in front of DMAC channel
                   2: post / drain / reap, the S_FULL refusal count, and the
                   sender's own blocked time as two columns.  Pure
                   bookkeeping -- it names no memory-mapped register, which
                   is what lets the host gate drive a full channel, an empty
                   one and an over-capacity producer on a machine with no
                   PS2 in it (11.6, and the seven image rows that keep it
                   that way).
    ps2_fp.h       the COP1 wrappers and TGL_TRUNC -- the file where an
                   illegal encoding family is named once instead of per call
                   site (6d).
    ps2_font.h     the 8x8 ASCII bitmap font, 128 characters -- crisp UI text
                   with no glyph rasteriser on the EE.
    ps2_sio.{h,c}  the cleanroom SIO0 UART driver (0x1000F180 / KPUTCHAR),
                   which is also the shell this chapter's instruments speak
                   through.
    ps2_pad.{h,c}  the cleanroom DualShock 2 driver: analog velocity
                   integration, deadband filter, button edge detection,
                   events.
    ps2_usb.{h,c}  the cleanroom OHCI host (0xBF801600 uncached) and the USB
                   HID boot-protocol keyboard and mouse decoders -- whose work
                   is divided by chapter 12's LAW 12.
    core_ps2.c     the platform core: the app suite, the TIP/IME badge, the
                   SIO shell, the event queue, the plane table's installation,
                   the RTOS heartbeat, the folded clock, and every bench
                   phase.
    test_ps2.sh    the image gate: architecture, entry point, driver symbols,
                   R5900 opcodes, the ABI law, the FPU law by mnemonic AND by
                   byte scan, the clip tests as six ordered c.olt.s, the
                   single-canvas law, the embedded volume, the ISO.

  DualShock 2 mapping, as the port defines it:

    left stick (LX,LY)   pointer velocity, deadband abs(axis-128) > 16
    Cross                left click -- and Return while a menu is open
    Square               right click -- and quick Backspace inside the OSK
    Circle               cancel / ESC, dismisses dropdowns and dialogs, closes
                         the OSK
    Triangle             cycle TIP/IME: ASCII -> Hiragana -> Katakana ->
                         Tibetan
    Start                toggle the desktop menu (the tracker)
    Select / L1 / R1     cycle the active window
    D-pad                BTRON_KEY_UP/DOWN/LEFT/RIGHT; also moves the cursor
                         16 px -- but NOT while a menu is open, which is rule
                         2
    L2 / R2              toggle the on-screen keyboard

    simulate from the shell:  pad <btns_hex> [lx ly]   e.g.  pad bfff 128 128

  LAW 17b (a launcher row is reachable with the D-pad alone), which is three
  rules, each earned by a measured defect:

  1  Two key encodings are live and BOTH are accepted.  A windowed host
     forwards its own keysyms (SDLK_UP/DOWN are 0x111/0x112,
     src/window/event.c) while every bare-metal driver -- the pad, the PS2 USB
     keyboard, the Mac Quadra ports -- injects the BTRON_KEY_* codes from
     include/btron/event.h (RIGHT 0x4000004F, LEFT ...50, DOWN ...51, UP
     ...52).  A handler matching one is dead on the other half of the targets;
     tracker_handle_key() used to match only the keysym, which is why the
     D-pad moved the highlight by nothing but the pointer's new pixel.
  2  While a menu is open the D-pad must not displace the cursor, and Cross
     must become Return.  tracker_handle_mouse_move() recomputes hover_index
     from the pixel it lands on, the row pitch is TRACKER_ITEM_HEIGHT = 20 px
     and one D-pad step was 16 px, so a move that accompanies the arrow
     selects whichever row the pointer reached.  A click is worse: it is
     delivered at the pointer, not at the highlighted row.
  3  A menu handler consumes a key only when it ACTED on it.
     workbench_process_event() asks global_menu_handle_key() before
     tracker_handle_key(), and global_menu_is_open() is true whenever the
     LAUNCHER is open as well as when a dropdown is (global_menu.c:239), so
     returning TRUE for an arrow with no dropdown active swallows the key one
     stage before the launcher sees it.  That is the exact shape of the first
     on-target measurement: 14 D-pad DOWN hops, highlight fixed at row 0.

  The state to read is global_menu_get_active(), not global_menu_is_open(): a
  dropdown worth navigating has active_menu > 0.

  MEASURE  BENCH_APP=1 BENCH_TIMEOUT=460 scripts/ps2_bench.sh presses the
           pad's own report bytes rather than calling the tracker's API
           (ps2_bench_launcher_key_route(), ps2_bench_launch_xmb_by_key()):

    [BENCH] key: hover 0->6 in 5 DOWN hops, want row 6, selected=' (XMB)'
    [BENCH] key: launcher=1 deskbar_open=1 dropdown=-1
    [BENCH] key: Cross on row 5 '(Terminal)' launched 1->2 menu=0 pointer 400,450->400,450

  dropdown=-1 is rule 3's condition; pointer 400,450->400,450 is rule 2; the
  five hops to row 6 are rule 1.  Host coverage of the same three rules is
  make test-tracker (78/78) and make test-global-menu (55/55), the latter
  including the fall-through assertion.

  USB.  The motherboard has two USB 1.1 ports on an OHCI block at 0x1F801600
  (uncached 0xBF801600).  The driver verifies presence through HcRevision
  (0x10 or 0x11), runs the controller to OHCI_CTRL_HCFS_OPER, and watches
  HcRhPortStatus[0..1] for connection.  Keyboard reports are the standard
  8-byte boot form with differential tracking across the 6-key rollover,
  modifiers and usage IDs mapped to BTRON keycodes, Caps Lock state, F1-F12,
  the navigation cluster, the keypad, and the authentic Japanese keys (Henkan,
  Muhenkan, Hiragana/Katakana toggle).  That differential is EDGE-ONLY by
  design, so a held key is two events and never a stream: the repeats a typist
  expects are not missing from the wire, they were never on it, and LAW 19f
  says who makes them and on whose numbers.  Mouse reports are the 3- or
  4-byte relative form, injected as EV_MOUSE_MOVE and EV_BUT_DOWN/EV_BUT_UP.
  What a count on this wire is WORTH is chapter 13's question, not this one.

  No keyboard?  L2/R2 (or `osk` in the shell) opens a 10x4 on-screen keyboard
  inside B-Editor -- row 0 digits, row 1 QWERTYUIOP, row 2 ASDFGHJKL plus RET,
  row 3 ZXCVBNM plus SPC, DEL, ESC -- navigated by the D-pad, typed by Cross,
  backspaced by Square, closed by Circle, IME-cycled by Triangle.

  The SIO0 shell is the console these books' rows come from (115200 8N1, with
  ANSI handling for arrows, home, end, delete and the two page keys):

    help info apps          hardware, and the app list
    open <app>              cabinet, editor, terminal, sound, chat, settings
    tip [mode]              ascii | hira | kata | tibetan, or cycle
    tasks                   RTOS task table and stack pointers
    mem                     RDRAM, the 12 MB kernel heap, VRAM usage
    desktop status          force a full redraw; show pointer and TIP mode
    mouse <x> <y> move <dx> <dy> click [1|2]
    key <char> type <text>  inject a keystroke / a string
    pad <hex> [lx ly]       simulated controller state
    sens [pct] maxstep [px]  the pointer's own policy: px per count (1..400%,
                             100 = verbatim) and px per INPUT period (0 =
                             none)
    ptrsrc [emu|hw|auto]    show, force, or re-detect the source profile
    ptrstat <tag> ptrcal    what is on the wire; loopback calibration
    reboot                  halt the Emotion Engine

  The four pointer commands and the order they must run in are chapter 13's;
  the clock rows are chapter 8's.  Nothing here invents a measurement.

18. The GS framebuffer architecture
-----------------------------------

  LAW 18 (one canvas, and it is the compositor's own array).  The GIF DMA
  reads the very memory the compositor painted.  core_ps2.c installs
  s_desktop_backbuffer into the driver with ps2_gs_set_framebuffer() as the
  FIRST act of ps2_kernel_main(), before the SIO and the GS come up, because
  the boot log is written into the canvas the screen shows.  There is no
  second framebuffer and no copy between two of them: the array the driver
  used to own (ps2_fb_memory, 800x600x4 = 1,920,000 bytes of .bss) and the
  one-word-per-pixel sweep that filled it (blit_backbuffer_rect_to_ps2fb,
  49,094 us of a full present) are both gone from the tree.

  WHY  Because the premise that made them necessary is false on this port: THE
       EE'S L1 CACHES ARE NEVER ENABLED.  Every coprocessor-0 write in the
       image is to Status (register 12 select 0) and there are exactly three
       of them -- btron_fp_init() raising CU1, ps2_ee_enable_interrupts() and
       ps2_ee_exit_handler() setting the global enable bit 16 (EIE) by
       read-modify-write, because PCSX2 does not decode ei (Unknown R5900
       COP0).  Nothing is written to the cache-control registers and no cache
       maintenance instruction exists anywhere, so the caches stay as the BIOS
       left them -- and the upload path reads its source through the uncached
       KSEG1 alias regardless (UNCACHED() in ps2_gs.c).  Every alias of an
       address reaches the same RDRAM and a bus master sees a store as soon as
       it lands.

  GATE  the single-canvas rows assert the premise and not the symptom: no
        ps2_fb_memory symbol, no present-path sweep, exactly one screen-sized
        CT32 object in the image and that it IS s_desktop_backbuffer,
        ps2_gs_set_framebuffer present as the install point, every mtc0 in the
        disassembly a Status write, none of them naming register 9 -- Count is
        the clock chapter 8 measures and writing it would break every elapsed
        time in the image -- and no cache op.

  LAW 18b (VRAM is addressed in two units, and both units are the whole of
  it).  The GS has 4 MB of eDRAM, laid out in pages of 2,048 32-bit words
  (8,192 B) and blocks of 64 words (256 B), with non-linear column-interleaved
  swizzling.  A texture or transfer address (TEX0.TBA, BITBLTBUF.SBA/DBA) is a
  14-bit index of 256-byte units and 16,384 x 256 = 4,194,304 exactly; a frame
  or Z-buffer base (FRAME.FBA, ZBUF.ZBA) is a 9-bit index of 8,192-byte units
  and 512 x 8,192 = 4,194,304 exactly.  Each field's width times its unit is
  the whole of VRAM, which is what makes the reading unambiguous rather than
  remembered -- and it falsified a comment in ps2_gs_pack.h that said 256-byte
  units for FRAME.FBA.  The encoders take a raw field value so no built word
  changed, but a caller's arithmetic would have been off by 32x.

  Consequence, arithmetic rather than taste (and the reason chapter 15's laws
  are shaped as they are): the canvas is CT32 at FBW 13 (832 px) x 600 =
  1,996,800 B; the XMB's GL window is 776x536 (xmb.c:3331 takes the desktop
  minus 24 and 64), whose own CT32 layer at FBW 13 would be 1,783,808 B; the
  icon atlas is a 384x384 upload but what the GS must address is the plane
  TinyGL allocated -- 512x512, because gl_image_alloc() rounds each axis up to
  a power of two and TEX0.TW/TH can only name one -- 1,048,576 B, not the
  589,824 B of staging pixels the app handed over.  Those three sum to
  4,829,184 B, which is 634,880 B MORE than the GS has, before the font sheet
  or any Z buffer.

  LAW 18c (no second full-screen surface).  The backend renders into the very
  canvas the CPU blit has just filled, with SCISSOR clipped to the window's
  rect.  What the allocator therefore reserves is the canvas at page 0
  (1,996,800 B rounded up to 244 pages of 8 KB), then texture pages out of the
  remaining 268: the icon plane 128, the font sheet's 128x128 plane 8, leaving
  132 pages -- 1,081,344 B -- for a wallpaper-as-texture, further atlases, and
  the one 8 KB page a Z buffer needs if a depth test is ever turned on.  A
  plane's span is a whole number of 16 KB units, not of 8 KB pages: a CT32
  picture is laid out in 4 KB blocks of 32x32 texels that pair into pages, and
  a plane's address enters fields of different units, so rounding to 16 KB is
  a whole number of blocks, of pages and of pages in even pairs.  The cost for
  the two planes this port has is nil, and it is the difference between a
  future 64-wide plane and its neighbour's texels arriving in the same block.

  LAW 18d (the canvas crosses as a blit, not as primitives).  B-System
  presents the desktop through the hardware Host-to-Local blitter rather than
  by rasterising the canvas: a full-screen primitive would re-introduce
  sub-pixel rounding, page-boundary clipping and per-packet DMA overhead for
  pixels that are already computed.  Five quadwords prime a transfer --

    BITBLTBUF (0x50)  DBA, DBW, DPSM = GS_PSM_CT32
    TRXPOS    (0x51)  DSAX, DSAY          (destination on the GS page)
    TRXREG    (0x52)  RRW, RRH            (the rectangle's extent)
    TRXDIR    (0x53)  0 = Host -> Local

  with the canvas's own DBW = (800+63)/64 = 13, and the pixels then stream as
  IMAGE-format packets (FLG=2) over DMAC channel 2, chunked at 8,000 quadwords
  because D2_QWC is a 12-bit field.  CT32 needs an even RRW and the stream is
  whole quadwords (4 px) wide, so a rect is widened to both, which stays
  inside the 832-pixel VRAM page.  A Host-to-Local IMAGE transfer carries no
  row pitch, so a narrow rectangle uploads one row at a time and a full-width
  band uploads one rectangle.  The source half of BITBLTBUF is left at zero
  knowingly: a Host-to-Local transfer ignores SBA/SBW/SPI because the source
  IS the quadword stream, so the destination half is the measured half.

  LAW 18e (the present is rect-limited, and a box rather than a row band).
  The rectangle comes from a set of up to PS2_BAND_MAX merged boxes --
  y-touching damage unions its x span, overflow falls back to the whole canvas
  -- and it is a box because the render pass costs about 0.3 us per pixel: 16
  rows at full width is 5,370 us with the byte-swap sweep still in the path,
  the same 16 rows clipped to the cursor sprite's own 16 columns is 528 us.
  Deleting the sweep is what the swap column measures; the gate expects it to
  read 0, and did: the whole-canvas row went from 274 ms to 265.782 ms and a
  16x16 sprite present costs 528 us.

  Video timing.  The shipped default is NOT the NTSC frame mode most PS2 prose
  assumes, and the reason is an emulator fact worth stating precisely:

    mode              SetGsCrt        SMODE2   DISPFB            DISPLAY
    ----------------  --------------  -------  ----------------  -------------------------
    800x600 on 720p   (0, 0x52, 0)    0x00     FBP 0, FBW 13,    DX 420 DY 40,
    (default)                          (prog)   PSM CT32          MAGH 0 MAGV 0,
                                                                  DW 799 DH 599
    DTV 480p          (0, 0x50, 0)    0x00     FBP 0, FBW 10,    DX 232 DY 35,
                                                                  MAGH 1, 1439x479
    NTSC 640x448      (1, 2, 1)       0x03     FBP 0, FBW 10,    DX 636 DY 50,
    frame mode        (interlaced)    (INT+    (CT32)             MAGH 3 MAGV 0,
                                                                  DW 2559 DH 447

    PMODE is 0xFF63 in all three: Circuit 1 + Circuit 2 enabled, SLBG = 0, so
    the frame buffer is the output.  BGCOLOR is (24, 48, 80), which is what
    fills the raster outside DW/DH.

  WHY the default is a 720p timing carrying an unscaled 800x600 window instead
  of a hardware-correct VESA 800x600@60 mode word: PCSX2 keys its entire CRT
  geometry off SMODE1's CMOD and LC and gives every VESA mode the one 640x480
  table entry (GSState.cpp VideoModeOffsets[2]), which GetResolution() then
  clamps the output to -- so a VESA timing can only ever show 640 of our 800
  columns and 480 of our 600 rows there, and its DY=25 is read as a -9 line
  offset because that entry expects baseDY=34.  The 720p entry is 1280x720
  with baseDY=24, so the same canvas comes out unclipped and unshifted.  On a
  real PS2 this paints 800x600 into the top-left of a 720p frame.  PROVEN on
  this emulator, by the monitor; INFERRED on a box, which this port has not
  driven -- see chapter 24.

  LAW 18f (the cursor is non-destructive).  Mouse cursor drawing backs up the
  background it covers into cursor_saved[16*16]; erasing restores those exact
  words, so there is no smear and no repaint of the region.  Under the GS
  backend this is the canvas's own rule; inside a GL layer the same argument
  is increment 6's textured quad (15, LAW 15d).

19. Desktop, compositor, input mapping
--------------------------------------

  LAW 19 (the compositor is one shared model; a PS2 fix that lands only on PS2
  has not landed).  src/window/wnd.c, src/desktop/*.c and
  src/graphics/dp_core.c are linked by the Pi 400, the hosted builds and the
  PS2 alike, so every change to them is made in the shared layer and verified
  against the arm64 target -- the standing non-regression gate is `make
  arm64-elf` after every PS2 round, and a PS2-only behaviour enters through
  #if defined(BTRON_PS2_TARGET) with a row proving the guarded branch is the
  one that shipped (chapter 22).

  LAW 19b (the canvas's content is decided by rules, and the rule that fired
  is printed).  Whether a present is the whole canvas is not a fact about the
  damage but a decision, so the [XMBT] cycle row carries why= as a bit decode:
  1 a non-move event, 2 the convergence timer, 4 global menu, 8 tracker, 16
  window-manager interaction, 32 IME, 64 band-set overflow, 128 damage that
  already spanned the canvas -- and dmg= shows the band set as the app and the
  pointer left it, BEFORE any of those rules replaced it.  Without the pair,
  "why did that frame repaint 480,000 pixels" is unanswerable from a console.

  LAW 19c (a window repaints on its own validity, not on the composite's
  mode).  redraw_all_windows_clip() used to run every intersecting window's
  paint callback whenever the composite was not blit-only, and one inval_wnd()
  made the whole composite non-blit-only -- so three windows that had not
  changed cost 55 ms a frame next to the XMB's own 184 ms.  Each window's
  repaint is now gated on its own img_valid.

  MEASURE  chapter 11's before/after: comp paint= 238,860 -> 184,260 us and
           render= 333,682 -> 279,041 us, with bfn= counting the paints that
           used to run.

  LAW 19d (no heap after init is this port's open exception, and it is
  bounded, checked, and named).  INTERPRETER.txt §3.3 forbids runtime
  allocation; core_ps2.c owns a 12 MB pool in .bss (PS2_HEAP_SIZE,
  core_ps2.c:92) that the desktop allocates from while it runs.  It used to be
  a bump allocator whose kfree() returned the block to nothing, so opening one
  window per menu click consumed the pool until a later allocation failed and
  the app painted nothing.  It is a first-fit pool with real coalescing now,
  and ps2_heap_check() walks it for the invariants -- payload inside the pool,
  sizes a multiple of the 16-byte header, no cycle -- on both sides of the
  open, which is what `[BENCH] heap before/after xmb: ... pool sound` reports.
  The header is padded to 16 bytes by a reserve word and a negative-size
  typedef asserts it, because with 12 MB of .bss the image sits about 1.5 MiB
  under ps2.ld's ASSERT and a header that grew by 8 bytes would quietly eat
  that headroom.  This is recorded as a misalignment (Appendix B) and not as a
  solved problem: lane 11's per-task stacks and §3.3 pull in the same
  direction, and neither is satisfied.

  The black XMB had THREE measured causes and only the third was in the app.
  A book that names them saves someone re-deriving them:
    1  the un-freable heap above;
    2  a window sized from a desktop record that had not been filled in yet,
       asking 952x564 -- larger than the canvas -- so the surface and its
       depth buffer were allocated beyond what the port presents.  The single
       source is btron_desktop_note_size() (src/desktop/desktop.c:510), called
       from the PS2 startup with the real mode; it is the one place the window
       layer reads the canvas size from before allocating.
    3  the double-precision FPU ops of LAW 6a, which did nothing at all and
       left the geometry uncomputed -- the screen was black because the
       arithmetic silently was not.
  MEASURE  the surface census, which is the decisive row and not an inference:
           [BENCH] xmb surface frame 3 : 768x500 black=0 other=384000
            first_colour=0xff0283cd box=0,0..767,499
  black=383999 other=1 was the failure -- one pixel lit, all the rest unlit --
  and a count of `Unknown R5900` rows in the thousands says which cause it is.
  Under PS2_GL=eegs this row reads black=384000 and MEANS something else
  entirely; that is chapter 9's reading rule 3.

  The desktop the compositor holds: teal 0x008080 with the retro dot-grid,
  five Real Body / Virtual Object icons (実身・仮身 Cabinet, 基本エディタ T-Editor, 端末シェル
  GTerm, 音響機器 Audio, 会話通信 Chat), the authentic window manager (hit-testing,
  z-order elevation to top_wnd, the 16x16 diagonal hatch resize grip, sliding
  titlebar tabs, the [X] close button, client-area event routing), the deskbar
  with ［BTRON］ システム(S) 実身・仮身(O) ウィンドウ(W) 道具・文字(T), the Japanese calendar plate
  with 日/月/火/水/木/金/土, and the TIP/IME badge [ ASC ] [ あ ] [ ア ] [ བོད ].

  LAW 19e (an app row is a WEAK opener, so a target without the app renders
  the row and does nothing).  The tracker's blocks are, in order: the live
  visible-window list, the application rows 端末 (Terminal) and 横断メディア メニュー
  (XMB), and the system rows.  The openers are weak declarations in
  include/btron/apps.h, which is why the launcher is linkable on a target that
  links no app -- and why the image gate that names an opener must ask the
  right symbol: chapter 20's table has a row that fails for exactly this
  reason.

  LAW 19f (the port obeys the Input applet, not a constant of its own).  The
  PS2 keyboard has NO repeat constants.  It reads the three values
  src/settings/input.c:16-18 defines for every target -- g_kbd_repeat_enabled,
  g_kbd_repeat_delay_us, g_kbd_repeat_interval_us -- shipped as *FX 11 = 16 cs
  (160,000 us before the first repeat) and *FX 12 = 40 cps (25,000 us between
  repeats), repeat ON; and it reads them LIVE, so the XMB's Input page changes
  what a held key does with no rebuild.  core_arm64.c:2703-2705 has always
  obeyed the same three on the Pi 400.  What the PS2 lacked was the READER,
  not the setting, and the fix creates no fourth copy of the numbers -- LAW 19
  applied to an input detail small enough to be forgotten.

  WHY  posix pays nothing for its repeat.  The host makes it, and SDL forwards
       every host repeat as another SDL_KEYDOWN (src/window/event.c:246),
       which get_evt() re-emits as another EV_KEY_DOWN.  The PS2 has no host
       under it, and its HID decoder is a six-slot differential
       (ps2_usb_process_keyboard_report(), src/drivers/ps2/ps2_usb.c:1444)
       that fires on edges only: a key held for a second is exactly two
       events, one press and one release.  The emulator's own repeat is
       unreachable, because PCSX2 hands over a DEVICE and not the host's
       keyboard settings.  So the typematic stream is produced inside the
       port, by the pass, under chapter 12's handover -- and asking the human
       to tap is not a fix.

  MECHANICS  ps2_kbd_arm(), ps2_kbd_disarm() and ps2_kbd_repeat_tick() in
             src/cores/core_ps2.c.  Three rules are load-bearing, and each is
             a bug avoided rather than a style:
  - ARMED ONLY WHEN ROUTED.  ps2_usb_on_key() swallows some chords for the
    compositor; a swallowed chord must not arm, or every interval would move
    the cursor or change the gain.
  - ONE KEY REPEATS: THE NEWEST STILL DOWN.  A later press re-arms the delay
    and the armed key's own release disarms, while releasing any OTHER key
    leaves it running.  That is what a host keyboard does, and the multi-key
    case is not what auto-repeat exists for.
  - THE TICK RUNS AFTER THE POLL AND BEFORE THE DISPATCH, in ps2_gui_pass()
    and in the Stage-1 loop.  The pass that could have seen the press is the
    pass that owes the repeat, and a release heard by this poll disarms before
    it can fire once more.  Stage 1 arms only btron_key < 0x100, because its
    prompt consumes characters and the codes above it have no binding there.
    On the GUI side a repeat is a PRESS WITH NO MATCHING RELEASE: the key
    really is still down, and a typematic stream from hardware is a run of
    downs ending in one up.

  LAW 19g (a tick pays every interval that elapsed).  A tick can run once per
  pass, and a pass is not 25 ms: this machine's measured XMB periods are
  141,519, 138,936 and 149,304 us (chapter 11).  One repeat per pass would
  therefore make the SETTING the frame rate -- the applet's 40 cps row and its
  12.5 cps row would feel identical, which is the opposite of having the
  setting.  So ps2_kbd_due() divides the elapsed gap by the interval, advances
  the schedule by the intervals it PAID and never by the clock, which is what
  keeps the MEAN rate the setting's rate, and caps the burst at
  PS2_KBD_REPEAT_MAX = 8 so that a long stall cannot flood the 256-slot event
  queue.  A gap longer than the cap can pay is DROPPED rather than banked: the
  schedule resynchronises instead of repeating at the cap forever.  And with
  no timebase the folded clock of chapter 8 reads a constant zero, every
  difference reads zero, due is zero, and repeat stays off -- a port with no
  clock does not invent a cadence.

  MEASURE  two rows, one build, 2026-10-13, from the run whose own [CFG] row
           names PS2_FP=hw:cop1 PS2_GL=eegs:

    [KBD] auto-repeat: enabled=1  delay=160000 us  interval=25000 us
          (Input applet's *FX 11/12; a held HID key repeats from here)
    [BENCH] kbd cadence: enabled=1 delay=160000 us pass=149304 us
            -> *FX 12 25000=5 40000=3 80000=1 reps/pass (cap 8)

  The first is the shipped default reaching the guest exactly as the applet
  wrote it.  The second is the loopback, in the ptrcal tradition of 13.1: it
  prices the law against the last stamped cycle -- the same period the [XMBT]
  cycle row above it prints -- with no hand on a key, and it is the row that
  says the three rates the menu offers are three DIFFERENT behaviours on this
  machine rather than one behaviour with three labels.  The labels of the
  claims, in the decode of appendix A:

    the image reads the applet's three globals, at their shipped
    defaults           PROVEN -- the [KBD] boot row
    the three *FX 12 rates are distinguishable at this machine's pass
                       period  PROVEN -- 5 / 3 / 1 repeats per 149,304 us, one
                               log
    an unpaid backlog is dropped, not banked, and the mean rate is the
                       schedule's  PROVEN BY CONSTRUCTION -- ps2_kbd_due()
                       advances by due x interval, or by the whole gap at the cap
    the FEEL: whether 160 ms is the right delay, whether Backspace deletes
                       at a readable speed  REQUIRES A HAND -- `make run-ps2`
    the burst size on a real console
                       INFERRED -- the divisor is measured at boot (295 ticks/us
                       here, ~147 there), so the period, and so the cap's use,
                       differ

  GATE   `make test-ps2 PS2_GL=eegs PS2_FP=hw` is 91/91 with the change in,
         and the frame bill did not move: draw= 49,277 / 46,826 / 46,263 us
         against 49,128 / 46,897 / 46,225 us from the same configuration one
         build earlier, with comp bg=9897 frame=20308 paint=46275 blit=30729,
         px=480000, and [GSTS] groups=51 at drop=0 coverdrop=0 holding.  The
         cost of an UNARMED key is two comparisons, which is why the bill is
         unchanged.  `make arm64-elf` exits 0 and rebuilds nothing, because
         the change lives in src/cores/core_ps2.c alone and no arm64 source
         list contains that file -- chapter 19's non-regression gate is
         satisfied by the link list, not by hope.  What this gate cannot do is
         print anything about a repeat that actually happened: the per-burst
         row [KBD] rep k=... due=...  exists only under HIDTRACE=1, so a
         default image is silent about a working repeat, and reading rule R1
         says silence is not absence.

20. The /SYS volume inside the executable
-----------------------------------------

  sc and tv run INSIDE the Terminal window: gterm_execute_cmd() derives a
  row/column count from the window's own client box and calls
  sc_session_init() (src/apps/gterm.c:372-406), which paints into the cell
  grid term_get_cell() reads back in paint_gterm.  No tty device is on that
  path, and the 12 MiB heap comfortably holds the ~2.7 MiB GTermState, the
  ~640 KiB TermContext and the ScContext.  The one thing that stops it is
  storage: sc_session_init() returns at its first statement, `if (vfs_init() <
  0) return -1;` (src/clu/sc/sc.c:175), and vfs_init() on this target is one
  expression (src/clu/vfs.c:35) asking whether ANY volume is mounted.  B-MSX's
  Games band comes from vfs_list_dir() over /SYS/msx, so a machine with no
  volume gets an emulator with no cartridges for exactly the reason it gets a
  terminal with no sc.

  LAW 20 (one volume, one builder, one manifest).  /SYS is btron_sys.vol -- 33
  manifest records in 2 drawers, 26 carrying a payload, in a 1 MiB volume --
  written by src/tools/mkbtronfs.c from src/tools/manifest.txt by the Makefile
  rule that already exists for it, the same image the hosted targets mount and
  make test-fs reads.  The PS2 build adds NO second description of that
  content: no file list, no sizes, no names.

  LAW 20b (the volume rebuilds when its content changes, which means the
  manifest's payload paths are PREREQUISITES).  make otherwise rebuilds
  nothing when doc/txt/PS2.txt changes -- which, since 2026-10-14, is the
  book you are reading, so editing a sentence here and building without this
  rule ships an image whose /SYS still holds the previous draft -- and the
  image ships the volume as it was
  last built -- the same stale-artifact disease chapter 4 and chapter 21
  document for PS2_GL and PS2_FP, arrived at from the other side: here the
  CONTENT changed, not the flag.  Two things had to be true for the sentence
  to be normative: the prerequisite list is emitted by the tool that reads the
  manifest (`--deps btron_sys.vol.d`, pulled in by -include beside the rule)
  and not by an awk one-liner in the Makefile, because that one-liner was a
  second model of the manifest's grammar -- it had to agree about quoting,
  about the `-` placeholder and about which of `FILE "name" BIN payload
  drawer`'s four fields is the path, and any one disagreement fails as a
  volume that quietly never rebuilds.  And neither mkbtronfs nor btron_sys.vol
  may be .PHONY, because a phony target has no timestamp for prerequisites to
  be measured against -- so the rule listed payloads it could not be gated by
  and rebuilt the volume every build instead.  MEASURE, on this build:
  touching assets/msx/Lode Runner (1984)(Sony)[a].rom rebuilds the volume AND
  the image; touching src/cores/core_ps2.c does not touch either.

  LAW 20c (the volume is a function of its inputs, not of the clock).  Every
  header stamp mkbtronfs used to write was time(NULL), so two runs over the
  same manifest produced different bytes -- harmless while the volume was a
  file you boot from, and a gate-breaker once it is .incbin'd: make ps2 runs
  the tool again, so the volume is always newer than the ELF's copy, and the
  row that compares them then matches an older generation against a newer one
  and reports 90 differing bytes at 0x1404 for a build that changed nothing.
  Therefore btron_fs_now_stamp() (src/fs/file.c) is the ONLY reading of "now"
  a volume takes -- vol.c's root header and file.c's create/close stamps both
  go through it, which also fixes the older bug of two time(NULL) calls
  straddling a tick and giving one volume two dates -- and
  btron_fs_set_stamp() pins it.  mkbtronfs pins before writing its first
  header, from SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH if the build exports one, otherwise from the
  newest mtime among the manifest and the payloads it names.  When no usable
  stamp can be read -- a tarball whose mtimes predate 2000-01-01 and no
  SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH -- the tool SAYS so on stderr and leaves the clock in,
  because the failure this law exists to prevent is a non-reproducible image
  that looks reproducible.  MEASURE: two runs two seconds apart are
  cmp-identical, and the volume rows are green on both.

  LAW 20d (delivery is inside the executable).  src/drivers/ps2/ps2_sys_vol.s
  pulls the volume into the link with `.incbin` and `.balign 1024` -- 1024
  because that is BTRON_BLOCK_SIZE, so every offset blk_mem_create()'s
  callbacks reach is a whole number of aligned blocks and no volume copy is an
  unaligned MIPS load.  It lands in .data (`.section .data.ps2_sys_vol, "aw"`
  matches the linker script's `*(.data.*)`, ps2.ld:38), which is writable RAM
  on this machine, so mkf, del and ins_rec work and vol_sync() writes back
  into the in-RAM copy; what does NOT survive is a reboot, which re-extracts
  the shipped image.  The ISO needed no new rule: $(PS2_ISO) copies BTRON.ELF,
  so the disc that boots the kernel already carries the volume.  The 1 MiB is
  the tail of .data and .bss starts exactly where the volume ends, so _end
  grew by the volume's own size -- and chapter 4's budget is what makes that a
  fact worth printing rather than a shrug.

  LAW 20e (the mount is the boot's, and the row that describes it is CALLED).
  btron_core_hfds_log() is that function on every target, and on this one it
  was neither called nor true: nothing in the PS2 image reached it --
  btron_kernel_init() (src/cores/core_init.c:21) is its only caller and lives
  in a TU linked here but entered only from src/desktop/main.c, which is in
  COMMON_SRCS and not in PS2_SRCS -- and its single row asserted three record
  names that are in no manifest.  It now mounts the embedded image
  (blk_mem_create -> vol_mount -> g_sys_vol) and prints name, block size,
  total and free blocks, all read back from vol_* accessors rather than
  restated.  The boot calls it as its storage step, before the banner, so
  startx, the SIO shell's CLU commands and the Terminal window all see the
  same mounted volume.  A failed
  mount is a [FS  ] row naming which of the three calls failed, because the
  failure this section exists to prevent is the SILENT one: sc falling back to
  clu_sc()'s stub line and looking like a missing feature.

  Why not the disc: a CD9660 reader is the right long-term answer for content
  a user burns, and not the cheap one.  The PS2's CDVD is an IOP peripheral,
  so an EE-side read goes MODLOAD -> LoadModule("cdvdman") -> SIF RPC -- a
  second processor's module loader and an RPC client, none of it present in a
  port whose rule is LAW 17, and none of it testable against the ELF-only
  launch path the bench runs.  The two routes differ only in where the bytes
  come from: both end at blk_mem_create over a buffer, so a CDVD reader added
  later changes the source of the image and not this section.  (Lane 13 is
  what makes the disc an EE-cheap answer rather than an EE-expensive one.)

  LAW 20f (a cartridge is a body, and ONE function reads bodies).  The four
  MSX payloads and two drawers are typed BIN, which mkbtronfs writes as
  RT_DATABOX and NOT RT_TADDATA: the type field is a reader's instruction, and
  RT_TADDATA tells the next reader that walks the record chain to decode it as
  text.  A ROM is opaque bytes, so it must be typed as opaque bytes -- rd_rec
  hands back either, and nothing complains until a body with a 0x00 in it
  comes back truncated.  The read path's half of the law: vfs_read_blob(path,
  buf, cap, &out_bytes) (src/clu/vfs.c:212) is the ONE function that moves a
  body, vfs_read_file and vfs_copy are both derived from it, and nothing reads
  a cartridge through a text wrapper -- a body whose length equals its buffer
  cannot come through vfs_read_file, which reserves the last byte for the
  terminator, and rd_rec clamps every read to the record's own size.
  src/apps/msx_app.c therefore asks for MSX_ROM_SIZE + 1 bytes: the extra byte
  is what tells a full 32 KiB page, the only thing this core maps, from a
  megaROM that would otherwise be silently cut in half, and it does so without
  a second size query because there is none -- a size comes from rd_dir, not
  from an open file.  The listing carries each record's fid beside its name
  (VfsEntry.fid) and the path handed to the reader is rebuilt through
  vfs_child_path, which re-attaches that fid as the path's `#<fid>` anchor:
  two records with the same name in the same drawer stay two different
  cartridges.

  LAW 20g (where a file lives is named in ONE place, and it is not the
  launcher's header).  MSX_BIOS_DEFAULT_DIR and MSX_ROM_CATALOGUE
  (include/emulators/tronmsx.h) are the only two places in the tree that say
  where C-BIOS and the cartridges are: third_party/openMSX/Contrib/cbios and
  assets/msx on a host, /SYS/cbios and /SYS/msx on a machine.  The bug this
  law closes was the PS2 port's first: the Games band listed both ROMs, Cross
  opened B-MSX, and the window answered "C-BIOS が読み込めません" while the BIOS sat
  in the volume -- because src/emulators/msx/tronmsx.c initialised s_bios_dir
  to the HOST folder, so the core always handed bios_load() a value, and
  bios_load() only consults the default when it is empty.

  LAW 20h (the working volume is WRITABLE, so the LINK re-derives it).  LAW 20d
  makes .data writable RAM because a filesystem writes, and the same freedom
  exists on the host: five suites mount btron_sys.vol itself, not a copy,
  through blk_file_create(path, 0 /* read/write existing */, 1024) -- test_fs.c
  :978 and :1027, test_editor_ui.c:1043, test_chokanji.c:531 and :999, and
  test_b_drivesetup.c, which mounts it as /SYS at :158 -- and two of them then
  CREATE records in it.  MEASURED, one suite at a time against a volume just
  rebuilt from the manifest: `make test-fs` leaves it untouched (0 bytes),
  `make test-drivesetup` costs 90 (its scratch `sys_boot_%d.cfg`, written at
  :959 into the real /SYS rather than into the in-memory one the same function
  allocates at :947), `make test-editor` takes the same reading to 40,832, and
  `make test-chokanji` adds nothing.  The mechanism is not a race and not the
  clock: test_editor_ui.c saves NOTE.md into the mounted real volume, deletes
  it, and calls vol_sync() at :1109-1121, which is the function that flushes
  the in-RAM image back over the host file -- a removed record leaves its body
  in the blocks it owned, which is why the delta is 40 KiB of data and no
  record NAME survives in the file.  So `make test`
  leaves the payload holding two tests' data; that write makes the object's
  prerequisite newer than the
  object, the next `make ps2` .incbin's it, and the shipped image's /SYS
  carries `sys_boot_34813.cfg` and a 40 KiB document body.  Nothing in the
  suite said so, because the two rows that exist to catch a stale payload both
  compare the ELF against the SAME MUTATED FILE: 2026-10-14, the alignment row
  and the bytes-are-what-mkbtronfs-wrote row were both green on a contaminated
  link, while the freshness row added under this law -- which compares the
  image's own blocks against a fresh build instead of against the working file
  -- read those 90 bytes and went red.  That row's own insertion renumbered
  every row after it, which is Appendix B's M5 happening again to this port's
  own gate; the row is named above and below rather than numbered.  The repair
  therefore lives in the link and not in a note: the rule
  for src/drivers/ps2/ps2_sys_vol.ps2.o rebuilds the manifest into
  .build/ps2-vol-fresh.vol first -- reproducible, per LAW 20c -- and replaces
  the working file when the two disagree, printing by how many bytes.  It runs
  BEFORE the compile, so the object always ends newer than its own input and a
  clean tree still has nothing to do: two `make ps2` in a row relink once and
  then idle, which is the control for the guard not being a churn machine.
  The root cause is a shared host test writing into a build artifact, and that
  is the B-System suite's decision rather than this lane's guardrail to spend;
  what this port owns is the weaker but enforceable claim that no image leaves
  the link carrying anything but the manifest.

  The volume's budget, read off the image: a volume that uses 732 blocks is
  not an empty formatted volume -- it holds 33 records and their index -- and
  blk_mem_create was given readonly = 0, so the writes mkf performs go
  somewhere.  292 blocks are left, and the 114,688 bytes of media cost 127 of
  them, so the headroom is about eight more 32 KiB cartridges before the
  link's ASSERT(_end <= 0x01FF0000) starts to be argued with.  THE VOLUME IS
  THE SMALLER OF THIS IMAGE'S TWO BUDGETS, which is the sentence chapter 4's
  arithmetic was written to make possible.  Both figures are the 2026-10-13
  reading: rebuilding the volume on 2026-10-14, the day this file became the
  payload, reports 737 used and 287 free -- five blocks more, and those five
  blocks are this book's own prose, because a size belongs with its date and
  this book now sits inside the number it quotes.  The later rebuilds of the
  same day, after 11.6 and the parked dial were written in, read 756 used and
  268 free -- nineteen blocks, and this book is the only payload file any of
  those rebuilds changed, so the whole rise is prose about the port rather
  than anything the port ships.  The paragraph recording that reading moved it
  a block again, and the one you are reading will move it once more, so do not
  quote this sentence for the current figure: re-run `make btron_sys.vol` and
  read mkbtronfs's own line.  THAT is the finding, not any one number -- the
  volume's smaller budget is spent by WRITING, which is exactly what a future
  cartridge will have to fit under.  Three more readings of the same afternoon,
  for the same reason: 761 and 263 on the rebuild that carried the parked
  dial's rewrite, 763 and 261 once LAW 20h and its GATE row were written in,
  and 765 and 259 with this sentence in it -- eight blocks above the 757 that
  section 11.6's landing reported, every one of them prose.  The ladder is
  still climbing on the same day: the rebuilds around LANE 9b's Price cell read
  774 used and 250 free, and 775 and 249 once the Budget cell's page unit was
  corrected to the allocator's own 8 KB, and 776 and 248 with this chapter's
  fourth stamp incident -- the one measured AFTER a green gate -- written into
  the dial rows below.  Then the census, and it crossed nine blocks at once:
  785 and 239 with LANE 9's six live rows and their verdict in the Price cell,
  the `Gate` premise rewritten around them, Appendix A's row 9b closed as
  REFUTED, and this sentence counting its own file.  And the WRITING this
  paragraph warns about is not only
  the user's: LAW 20h is the reason a block count read off
  `mkbtronfs` and a block count read off `btron_sys.vol` after a `make test`
  are two different numbers, and only one of them describes the volume an
  image ships.

  GATE  ten rows of scripts/test_ps2.sh read this section off the ELF, and
        each was shown to be able to go red:

    row                                   control that turned it red
    ---------------------  ---------------------------------------------
    volume present, 1024-  the same arithmetic at a fake 0x00580801
    aligned, whole blocks  gives %1024 == 1
    the ELF's bytes are    one changed byte at 512 KiB in a scratch copy;
    mkbtronfs's bytes      and the identical dd | cmp against ELF offset 0
    the image's bytes are  BOTH rows above green on a contaminated link:
    a FRESH mkbtronfs      `make test-drivesetup` writes sys_boot_*.cfg
    build, not the         into the working volume, `make ps2` embeds it,
    working file           and the pair agrees with itself (90 bytes)
    the boot calls the     asked of <ps2_gs_init> instead: 0
    mount
    sc's real session init asked of <term_session_init>: 0
    won the link
    B-MSX's real opener    asked of open_gterm_window, which this image
    won the link           really does link WEAK at 220 bytes: fails
    Cross on a Games row   asked of <open_msx_window_with_rom>, which only
    creates task+window    forwards: 0
    the band's rows come   asked of <msx_load>: 0
    from the mounted volume
    a cartridge reaches    asked of <msx_open>: 0 for both readers -- the
    the core through the   scan is not finding one call site twice
    one reader
    the code asks the      the first version scanned the WHOLE image and
    volume, not the        went red on this file's own sentence about the
    checkout, for both     bug -- 2 hits, both prose.  Section scoping is
    drawers                what makes the row read code, not documentation

  The 1 MiB is LOAD cost, not present cost, and the row beneath the mount says
  so:

    `[PS2] whole-canvas paint cost: render=56075 us  swap=0 us  upload=820 us
    total=56.895 ms`.

  The upload is 820 us because the GIF DMA reads the compositor's own
  backbuffer; nothing in the present path walks .data, so a volume in the
  image cannot slow a frame.

  What this does NOT prove, stated in the same voice as the rest: the gate is
  static, reading the ELF rather than the screen.  Two things the screen has
  answered -- sc opening in the Terminal window instead of printing "not
  available yet", and the Games band listing the two cartridges with Cross
  booting them -- were the user's to verify and they hold; they are no longer
  an inference from `msx_init -> 0`.  What has NOT been shown by anything in
  this section is SPEED: B-MSX runs, and it runs too slowly to play well,
  which is a per-frame cost question owned by chapters 10 and 11 and not a
  storage one.  Nothing in this section's machinery is on that path: the
  core's Z80, VDP and PSG loops are integer code that reads no float, and the
  machine's frame is a 256x212 screen integer-scaled into the window
  (MSX_SCR_W/MSX_SCR_H, src/apps/msx_app.c:335), so the bill is emulation
  cycles plus a blit -- a volume read happens once per cartridge, not once per
  frame.  And a mkf that survives until the reboot is likewise unverified,
  which is the same kind of claim: about the machine, not about the image.
  Editing this book is itself a manifest payload, so the volume rebuilds on
  the next make ps2 -- and the second row of the table above is the reason
  that is a feature rather than a hazard.
=====================================================================
PART SEVEN -- BUILDING, VERIFYING, RUNNING
=====================================================================

  A spec that cannot be rebuilt is a story.  This Part states the dials that
  select what gets built, the gate that reads the artifact back, the machines
  it has been run on, and the honest list of what none of them settles.

21. Build dials and their stamps
--------------------------------

  LAW 21 (every dial is a stamp, and every gate is handed the dial).  A build
  flag that selects code must be recorded in a file whose CONTENT names the
  value the objects were built with, and the verification step must receive
  the same value as the build.  Neither half is bookkeeping: each exists
  because an image was once graded for a pipeline it did not contain.

  THE FLAG YOU DID NOT CHANGE IS STILL THE FLAG YOU CHANGED.  make tracks file
  timestamps, not variable values, so a source that has not been edited stays
  older than its object and the object of the PREVIOUS dial value is linked
  into the next image.  Three symptoms of that disease are documented in this
  port, one per dial, and the fix is the same shape every time.  The PS2_MSX
  row below carries that shape PREVENTIVELY -- a dial that moves a source list
  rather than a recompile, so no incident was needed to want it -- and PS2_FP
  and PS2_MSX together with the two measured before them make five stamped
  dials, of which the last is PS2_FP_COUNT:

    PS2_GL      .build/ps2-gl.stamp, compared by content; the three objects
                that read the backend are deleted when it disagrees (chapter
                14).  That parse-time shell runs on EVERY make, including one
                that names no PS2 target: measured 2026-10-13, 36 s after the
                eegs image passed 91/91 the stamp read tinygl again and those
                three objects were gone -- a host-side round (`make -k test`
                or `make arm64-elf`, either is enough) had reset the dial
                while the ELF on disk went on describing eegs, so the next
                plain `make ps2` would have relinked the other pipeline under
                a book quoting these rows.  The third time a stamp in this
                port has had to explain itself, and the reason the gate below
                is handed its dial rather than run bare.  It fired a fourth
                time on 2026-10-14, and this time AFTER a green gate: the eegs
                image had passed 93/93, then the lane's own `make arm64-elf`
                reset the stamp and deleted those three objects, so the image
                and the object set disagreed while both looked current by
                mtime.  Nothing in the artifact shows that -- only the tree --
                which is why a chain's last three commands are now the volume
                rebuild, the link and the image gate, with every other host
                round ahead of them.  The volume step is itself such a round,
                and the same pass measured it: `make btron_sys.vol` names no
                PS2 target, so it writes the default dial, and it is the link
                immediately after it that puts `eegs` back on the bus.  The
                reset does not even need a real round.  Measured 2026-10-14:
                `make -n btron_sys.vol`, a dry run that executes no recipe,
                printed `[PS2] PS2_GL is now tinygl` and wrote the stamp
                anyway -- the comparison is a parse-time shell, not a recipe.
                So `make -n` is not a read-only probe of this tree, and a bare
                one is followed by the same restoration round as a bare
                `make`.  The row now has a teeth-mark the book did not have
                before: the image gate's `GLDEF_RET <= 3` assertion passes on a
                tinygl image, because 0 is a legal answer, so the gate could
                grade the wrong pipeline while the book quoted eegs rows.  It
                therefore reads the constant back against the dial the
                invocation named -- `make test-ps2 PS2_GL=eegs` wants
                `gl_backend_default()` to return 3 -- and the Makefile hands the
                script PS2_GL, PS2_FP, PS2_FP_COUNT and PS2_MSX so the
                comparison has an input.  A gate run bare still grades the
                default; what it can no longer do is grade it silently.
    PS2_FP      .build/ps2-fp.stamp, and PS2_FP_OBJS =
                src/drivers/ps2/ps2_builtins.ps2.o
                src/gl/tinygl/vertex_stage.ps2.o src/apps/xmb_wave.ps2.o --
                the only three units built with -mhard-float plus
                PS2_FP_CSR_COST, keyed by one substitution (PS2_CC := $(subst
                -msoft-float,-mhard-float,$(PS2_CC))) so the law has one home.
                This dial is a LEVER, not a flag: it is shared with
                ps2_builtins.c, so PS2_FP=soft withdraws COP1 from the whole
                soft-float runtime, which measured 500,386 us a frame rather
                than the ~74 ms the absence of row 7 predicted.  Stamp note
                worth knowing: $(shell ...) runs while make PARSES, so even
                `make -n PS2_FP=hw` deletes those three objects.  A dry run of
                this target is not free, and this is the second time in this
                port a stamp has had to explain itself.
    PS2_MSX     .build/ps2-msx.stamp, and a SOURCE LIST as well as a flag:
                PS2_MSX_SRCS is empty at 0 and holds MSX_SRCS plus
                src/apps/msx_app.c at 1, while core_ps2.c compiles its [MSX]
                bench rows under -DBTRON_PS2_MSX=1.  PARKED at 0 since
                2026-10-14, and the REASON moved the same day, which is the
                part worth keeping.  It opened as a link rescue: the tree
                that failed `make ps2` read slots.c at 5,496,436 B of .bss
                and msx_app.c at 4,323,888, with every *.ps2.o summing
                35,809,162 B against ps2.ld's ASSERT at 0x01FF0000 =
                33,488,896 B -- 2,320,266 over.  That reading carried a second
                claim, and it was wrong: the PS2 heap pool is 11 MiB
                (`PS2_HEAP_SIZE`, src/cores/core_ps2.c:112), it is not
                MSX-gated, and it is in both builds -- so pool trimming was
                never the lever here, whatever the gap had been.  The control
                that tests a park is the un-park, and the one run at 02:24
                fails to fail: `make ps2 PS2_MSX=1 PS2_GL=eegs PS2_FP=hw`
                LINKS, at ELF 6,018,220 bytes and _end = 0x01F89A00.  Today's
                objects read slots.c 2,350,712, bios.c 442,368, vdp.c 393,998,
                msx_app.c 129,580 -- 3,316,862 B summed over the fifteen units
                the dial adds -- while the two LINKED images differ by
                3,316,864 B of .bss and 3,402,880 B of _end.  Do not read
                those as one decomposition: the un-park also compiles
                core_ps2.c's 1,048,577 B bench ROM buffer (`rom`, `nm -S`),
                and the object sum plus that exceeds the image delta by almost
                exactly that buffer, so the split between the two is a
                link-time matter and has not been measured here (R3).  What IS
                measured is the pair: tronMSX inside costs 3,402,880 B of
                _end and leaves 419,328 B of headroom rather than nothing.
                The two readings of the same two filenames are 3.1 MiB and
                4.2 MiB apart, which are not accidental widths; but only a
                slots.c other than today's can yield 5,496,436, and this
                tree's slots.c and msx_app.c were rewritten at 01:01 today and
                committed by the B-MSX lane as 9bbec9d and 6900bf8, with both
                objects rebuilt at 01:01:54.  That attribution is INFERRED
                from mtimes, not measured -- the object that carried the old
                figure is gone.
                So the park is no longer what makes the image link.  It stays
                for two other reasons: 419,328 B is 410 KiB, one cartridge,
                where the parked image has 3,822,208 -- 9.1x the ceiling --
                and the runtime answer is still missing (M17: tronMSX asks
                the HOST folder for C-BIOS, so the Games band opens a window
                saying it cannot find a BIOS the volume does hold).  A source
                list this tight, and a feature that ships broken, is enough
                to keep a dial at 0 without a link error to justify it.  The
                stamp exists because a source-list change is not a
                recompile: without it make would link yesterday's [MSX] rows
                into today's parked image, and the pair above -- two readings
                three megabytes apart from files of the same name -- is this
                day's own evidence for that sentence.  At 0 the five B-MSX
                image rows print [SKIP] by name, and src/apps/xmb.c's weak
                open_msx_window*() stubs make the Games band a menu row that
                does nothing rather than a link error.
    PS2_FP_COUNT .build/ps2-fpc.stamp, and PS2_FPC_OBJS =
                src/drivers/ps2/ps2_builtins.ps2.o src/apps/xmb.ps2.o
                src/cores/core_ps2.ps2.o src/cores/core_ps2.pbench.o -- the
